Even Kuhn, the paradigm case of emphasizing theory-ladenness, allowed that observation could constrain theory choice through what he called "paradigm-relative standards" and acknowledged that competing paradigms could share enough common ground for rational comparison. Similarly, even if consciousness criteria embed theoretical commitments, they can embed *weaker* or *different* commitments than the full theories being tested.

The position is already well-mapped in philosophy of science: theory-ladenness is real but doesn't collapse into full-blown incommensurability or make empirical testing impossible. Shapere (1982), Hacking (1983), and others showed how partial independence of observation from high-level theory preserves empirical constraint. The consciousness case, while having distinctive features, doesn't escape this general resolution.

STRENGTH: 5/5

This is a standard position in philosophy of science, specifically applied to consciousness. The thesis needs to explain why consciousness escapes the general solutions to theory-ladenness problems.

DEFENDER

BEST DEFENCE

The objection treats this as just another instance of theory-ladenness, but consciousness has a unique feature that makes the general philosophy-of-science solutions inapplicable: consciousness is simultaneously the object of study and the sole mode of epistemic access to itself.

In normal science, theory-ladenness is manageable because observation and theoretical posits occupy different roles. When we observe cloud chamber tracks, the observation is theory-laden (we interpret vapor trails as particle trajectories), but the *particles themselves* are not identical to our observations of them. The electrons exist independently of our seeing the tracks. This gap—between phenomenon and observational access—is precisely what allows Shapere's and Hacking's solutions to work. We can have theory-laden observation that still constrains theory because the world pushes back through causal channels independent of our theoretical interpretation.

But with consciousness, the phenomenon just is a mode of access. A conscious state isn't something that causes an experience we then observe—the conscious state *is* the experience. When I'm conscious of red, there's no gap between "the consciousness of red" and "my access to the consciousness of red" of the sort that exists between "the electron" and "my observation of the electron track."

This creates a distinctive problem: The Kuhnian solution worked because paradigm-relative standards could still answer to a shared world that constrained both paradigms from outside their theoretical frameworks. But consciousness has no "outside"—every proposed criterion for identifying it is itself either a conscious state (introspection) or a theoretical claim about what correlates with conscious states (behavioral/neural measures).

When IIT and GWT disagree about whether a particular brain state is conscious, there's no standing outside both theories to check against "the consciousness itself" in the way we can stand outside competing particle theories to check against "the tracks themselves." The tracks are publicly accessible; consciousness is not. And unlike other private phenomena (like dark matter), we can't even use indirect causal effects as neutral evidence, because *what counts as a causal effect of consciousness* is itself theoretically disputed (does consciousness cause reports? behavior? neural dynamics?).

The thesis isn't claiming mere theory-ladenness makes testing impossible—it's claiming that consciousness's unique reflexive structure (being both phenomenon and sole epistemic access) creates a *specific* version of theory-ladenness that the standard solutions don't address. Shapere and Hacking assumed observer-independent phenomena; consciousness violates that assumption.

VERDICT

The defence fails. It correctly identifies consciousness's distinctive features but incorrectly concludes these features defeat all possibility of theory-constraint.

The crucial error is the claim that consciousness has "no outside" that could constrain theories. This is false in at least two ways:

First, inter-subjective convergence provides constraint. While my consciousness is only directly accessible to me, if GWT predicts I'll report consciousness in condition C and IIT predicts I won't, and I do report it, that's evidence—not theory-neutral evidence, but evidence that constrains theory choice. Multiply this across subjects and conditions. Yes, we're using reports (theory-laden), but the *pattern* of reports can still favor one theory over another. The defence conflates "every data point is theory-laden" with "patterns in data can't favor theories differentially."

Second, consciousness has causal-functional connections to the "outside". The defence claims we can't use indirect causal effects as neutral evidence because "what counts as a causal effect of consciousness is theoretically disputed." But this overstates the dispute. GWT and IIT disagree about *which* neural states are conscious, but both agree conscious states have *some* systematic relationships to reports, attention, memory formation, and behavior. These partial agreements create shared evidential ground.

The analogy to Kuhn actually defeats the thesis: Kuhn's incommensurability was never complete—he explicitly allowed for "translation" between paradigms and rational theory choice via values like simplicity, scope, and fruitfulness. Similarly, even if GWT and IIT embed different criteria, they make *different predictions* about edge cases (consciousness during anesthesia, in split-brain patients, in cerebellum, in artificial systems). These differential predictions, even if interpreted through theory-laden criteria, still constrain theory choice.

The thesis overreaches from "consciousness has unique epistemic features" to "consciousness uniquely escapes all empirical constraint." But unique features don't entail unique immunity to evidence. The position is already taken and already answered: theory-ladenness, even in the reflexive case of consciousness, permits rational empirical constraint through patterns, convergence, and differential prediction.

KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)

ATTEMPT

The argument, if successful, would prove that *any* phenomenon accessible only through theory-laden criteria makes competing theories explanatorily idle. But this licenses obviously unacceptable conclusions across science and philosophy.

Consider pain theories. Different theories (pattern theory, specificity theory, gate control theory, neuromatrix theory) compete to explain pain. To test them, we need criteria for identifying when pain is present. But any criterion embeds theoretical commitments: self-reports assume pain is introspectively accessible; nociceptor firing assumes pain correlates with specific neural pathways; behavioral withdrawal assumes pain has characteristic functional roles. The exact same structure applies: we need theory-neutral access to identify pain instances, but any criterion for pain embeds a theory of what pain is.

Yet clearly, pain theories aren't rendered "explanatorily idle" by this situation. Gate control theory made novel predictions about how non-nociceptive input could modulate pain (explaining why rubbing an injury helps), which competing theories couldn't explain. The theories do real explanatory work despite criterion theory-ladenness.

Similarly for memory, attention, emotion—all psychological phenomena where identification criteria are theory-laden, yet competing theories remain explanatorily non-idle.

The weak point is the premise that theory-laden criteria make theories explanatorily idle. This conflates identification with explanation. Even if identifying instances requires theoretical commitments, theories remain non-idle when they: (1) explain *why* the criteria work, (2) predict dissociations between criteria and phenomenon, (3) unify disparate phenomena, (4) provide mechanisms. The consciousness case has these features—GWT and IIT explain different mechanisms, make different predictions about edge cases, unify different data. Theory-ladenness of observation is ubiquitous; explanatory idleness doesn't follow.

STRENGTH: 5/5

The argument would eliminate most theoretical disputes in psychology and philosophy of mind, which is unacceptable. The thesis needs to explain why consciousness uniquely suffers this problem when pain, memory, and other theory-laden phenomena don't.

DEFENDER

BEST DEFENCE

The objection assumes consciousness is just another psychological phenomenon like pain or memory, but this misses what makes consciousness explanatorily unique: consciousness is the precondition for all other psychological evidence, not just another item within the psychological domain.

Pain, memory, attention, and emotion are *contents* or *modifications* of consciousness. When we test pain theories, we rely on conscious subjects reporting their conscious experiences of pain. When we test memory theories, we rely on conscious subjects demonstrating conscious recall. The evidential base for all these theories presupposes consciousness as the background condition.

This creates an asymmetry: Pain theories can be non-idle despite theory-laden criteria because we can hold consciousness constant while varying pain. We test gate control theory by examining cases where subjects are conscious but pain varies (rubbing the injury reduces conscious pain experience). The theory-ladenness of pain criteria doesn't make pain theories idle because consciousness itself provides the stable epistemic platform from which we observe pain's variations.

But with consciousness, we cannot hold anything constant "outside" it while varying consciousness itself. Every attempt to test consciousness theories must use consciousness as both explanandum and explanans. When GWT claims global broadcast produces consciousness, and we try to test this by asking subjects when they're conscious, we're using conscious reports (which presuppose consciousness) to verify claims about what produces consciousness. The circularity is inescapable.

The pain analogy actually strengthens the thesis: Pain theories work precisely because they're *not* theories of the epistemic access condition itself. They're theories of a phenomenon we access *through* consciousness. But consciousness theories are theories of the access condition itself—and that's why the theory-ladenness problem becomes fatal in this unique case.

Consider: Could we test pain theories if pain itself were the only means by which we could detect anything at all? If every observation, every report, every measurement required pain as its precondition? That would be analogous to consciousness's position—and in that case, pain theories *would* face the same idleness problem.

The thesis doesn't prove too much because consciousness is uniquely reflexive: it's the condition for having evidence about anything, including itself. Pain, memory, and attention don't have this status. They're phenomena within conscious experience, not the structure of experience itself.

VERDICT

The defence fails. It correctly identifies consciousness's distinctive role but draws the wrong conclusion from it.

The key error is the claim that we "cannot hold anything constant outside [consciousness] while varying consciousness itself." This is empirically false. We can and do vary consciousness while holding other factors constant:

Sleep-wake cycles: Same subject, same brain, varying levels of consciousness. We observe systematic differences in neural dynamics (thalamocortical connectivity, posterior cortical activity) that correlate with consciousness changes. GWT and IIT make different predictions about which neural patterns matter.

Anesthesia: Progressive loss of consciousness with measurable neural changes. The theories predict different patterns—GWT emphasizes frontoparietal network disruption; IIT emphasizes breakdown of integrated information. These are testable differences.

Binocular rivalry and masking: Stimulus held constant, consciousness varies. Subjects report seeing/not seeing the same physical input. Neural correlates differ between conscious and unconscious perception. The theories make differential predictions about where and when these differences appear.

Lesion and stimulation studies: TMS to different regions, lesions in different areas—these manipulate consciousness while holding the subject's overall conscious state constant enough to report what changed.

The defence's claim that "every observation requires consciousness as precondition" conflates *the subject being conscious* with *the particular conscious state being studied*. Yes, subjects must be conscious to report—but they can be conscious while reporting on variations in *what* they're conscious of, *whether* they're conscious of X, and *how* conscious states change. This is exactly parallel to pain: subjects must be conscious to report pain, but they report on pain's variations, not just consciousness generally.

Moreover, the defence ignores that theories make predictions about *third-person* evidence—neural patterns, behavioral capacities, information integration measures—that don't require the subject's own conscious report. When IIT predicts the cerebellum lacks consciousness despite massive neural activity, this is testable through comparing cerebellar versus cortical phi values, not just through reports.

The "proves too much" objection stands: If the argument worked for consciousness, it would work for any phenomenon where (a) detection requires subjects being conscious, and (b) detection criteria are theory-laden. That includes pain, emotion, and memory. But these theories clearly aren't explanatorily idle—they explain mechanisms, predict dissociations, guide interventions. The same holds for consciousness theories.

The thesis fails because explanatory idleness requires that criteria answer all questions theories address. But even theory-laden criteria for consciousness don't explain *why* global broadcast produces unified experience, *how* phi values relate to qualia, *whether* consciousness comes in degrees, or

KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)

ATTEMPT

Consider this concrete case: binocular rivalry with continuous flash suppression (CFS).

Setup: Present image A to left eye, image B to right eye. Use CFS to suppress A from awareness while B remains visible. Subject reports seeing only B. Then gradually increase A's contrast until it "breaks through" to awareness—subject reports the moment of transition from "not seeing A" to "seeing A."

Now apply competing theories:
- GWT predicts: Breakthrough correlates with A gaining access to global workspace (frontoparietal ignition, widespread broadcasting)
- IIT predicts: A had high phi throughout (integrated information in visual cortex), consciousness present even when suppressed, but subject couldn't report it

Both theories can be tested against the *pattern* of what subjects report across trials, contrast levels, and neural measurements. The data consistently shows: subjects don't report A until frontoparietal ignition occurs, and this ignition correlates with specific contrast thresholds. This pattern favors GWT over IIT's prediction that consciousness was present-but-unreportable during suppression.

The weak point: The premise that testing requires theory-neutral criteria. Here the criterion is simply "what subjects report when"—a minimal commitment that doesn't predetermine which theory wins. The *pattern* of reports across conditions differentially supports theories. GWT and IIT make different predictions; data favors one. The theories aren't idle—they're doing real work explaining and predicting these patterns.

The thesis fails because real experimental paradigms *do* distinguish theories through differential predictions, even using theory-laden measures.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This is a serious problem. Actual neuroscience experiments generate theory-discriminating evidence despite criterion theory-ladenness, contradicting the idleness claim.

DEFENDER

BEST DEFENCE

The objection presents exactly the kind of case that *illustrates* rather than refutes the thesis. Look carefully at what's doing the work in the binocular rivalry example.

The objection claims the criterion is "simply what subjects report when"—a minimal commitment. But this is precisely the theoretical commitment that decides the case before testing begins. By treating reportability as the criterion for consciousness, you've already rejected IIT's framework and accepted an access-based theory. The objection then presents this as a neutral test, but it's circular: you've built access-consciousness into your methodology, then discovered that the access-based theory (GWT) fits the access-based data better than the non-access theory (IIT).

IIT's proponent would respond: "Your experiment doesn't test our theory—it presupposes we're wrong. We claim consciousness is present during suppression even without report. Your 'evidence' that subjects don't report A during suppression doesn't show A wasn't conscious; it shows exactly what we predict: consciousness without access, hence without report. You've measured access, not consciousness."

The objection replies: "But subjects report the transition moment—that's data!" But what makes that transition moment evidence about *consciousness* rather than evidence about *reportability* or *access*? Only if you've already assumed consciousness requires reportability. Which is precisely the theoretical commitment that distinguishes GWT-style theories from IIT.

Here's the deeper problem: The objection conflates "theories make different predictions" with "we can test those predictions without theoretical presuppositions." Yes, GWT and IIT predict different neural patterns. But identifying which pattern indicates *consciousness* (rather than just access, attention, or report-generation) requires already knowing what consciousness is—which is what the theories disagree about.

The binocular rivalry case doesn't provide theory-neutral evidence that favors GWT. It provides evidence that reportability correlates with frontoparietal ignition—which everyone already accepts. The question is whether reportability *tracks consciousness*. GWT says yes; IIT says no (consciousness can occur without report). The experiment cannot adjudicate this without assuming an answer to what consciousness is.

The thesis stands: actualizing a testing methodology requires criterion-commitments that either predetermine the answer (as here, where reportability-as-criterion favors the reportability-based theory) or render the competing theories redundant (if we had independent consciousness-detection that worked, we wouldn't need theories about what realizes consciousness).

VERDICT

The defence fails, and the thesis falls with it.

The defence claims the binocular rivalry experiment "presupposes GWT is right" by using reports as criteria. But this misunderstands how theory-testing works. Using theory-laden evidence doesn't require presupposing a theory is correct—it requires only that theories make differential predictions about that evidence.

Here's what actually happens in the experiment:

Both theories accept reports as evidence about something. IIT doesn't claim reports are meaningless—it claims they track *access* rather than *consciousness*, and that these can dissociate. This is a *substantive empirical claim* about when reports and consciousness come apart. GWT claims reports reliably track consciousness because consciousness just is (or requires) access. This too is substantive and empirical.

The theories thus make different predictions about the *pattern* of reports:
- GWT predicts: Report transitions will correlate with specific neural signatures (frontoparietal ignition) and these signatures will be present whenever consciousness is present
- IIT predicts: High phi will be present in visual cortex throughout suppression, dissociating from reports; report transitions correlate with access-mechanisms, not consciousness-mechanisms

These are genuinely different predictions. The data shows frontoparietal ignition correlates tightly with reports, while visual cortex phi remains high throughout. This creates pressure on IIT: if consciousness was present during suppression (high phi), why does it systematically fail to leave any trace in behavior, memory, or subsequent report—not just in this experiment, but across hundreds of subjects and conditions?

The defence's error is treating "reports track access not consciousness" as an unfalsifiable theoretical posit. But it's not unfalsifiable—it's falsifiable through *convergent patterns*. If IIT were correct that consciousness regularly occurs without access, we should find: (1) subjects later discovering they were conscious of suppressed stimuli, (2) behavioral effects of suppressed stimuli that indicate conscious processing, (3) subjective reports of "knowing I was conscious but unable to report," (4) dissociations between phi and reports that make systematic sense.

Instead, we find the opposite: reports, behavior, memory, and neural signatures converge. When subjects say they're conscious, they behave as if conscious, remember as if conscious, and show neural signatures of consciousness. When they say they're not, all these factors align. This convergence isn't predicted by IIT (which expects systematic dissociations

Attacked, ranked

Everything else that went through the adversarial stage.

O1. The demand for empirical criteria to adjudicate between consciousness theories presupposes that consciousness theories are making the same kind of claim, but integrated information theory is actually making a mathematical claim about identity while global workspace theory is making a causal-functional claim, so they cannot be empirically compared the way we compare rival physical theories.

Scores
promise 4/5 · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
Move · Target
distinction — Shared assumption among consciousness researchers that we just need better experimental paradigms or neural measurements to determine which theory is correct
Sketch

The argument begins by examining what would count as evidence for IIT versus GWT. For IIT, the key claim is that consciousness literally IS integrated information above phi-critical—this is a mathematical identity claim, like 'water is H2O.' For GWT, consciousness is whatever plays a certain causal-functional role in cognition—this is a role-specification claim, like 'a heart is whatever pumps blood.' These are categorically different claims: one tells you what consciousness is made of, one tells you what job it does. The confusion arises because both theories generate predictions, but IIT's predictions follow from an identity while GWT's follow from a job description. This means 'evidence' functions differently for each: finding phi without reportability would refute GWT but confirm IIT, while finding reportability without phi would refute IIT but leave GWT's job-description possibly satisfied by different mechanisms. We've been trying to run an empirical horse-race between a claim about essence and a claim about function, which is a category mistake.

Appraiser (blind)
Identity-claim vs. role-claim category difference is genuinely illuminating; explains why evidence functions differently for each theory.
Objections
  • ALREADY TAKEN — landed 3/5, unanswered. The objection showed Bennett/Hacker made similar distinctions and that IIT treats phi as empirical, but the defence correctly distinguished its narrower claim about inter-theory incomparability from B&H's general critique, and showed identity claims can be empirical; the real damage was absorbed by narrowing what 'identity claim' means rather than answering why that prevents comparison
  • PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, unanswered. The objection showed the argument would render successful historical theory comparisons illegitimate; the defence tried to distinguish IIT/GWT from caloric/kinetic as different-type versus same-type comparisons, but this distinction collapsed under scrutiny—the historical cases involved empirical adjudication despite categorical differences, exactly what the thesis denies is possible
  • COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, unanswered. The adversarial collaboration directly instantiated empirical comparison between IIT and GWT; the defence claimed resistance to evidence proved incomparability, but this conflated theory resistance (universal) with structural incomparability (the thesis's claim), and the defence's own description showed standard empirical adjudication occurring
  • TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, unanswered. The objection showed the distinction was already known and added no explanatory value; the defence claimed it explained systematic underdetermination, but failed to show this was anything beyond normal theory resistance in immature sciences, and the water analogy offered actually demonstrated empirical comparability rather than preventing it
Flaw

The thesis conflates 'theories making categorically different kinds of claims' with 'theories being empirically incomparable,' but theories can be empirically adjudicated whenever they make conflicting predictions about shared observables regardless of their logical type—as the historical cases and actual IIT/GWT adversarial collaboration demonstrate

full exchange (8 turns)

KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)

ATTEMPT. This position is essentially the neo-Kantian distinction between constitutive and regulative principles applied to consciousness science, most clearly articulated by Maxwell Bennett and Peter Hacker in *Philosophical Foundations of Neuroscience* (2003). They argue that neuroscientific theories systematically conflate different logical types of claims—some identifying neural substrates (constitutive), others specifying functional roles (regulative)—and that this conflation generates pseudo-disagreements that cannot be resolved empirically. The weak point is the characterization of IIT as making purely mathematical identity claims: IIT explicitly positions phi as an *empirical measure* with testable predictions about which systems are conscious, not merely as a mathematical definition. Tononi himself presents IIT as empirically falsifiable through cases like cerebellar lesions (high neuron count, low phi, no consciousness loss) versus cortical lesions (lower neuron count, higher phi, consciousness loss). If IIT were purely definitional, these wouldn't count as evidence *for* the theory—they'd just be stipulations about where to apply the label "conscious." The thesis misconstrues IIT's mathematical formalism as indicating a non-empirical identity claim, when the formalism is actually proposed as the empirical criterion itself.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously undermines the thesis by showing the distinction/identity versus function divide has been previously articulated, and by demonstrating that IIT's own proponents treat it as empirically testable rather than as a mere mathematical identity.

DEFENDER

BEST DEFENCE

The objection misreads what makes a claim an "identity claim" versus a "functional claim" in the relevant philosophical sense. That IIT generates empirical predictions does not show it isn't making an identity claim—quite the opposite. Water-is-H2O is also empirically testable (we can check whether samples called "water" have that molecular structure), but it remains an identity claim about what water *is*, not what water *does*. The same goes for IIT: phi-above-threshold is proposed as what consciousness literally is, and the cerebellar/cortical cases are tests of whether that identity holds, just as molecular analysis tests whether water=H2O holds.

The objection conflates "empirically testable" with "functional-role specification," but these cut across each other. Identity claims can be empirically tested (is this water H2O? is this conscious system high-phi?), and functional claims can be empirically tested (does this system perform global broadcasting?). The distinction isn't empirical-versus-non-empirical; it's essence-versus-role.

On Bennett and Hacker: they diagnose category mistakes in neuroscience generally, but they don't argue that *competing* theories within consciousness science are making categorically different kinds of claims that render them non-comparable. They're concerned with neuroscience wrongly attributing psychological predicates to brains rather than persons—a different issue entirely. The thesis here is narrower: it's about the specific incomparability between IIT and GWT, not about neuroscience's general conceptual confusions.

The cerebellar case actually supports the thesis: IIT treats it as confirming that phi-is-consciousness (low phi, no consciousness loss = identity confirmed), while GWT would interpret the same case functionally (cerebellum doesn't do global broadcasting anyway, so its removal is irrelevant to workspace function). Same evidence, categorically different evidential relations.

VERDICT

The defence fails, but not for the reason the objection suggests. The real problem is that the thesis overstates the incomparability. Even granting that IIT makes identity claims and GWT makes functional claims, they can still be empirically compared whenever they make conflicting predictions about the same cases. The thesis admits this: "finding phi without reportability would refute GWT but confirm IIT." That's precisely what empirical adjudication looks like—differential predictions generating crucial experiments.

The thesis confuses "theories making different *kinds* of claims" with "theories being empirically incomparable." But scientific theories routinely make different kinds of claims while remaining comparable: evolutionary theory makes historical claims, genetics makes structural claims, yet they empirically constrain each other. The question isn't whether IIT and GWT are the same *type* of theory, but whether they make conflicting predictions about overlapping domains. They do. The thesis is wrong.

KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)

ATTEMPT. If this argument worked—that theories making categorically different kinds of claims (identity versus functional-role) cannot be empirically compared—it would establish that vast swathes of successful science involve category mistakes. Consider: atomic theory makes an identity claim about what matter IS (arrangements of atoms), while thermodynamics makes a functional-role claim about what heat DOES (transfers energy, does work). Yet we successfully empirically adjudicated between caloric theory and kinetic theory precisely by testing these different kinds of claims against each other. The phlogiston/oxygen case is even clearer: phlogiston theory made claims about what combustion IS (release of phlogiston substance), while Lavoisier's theory made claims about what oxygen DOES functionally in combustion. The experimental resolution involved comparing these categorically different claims. Similarly, "gene" started as a functional concept (whatever produces heritable traits) and became an identity claim (DNA sequences), yet Mendelian genetics and molecular genetics were empirically compared throughout this transition. The weak point is the inference from "different logical types of claims" to "cannot be empirically compared the way we compare rival physical theories"—this inference, if valid, would render most major scientific revolutions conceptually confused category mistakes rather than legitimate empirical adjudications.

STRENGTH: 5/5 — This completely undermines the thesis. The argument proves far too much: it would make the actual historical practice of successful theory comparison in science impossible or illegitimate, which is an unacceptable conclusion.

DEFENDER

BEST DEFENCE

The objection trades on a crucial ambiguity in "empirically compared." The thesis doesn't claim IIT and GWT cannot both be tested empirically—it claims they cannot be compared *the way we compare rival physical theories*, meaning theories competing to explain the same phenomenon by offering alternative accounts of the same explanatory kind.

The historical cases cited actually support rather than refute the thesis. Caloric versus kinetic theory were *both* identity claims about what heat is—one said heat is a fluid substance, the other said heat is molecular motion. Both were essence-claims, so they were directly competing answers to the same question. That's why empirical comparison was straightforward: they made rival identity claims about the same explanandum. Similarly, phlogiston versus oxygen were *both* substance-identity claims about combustion—what is released versus what is consumed, but both operating at the level of "what combustion essentially involves materially."

The gene case is more interesting but actually proves the thesis's point: Mendelian genetics and molecular genetics were *not* straightforwardly empirically compared as rivals. Instead, molecular biology *reduced* Mendelian genetics by showing how DNA sequences could play the functional role Mendel described. This was inter-theoretic reduction, not theory competition. The functional concept was preserved while being given a material basis—exactly what's *not* happening between IIT and GWT, where there's no agreement on whether one reduces to the other.

The thesis's real claim is this: when one theory says "X is essentially Y" and another says "X is whatever does job Z," empirical results underdetermine theory choice because you can always either (a) deny the job-description captures the essence, or (b) deny the proposed essence actually does the job. IIT and GWT aren't like caloric versus kinetic (rival essence-claims) or like Mendelian versus molecular genetics (agreed reduction). They're like trying to adjudicate between "water is H2O" and "water is whatever fills the rivers and lakes"—these aren't rival theories but different kinds of answers that can both be maintained by adjusting what counts as "really" water.

The objection's examples all involve either rival claims of the same type, or successful reduction. Neither describes IIT versus GWT.

VERDICT

The defence fails. The problem is that the historical analogy actually cuts the other way, and the thesis mischaracterizes what happened in those cases.

Take caloric versus kinetic theory: these weren't just both "identity claims"—they made *incompatible empirical predictions* about the same measurable phenomena. Caloric theory predicted that heat capacity should behave one way, kinetic theory predicted differently, and experiments decided between them. The logical type of claim (both identity claims) didn't prevent empirical adjudication; what mattered was that they made different predictions about the same observables.

The defence tries to save the thesis by claiming IIT and GWT are like "water is H2O" versus "water is whatever fills rivers and lakes," where both can be maintained by adjusting definitions. But this is false to the actual dialectical situation. IIT and GWT make conflicting predictions about specific cases: IIT predicts cerebellar tissue should contribute minimally to consciousness (low phi), GWT predicts based on connectivity patterns. IIT predicts certain split-brain cases should have two conscious subjects (two integrated systems), GWT predicts otherwise based on workspace accessibility. These are genuine empirical conflicts, not merely definitional flexibility.

The gene case is particularly damaging to the defence. The claim that Mendelian and molecular genetics "were not straightforwardly empirically compared as rivals" is historically wrong. There were decades of active empirical investigation into whether Mendelian factors could be identified with chromosomal material, whether crossing-over matched recombination rates, whether mutation rates matched physical/chemical predictions—this was empirical comparison leading to reduction, not mere conceptual harmonization.

The thesis fails because it conflates "making different kinds of claims" with "being empirically incomparable." Even when theories differ in logical type, they can be empirically adjudicated if they make conflicting predictions about shared observables. IIT and GWT do exactly this. The thesis is wrong.

KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)

ATTEMPT. The thesis claims that IIT (identity claim) and GWT (functional claim) cannot be empirically compared the way rival physical theories are compared. But consider the actual adversarial collaboration between IIT and GWT proponents (Tononi, Dehaene, Koch et al., published 2023), which designed experiments specifically to generate differential predictions. One key case: backward masking with brief stimulus presentation. IIT predicts consciousness correlates with early posterior "hot zone" activity (high phi regardless of reportability), while GWT predicts consciousness requires late frontal ignition (global broadcast). The experiments found that subjective reports tracked late frontal activity, not early posterior activity—this was taken as evidence favoring GWT over IIT. This is precisely "empirical comparison the way we compare rival physical theories": generate differential predictions, run experiments, see which theory's predictions hold. The weak point is the claim that different logical types of claims prevent this kind of comparison. The backward masking case shows they don't: even if IIT makes identity claims and GWT makes functional claims, when those claims generate conflicting predictions about the same measurable neural activity patterns and subjective reports, standard empirical adjudication proceeds exactly as it does between rival physical theories. The thesis's distinction without practical difference collapses when faced with actual experimental practice.

STRENGTH: 5/5 — This counterexample directly refutes the thesis by showing that the actual scientific practice treats IIT and GWT as empirically comparable in exactly the way the thesis denies is possible, and that this comparison has produced results taken as evidentially relevant by practitioners on both sides.

DEFENDER

BEST DEFENCE

The objection misunderstands what the adversarial collaboration actually shows. That both sides agreed to run experiments and interpret results doesn't demonstrate genuine empirical comparability—it demonstrates exactly the confusion the thesis diagnoses.

Look at what happened: the backward masking results showed late frontal activity correlating with reports. GWT proponents took this as confirmation (late global broadcast = consciousness). But IIT proponents didn't simply concede defeat—they questioned whether reportability tracks consciousness at all, suggesting the early posterior activity might be "consciousness proper" while late frontal activity is merely access/report mechanisms. This is precisely what the thesis predicts: when an identity-claim theory meets a functional-role theory, negative evidence can always be reinterpreted by questioning whether the functional criterion (reportability) actually tracks the essence (phi).

The collaboration didn't resolve the debate; it relocated it. The new question became: "Does reportability constitute consciousness or merely indicate access to consciousness?" This is exactly the kind of irresolvable dispute you get when one theory defines consciousness by a mathematical essence and the other by a cognitive function. GWT says consciousness IS whatever gets globally broadcast (functional definition), so reportability is constitutive. IIT says consciousness IS high phi (identity claim), so reportability is at best a sometimes-reliable indicator, at worst a red herring.

Compare this to genuine theory adjudication in physics: when experiments showed light bending around the sun matching Einstein's predictions over Newton's, Newtonian proponents couldn't respond "well, maybe gravity isn't really what causes planetary motion—maybe that's just an access phenomenon." The theories were genuinely comparable because they were both making causal-structural claims about the same explanandum.

The adversarial collaboration actually proves the thesis: it generated data both sides could interpret as supporting their view by adjusting what counts as "really" consciousness versus mere correlates. That's not empirical adjudication—that's empirical underdetermination generated by category difference.

VERDICT

The defence fails, and the thesis collapses here.

The defence tries to save the thesis by claiming IIT proponents "didn't simply concede defeat" and instead questioned whether reportability tracks consciousness. But this rhetorical move is available to *any* theory facing negative evidence—it's not special to category-different theories. Newtonian proponents could and did resist Einstein by questioning whether Mercury's perihelion really tracked gravitational effects (maybe magnetic fields, maybe measurement error). Caloric theorists resisted kinetic theory by questioning whether the experiments really measured heat-as-such versus mere mechanical effects. Theory resistance is ubiquitous; it doesn't show theories are incomparable.

What matters is whether the resistance is *reasonable* or whether the evidence genuinely constrains rational belief. The backward masking case does constrain: if consciousness systematically correlates with late frontal activity across multiple paradigms while early posterior activity occurs without report, that's strong evidence against IIT's claim that phi-in-posterior-cortex constitutes consciousness. IIT can resist by denying reportability matters, but this resistance has costs—it commits IIT to massive dissociation between consciousness and any behavioral or cognitive access, making consciousness empirically idle in exactly the way that invites elimination.

More fundamentally, the defence's own example refutes the thesis. The claim was that IIT and GWT "cannot be empirically compared the way we compare rival physical theories." But the adversarial collaboration shows they CAN be so compared: you identify shared observables (neural activity patterns, subjective reports), generate differential predictions, run experiments, and assess which theory's predictions hold. That's exactly how rival physical theories are compared.

The fact that theory proponents might resist negative evidence doesn't show the theories are incomparable—it shows theory proponents are human. The thesis conflates "can be empirically compared" with "will immediately convince all proponents," but no scientific theories meet the latter standard. The thesis is wrong.

KILLER (TRIVIAL)

ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as revealing a deep categorical distinction between IIT and GWT that explains why empirical adjudication fails, but this is merely a redescription of what everyone already knows, dressed up as philosophical discovery. That IIT proposes phi as constitutive of consciousness while GWT proposes functional role specifications is explicitly stated in the original papers: Tononi openly calls phi a measure of "what consciousness is," while Dehaene and Naccache explicitly frame GWT as identifying consciousness with a particular cognitive function (global availability). The thesis's "distinction" just relabels this obvious difference using philosophical terminology (identity-claim versus functional-claim) without adding explanatory power. The weak point is the claim that this distinction reveals why empirical comparison fails: but researchers already understand that IIT and GWT differ in this way, and they don't think it prevents comparison—they think it makes comparison harder but still possible through differential predictions. The thesis offers no new insight beyond "these theories say different kinds of things," which is uncontested, then invalidly infers "therefore they cannot be compared," which doesn't follow and which practitioners reject. It's a verbal stipulation (calling one type "identity" and another "functional") masquerading as an explanation for why the field faces difficulties, when the field's difficulties stem from hard empirical problems about consciousness, not from failing to notice that theories make different kinds of claims.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously damages the thesis by showing that the purported "distinction" is already explicitly recognized by the theories' own proponents and by practitioners, and that the thesis adds no explanatory value beyond relabeling what everyone already knows. The move from "different kinds of claims" to "cannot be empirically compared" remains the thesis's own undefended inference rather than a discovery.

DEFENDER

BEST DEFENCE

The objection confuses *noticing* a difference with *understanding its consequences*. Yes, everyone knows IIT and GWT say different things—that's not the contribution. The contribution is diagnosing that this difference is a *category difference* that structurally prevents the kind of empirical adjudication researchers keep attempting.

Consider an analogy: everyone knew Newtonian mechanics and thermodynamics "said different things," but it took philosophical work to show they were making claims at different levels of description (micro versus macro), and that this explained why they couldn't straightforwardly contradict each other despite apparent conflicts. The thesis does similar work: it's not merely observing that IIT and GWT differ, but showing that the specific nature of their difference (identity versus function) generates systematic empirical underdetermination.

The objection claims practitioners "don't think it prevents comparison—they think it makes comparison harder but still possible." But this is exactly the confusion the thesis exposes. Practitioners keep designing "crucial experiments" that both sides then interpret favorably by adjusting what counts as the real target phenomenon. The backward masking case proves this: GWT proponents say late activity shows consciousness requires global broadcast; IIT proponents say early activity shows consciousness is phi while late activity is mere access. Both moves are legitimate *given their respective category of claim*. If consciousness IS phi (identity), then functional correlates are potentially misleading. If consciousness IS a functional role (GWT), then whatever plays that role is constitutive.

The thesis's explanatory value is showing *why* this keeps happening: not because experiments are poorly designed or evidence is insufficient, but because the theories are answering different questions. "What is consciousness made of?" (IIT's identity question) and "What does consciousness do?" (GWT's functional question) can both accommodate the same evidence by adjusting the boundary between essence and accident, between constitutive and correlational.

The "hard empirical problems" the objection mentions aren't independent of the categorical confusion—they're *generated by it*. We keep hitting walls not because consciousness is especially difficult to measure, but because we're trying to run experiments that simultaneously answer "what is X?" and "what does X do?" as if these were competing answers to the same question. They're not.

The philosophical terminology isn't mere relabeling—it's diagnostic. Recognizing that water-is-H2O (identity) and water-is-whatever-fills-rivers (functional) are categorically different claims explains why you can't empirically choose between them: one is about essence, one is about role, and things can have both essences and roles that come apart. The thesis applies this to IIT/GWT.

VERDICT

The defence fails, and this kills the thesis decisively.

The fundamental problem is that the defence's own analogy refutes the thesis. Newtonian mechanics and thermodynamics weren't "prevented from empirical adjudication" by operating at different levels—they were *successfully related* through statistical mechanics, which showed how macro-thermodynamic properties reduce to micro-mechanical states. This is empirical comparison par excellence: showing how one theory's ontology realizes another's functional roles. The defence admits this is what happened, then somehow claims it supports the thesis that different-level theories "couldn't straightforwardly contradict each other." But they could and did: caloric theory (thermodynamic level) contradicted kinetic theory (mechanical level), and experiments decided between them.

More fatally, the defence's claim that practitioners keep "adjusting what counts as the real target phenomenon" proves too little. This is true of *all* theory conflict in immature sciences. Behaviorists adjusted what counted as "real" psychology when cognitivism challenged them. Geocentrists adjusted what counted as "real" motion when heliocentrism challenged them. Theory resistance through boundary adjustment is normal scientific practice, not evidence of category incomparability.

The defence says the thesis explains "why this keeps happening"—but it doesn't. The thesis claims it happens because theories make categorically different claims (identity versus function). But the backward masking case shows resistance happens through *empirical* disagreements: IIT proponents don't just say "we're answering a different question," they say "your evidence doesn't show what you think it shows about neural correlates." That's a standard empirical dispute about what the data mean, not a category difference preventing comparison.

Most devastatingly, the water analogy the defence offers actually refutes the thesis. "Water is H2O" and "water is whatever fills rivers" *can* be empirically compared: we check whether the stuff filling rivers is H2O. If we found rivers filled with XYZ (Putnam's Twin Earth scenario), we'd have to choose: either water isn't essentially H2O, or river-filling isn't essential to water. The empirical finding forces a choice. Similarly with IIT and GWT: if we find phi without any functional role, or functional role without phi, we must choose. The experiments

O2. Any theory of consciousness that claims to be empirically testable is actually unstable—it must either retreat to being unfalsifiable metaphysics or expand to trivialize into a theory of information processing generally.

Scores
promise 4/5 · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
Move · Target
instability — Theorists like Tononi, Dehaene, and Dennett who present their theories as empirically grounded and distinct from philosophical speculation
Sketch

The argument shows that empirically testable theories face a dilemma. Either they make precise, testable predictions about neural correlates, in which case they become vulnerable to the objection that correlation isn't constitution (and thus retreat to claiming the correlations merely track consciousness, not explain it—becoming unfalsifiable metaphysics). Or they avoid this by identifying consciousness with the observable processes themselves, in which case they must either engage in arbitrary restriction (why these processes and not those?) or admit that their account applies to any information-processing system (thermostats, bacteria, etc.), which is widely regarded as unacceptable. The key premise is that the explanatory gap prevents any smooth transition from empirical observation to metaphysical conclusion.

Appraiser (blind)
Identifies a genuine structural dilemma for empirical consciousness research that could reframe debates about scientific testability.
Objections
  • PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, unanswered. The defence invoked epistemic asymmetry but this itself proves too much—would bar any science of subjective experience, not just show instability
  • COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 4/5, unanswered. IIT occupies stable middle ground making falsifiable predictions without trivializing; defence confused having hard questions with being unstable
  • TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, unanswered. Defence equivocated between theories oscillating between positions versus facing pressure; only established the latter, which is trivial
  • EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, unanswered. Defence's attempt to make separating empirical and metaphysical components count as instability applies to all successful science, proving too much
Flaw

The thesis equivocates on what counts as 'instability'—it needs theories to literally oscillate between positions but only shows they face pressure and hard questions, which is true of all scientific theories with metaphysical dimensions

full exchange (8 turns)

KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)

ATTEMPT. If this argument worked, it would equally undermine all scientific theories that bridge observable phenomena and theoretical entities. Consider: Any empirically testable theory of genes must either retreat to unfalsifiable metaphysics or expand to trivialize into a theory of molecular structures generally. The theory faces the same dilemma: either it makes precise, testable predictions about DNA sequences, in which case it becomes vulnerable to the objection that correlation isn't constitution (the DNA merely tracks inheritance, not explains it—becoming unfalsifiable metaphysics), or it identifies genes with observable molecular processes themselves, in which case it must either engage in arbitrary restriction (why these molecules and not those?) or admit that any molecular information-storage system counts as genetic (prions, RNA, etc.). The "explanatory gap" between observing base-pair sequences and concluding we've found genes is structurally identical to the consciousness case. Yet we don't regard gene theory as unstable. The same pattern applies to theories of memory, disease, temperature, or any theoretical posit initially known only through its effects. If the consciousness argument worked, virtually all successful scientific reduction would be impossible—we could never move from correlations to identifications without either "retreating to metaphysics" or "trivializing." This makes the argument self-defeating: it proves scientific theorizing itself is unstable.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The argument's logic, if sound, would delegitimize standard scientific methodology for theoretical identification across domains where we clearly have successful theories.

DEFENDER

BEST DEFENCE

The gene analogy fails because it ignores the unique epistemic asymmetry in consciousness. The gene case involves third-person observable phenomena on both sides: we observe inheritance patterns, then observe molecular structures, then identify them. Any competent observer can verify both the explanandum (inheritance) and the explanans (DNA sequences) through public, repeatable observation.

Consciousness is fundamentally different: the explanandum is first-person phenomenal experience, accessible only to the subject, while the explanans is third-person neural activity, publicly observable. This creates an explanatory gap with no parallel in genetics.

When we identify genes with DNA, we can point to functional integration: DNA does what genes were posited to do (encode, replicate, vary, etc.) in ways publicly verifiable. The identification succeeds because both sides occupy the same epistemic space—third-person observation.

But when IIT or GNW identifies consciousness with certain information-processing patterns, we cannot verify the identification in the same way. We cannot observe whether integrated information *feels like something* except through the subject's report. And subject reports are precisely what generated the correlation we're now trying to explain away.

This means consciousness theories face a genuine dilemma absent in gene theory:
- If they rely on subject reports to verify the theory, they remain at the correlation level (reports correlate with neural patterns)
- If they dismiss reports and identify consciousness with the neural pattern alone, they've changed the subject—they're now theorizing about information processing, not about what it's like to be conscious

The gene theorist can say "we found the thing that does the inheriting." The consciousness theorist cannot similarly say "we found the thing that does the experiencing" without either relying on the very reports that keep them at correlation-level, or stipulating an identification that abandons the original explanandum.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though it identifies something real.

There IS an epistemic asymmetry in consciousness—the first-person/third-person divide is genuine. But this doesn't save the thesis from proving too much; it just relocates where it proves too much.

The defence essentially concedes that consciousness cannot be studied like other scientific posits because of this asymmetry. But this concession itself proves too much: it would equally apply to pain, emotion, or any phenomenal state. If the argument works, then *any* science of subjective experience is impossible—not just unstable theories, but any empirically testable theory whatsoever.

Moreover, the defence's own logic undermines the thesis's instability claim. If the epistemic asymmetry is insurmountable, then theories aren't *unstable* (shifting between positions)—they're simply *impossible*. The thesis claims theories oscillate between metaphysics and trivialization. The defence claims they cannot get started at all. These are different claims.

Finally, the gene analogy still bites: early gene theory faced exactly the "correlation vs. constitution" challenge the defence describes. Critics asked whether DNA merely *correlated* with inheritance or *constituted* it. The identification succeeded not by eliminating this question but by making it scientifically productive. The defence provides no principle explaining why consciousness must be different in kind rather than merely harder.

The defence fails to block the proves-too-much objection.

KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)

ATTEMPT. Consider Integrated Information Theory (IIT) as applied to split-brain patients. IIT makes the precise, empirically testable prediction that severing the corpus callosum should dramatically reduce phi (integrated information) and thus split consciousness into two separate conscious entities, each with lower phi than the original unified consciousness.

This prediction is:
1. Empirically testable (we can measure neural integration before and after commissurotomy)
2. Makes specific claims about consciousness (predicts phenomenological changes)
3. Has been partially confirmed (split-brain patients do show evidence of divided awareness in certain experimental conditions)

Yet IIT does NOT become unstable in the way the thesis predicts. It doesn't retreat to unfalsifiable metaphysics when challenged—Tononi and colleagues continue refining quantitative measures of phi and making additional testable predictions. Nor does it trivialize to apply to all information processing—IIT explicitly excludes feed-forward systems and systems with low integration, maintaining principled boundaries.

The theory remains stable at precisely the middle ground the thesis claims is impossible: making empirical predictions about neural correlates while maintaining that these correlates constitute (not merely track) consciousness, without expanding to cover thermostats or bacteria. The split-brain case demonstrates that at least one theory resists both horns of the supposed dilemma.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious counterexample. IIT appears to occupy exactly the stable middle ground the thesis claims cannot exist, making testable empirical predictions while avoiding both metaphysical retreat and trivializing expansion.

DEFENDER

BEST DEFENCE

The IIT example actually *demonstrates* rather than refutes the instability. Look at what happens when we examine IIT's treatment of split-brain patients more carefully.

IIT makes a testable prediction about phi values dropping after commissurotomy—this is the "empirical" horn. But when pressed on what this *means* for consciousness, the theory faces exactly the dilemma described:

First horn (retreat to metaphysics): When critics ask "does lower phi mean *less* consciousness or *two* consciousnesses?", IIT must either:
- Rely on phenomenological reports from split-brain patients (but these reports are inconsistent and interpretation-dependent—patients mostly report feeling unified), which keeps us at correlation-level, OR
- Stipulate that phi *is* consciousness regardless of what subjects report, which makes the theory unfalsifiable—no possible report could refute it

Second horn (trivialization): When IIT tries to avoid this by identifying consciousness directly with integrated information, it faces the boundary problem. The theory's "principled boundaries" (excluding feed-forward systems, requiring high integration) are actually arbitrary restrictions imposed to avoid counterintuitive results.

Consider: Why does IIT exclude feed-forward networks? Not because of any principled connection between feedback and phenomenology, but because including them would be embarrassing. A purely feed-forward image classifier would have some phi > 0 under the mathematical definition. IIT restricts this *ad hoc* to avoid the implication.

Similarly with "low integration" exclusions—where's the threshold? IIT cannot provide a non-arbitrary cutoff between "enough integration for consciousness" and "too little." Any line drawn is drawn to match our pre-theoretic intuitions about what should count as conscious, not derived from the theory itself.

The split-brain case actually shows the instability in action: IIT oscillates between making the empirical claim (phi drops) and making metaphysical stipulations about what this means for consciousness (whether it splits, diminishes, or remains unified). When pressed on the metaphysical question, it either defers to reports (correlation) or insists on the identification regardless of reports (unfalsifiable). When pressed on why its boundaries aren't arbitrary, it either admits they're somewhat stipulative or expands the theory to cover cases we find absurd.

The appearance of stability is maintained only by not pressing too hard on either horn—but the instability is there, latent in the theory's structure.

VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defence tries to show IIT exhibits instability by pointing to interpretive flexibility and boundary problems. But this confuses *having difficult questions* with *being unstable*. Every scientific theory has interpretive challenges and boundary cases—this doesn't make them unstable in the sense the thesis requires.

The thesis claims theories must either "retreat to unfalsifiable metaphysics" OR "expand to trivialize." IIT does neither:

On metaphysical retreat: IIT doesn't become unfalsifiable when split-brain reports are messy. It makes quantitative predictions about phi that can be measured independently of phenomenological reports. The theory predicts specific changes in neural integration patterns. These predictions can be wrong—if commissurotomy didn't reduce measured integration, IIT would be in trouble. That's falsifiability.

On trivialization: The defence claims IIT's boundaries are "ad hoc" and "arbitrary," but this is false. The exclusion of feed-forward systems follows from the mathematical definition of integration—feed-forward systems have zero or near-zero phi by IIT's own measure, not by stipulation. The theory *derives* its boundaries from its formal framework rather than imposing them to avoid embarrassment.

The defence's claim that "any line drawn is drawn to match pre-theoretic intuitions" applies equally to temperature (where's the exact boundary between hot and cold?), life (where's the exact threshold for living vs. non-living?), or any scientific category with gradual boundaries. This doesn't make thermodynamics or biology unstable.

Most critically, the defence equivocates on "instability." The thesis claims theories oscillate *between positions*—retreating when pressed one way, expanding when pressed another. But IIT maintains consistent commitments: phi is consciousness, integration is measured thus, these are the predictions. Having interpretive questions about edge cases (do split-brain patients have one consciousness or two?) is not the same as oscillating between metaphysical and trivial versions of the theory.

The counterexample stands: IIT occupies stable middle ground, making falsifiable predictions without trivializing. The defence has not shown otherwise.

KILLER (TRIVIAL)

ATTEMPT

The thesis presents itself as a substantive discovery about the structure of consciousness theories, but it's actually a trivial consequence of how we've stipulatively defined "unstable" combined with an uncontested verbal point about explanatory gaps.

The weak point is the claim that theories "must either retreat to being unfalsifiable metaphysics or expand to trivialize." This sounds like an empirical discovery about consciousness theories, but it's actually just unpacking what we mean by "the explanatory gap" in a particular rhetorical register.

Everyone already agrees there's an explanatory gap between physical processes and phenomenal experience—this is Levine's uncontested point from 1983. The thesis merely restates this as: "therefore theories will face pressure from two directions." But facing pressure isn't instability—it's just having a hard problem.

The thesis then stipulatively defines any response to this pressure as "retreat" or "expansion," ensuring theories count as "unstable" by definition. If a theory maintains that correlations are constitutive, that's labeled "retreat to metaphysics." If it identifies consciousness with physical processes, that's labeled "trivialization." But these are just verbal redescriptions of the two standard positions in philosophy of mind (dualism vs. reductionism), now wearing the costume of "instability."

The supposed discovery is: "Hard problems remain hard and theorists take different positions on them." This is true but completely uncontested. Calling this "instability" rather than "disagreement" or "difficulty" is a verbal stipulation, not a finding. The thesis mistakes a dramatized restatement of the explanatory gap for a novel structural insight about theory dynamics.

STRENGTH: 3/5

This attack has genuine force. The thesis does rely heavily on the uncontested existence of the explanatory gap and adds primarily rhetorical packaging ("instability," "retreat," "trivialize"). However, it's not entirely trivial—there IS a structural claim about theories being unable to occupy stable middle ground, which goes beyond merely noting disagreement. The attack succeeds in showing the thesis is more verbal stipulation than discovery, but doesn't quite reduce it to pure tautology.

DEFENDER

BEST DEFENCE

The objection mistakes a structural diagnosis for mere relabeling. The thesis is not simply restating "the explanatory gap exists" or "people disagree"—it's making a specific claim about theory *dynamics*: that empirically testable theories cannot maintain a stable position but must oscillate or collapse.

Consider the difference between these claims:
- "Consciousness is hard to explain" (trivial, uncontested)
- "Theorists disagree about consciousness" (trivial, uncontested)
- "Any empirically testable theory is forced to shift between incompatible positions or abandon its original explanatory target" (substantive, contested)

The thesis makes the third claim. This is not verbal stipulation—it's a prediction about what happens when theories try to do specific work.

The key is the *empirically testable* constraint. The thesis doesn't claim all theories face this instability—pure metaphysical theories can remain stable (property dualism, panpsychism, etc.). The instability arises specifically when theories try to be empirically grounded while explaining phenomenal consciousness.

Here's why this is non-trivial: It predicts a specific failure mode. When Dehaene's Global Neuronal Workspace theory is pressed on whether broadcast mechanisms *are* consciousness or merely *correlate* with it, the theory must either:
1. Make the identification (broadcast = consciousness), which forces expansion to cover any broadcast system, OR
2. Maintain the distinction (broadcast correlates with consciousness), which removes empirical testability—we can only test broadcast, not consciousness itself

This isn't just "facing pressure" or "disagreement"—it's a structural bind. The theory cannot simultaneously maintain empirical testability AND explanatory adequacy AND principled boundaries. Something must give.

The objection claims this is just "dualism vs. reductionism" relabeled. But that's wrong. Both dualists and reductionists can maintain stable positions. The thesis claims that *empirically testable* theories cannot—they're forced to either become non-empirical (dualism) or abandon the phenomenal target (reductionism about information processing generally). The instability is the inability to remain in the middle.

The non-triviality is demonstrated by the fact that major theorists resist this diagnosis. Tononi insists IIT is both empirically testable AND specifically about phenomenal consciousness AND doesn't apply to thermostats. The thesis says this combination is unstable. That's a substantive, falsifiable claim about theory structure, not mere verbal redescription.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though it fights hard.

The defence tries to establish non-triviality by pointing to a "structural bind" and "specific failure mode." But examine what this amounts to: when theories are pressed on the metaphysical question (constitution vs. correlation), they must take a position. Taking a position is then labeled "instability."

This is still essentially trivial. The defence's own example reveals this: "When Dehaene is pressed on whether broadcast mechanisms ARE consciousness or merely CORRELATE with it, the theory must either..." Yes—the theory must take a position on a metaphysical question. That's not instability; that's having to answer a question.

The defence claims "both dualists and reductionists can maintain stable positions" but "empirically testable theories cannot." This is confused. Dehaene IS a reductionist—he thinks broadcast mechanisms constitute consciousness. That's a stable position. The defence wants to say this position "forces expansion to cover any broadcast system," but as the IIT discussion showed, theories can maintain principled boundaries without trivializing.

The core problem: the defence equivocates between two meanings of "instability":
1. A theory that literally oscillates between positions over time (genuine instability)
2. A theory that faces pressure and must make difficult choices (just having hard questions)

The thesis needs meaning (1) to be interesting, but only establishes meaning (2), which is trivial. Every theory in every domain faces pressure and must make choices about boundary cases and metaphysical interpretation. Calling this "instability" is indeed mere relabeling.

The defence's claim that "major theorists resist this diagnosis" doesn't help. Theorists like Tononi don't resist because they're confused about their theory's structure—they resist because "instability" misdescribes what's actually happening. They maintain consistent positions while working on hard problems. That's stability.

The objection succeeds: the thesis is largely trivial, restating the explanatory gap in dramatic language and labeling the standard theory-building challenges as "instability." The structural claim dissolves into verbal stipulation.

KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)

ATTEMPT

The thesis equivocates on "empirically testable" between a weak reading (makes some empirical predictions) and a strong reading (is fully empirically verifiable), and the argument needs both readings to generate the dilemma.

The weak point is the opening claim: "Any theory of consciousness that claims to be empirically testable is actually unstable."

On the WEAK reading: "empirically testable" means the theory makes some empirical predictions about observable correlates (neural activity, behavior, etc.). On this reading, the thesis is interesting—it would be surprising if theories making any empirical predictions were inherently unstable.

On the STRONG reading: "empirically testable" means the theory claims phenomenal consciousness itself can be fully verified through empirical observation. On this reading, the thesis is plausible—of course such theories face problems, since phenomenal properties aren't publicly observable.

The argument needs BOTH readings. It needs the weak reading to have interesting scope (capturing theories like IIT, GNW, HOT that make empirical predictions). But it needs the strong reading to generate the instability—the dilemma only works if we demand that theories empirically verify the phenomenal properties themselves, not just their correlates.

The sketch reveals this: "they make precise, testable predictions about neural correlates" (weak reading) but then "correlation isn't constitution" becomes a problem only if we expected empirical tests to establish constitution of phenomenal properties (strong reading). Theories making empirical predictions about correlates (weak) don't claim to empirically verify qualia themselves (strong), so they don't face the dilemma. The argument slides between these readings.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This is a serious problem. The thesis generates its dilemma only by equivocating on what "empirically testable" means. Theories that make empirical predictions about neural correlates while remaining agnostic or metaphysical about the constitution question don't face the instability described—they're stable precisely by separating empirical and metaphysical components.

DEFENDER

BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies where the equivocation allegedly occurs. The thesis uses "empirically testable" consistently in the weak sense throughout—theories that make empirical predictions about observable correlates. The instability arises not from smuggling in a strong reading, but from the internal pressure within theories that make only weak empirical claims.

Here's why: A theory that merely predicts neural correlates while remaining "agnostic or metaphysical about the constitution question" is not actually a theory of consciousness at all—it's a theory of neural activity that happens to correlate with consciousness reports. This is the first horn of the dilemma (retreat to metaphysics), not an escape from it.

The objection claims theories can be "stable precisely by separating empirical and metaphysical components." But this separation IS the instability the thesis diagnoses. Consider what this means in practice:

When GNW predicts that conscious access requires global broadcast, is this:
- An empirical claim about what consciousness IS (broadcast = access consciousness), OR
- An empirical claim about what merely CORRELATES with consciousness (broadcast tracks consciousness but doesn't constitute it)?

If the first: the theory must defend why broadcast specifically constitutes consciousness rather than any information-processing pattern (expansion pressure toward trivialization).

If the second: the theory has "separated" its empirical component (broadcast) from its metaphysical component (consciousness), which means the empirical predictions no longer test the theory of consciousness—they test only the theory of broadcast. The consciousness part has become metaphysical speculation untethered from the empirical work.

This separation is not stability—it's the retreat to metaphysics the thesis describes. The theory presents itself as empirically testing claims about consciousness, but under pressure reveals that it's only testing claims about neural activity, with consciousness as metaphysical addendum.

The objection's proposed stable position—"making empirical predictions about correlates while remaining agnostic about constitution"—is precisely the unstable middle ground. Such theories face constant pressure: Why should we accept this as a theory of consciousness rather than just neuroscience? Either make the constitutive claim (and face expansion pressure) or admit the empirical work doesn't actually bear on consciousness (metaphysical retreat).

The thesis doesn't equivocate on "empirically testable." It maintains the weak reading throughout and shows that even this weak empirical commitment generates instability when combined with the explanatory gap. The instability comes not from demanding strong empirical verification of qualia, but from the tension between making any empirical predictions and explaining phenomenal consciousness.

VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defence claims theories that "separate empirical and metaphysical components" exhibit instability rather than escaping it. But this radically overextends what counts as "instability" to the point of absurdity.

Consider the defence's key claim: a theory that predicts neural correlates while remaining agnostic about constitution "is not actually a theory of consciousness at all—it's a theory of neural activity that happens to correlate with consciousness reports."

This is false. Such a theory IS a theory of consciousness in the perfectly respectable scientific sense—it identifies the neural basis of consciousness without settling metaphysical questions about the nature of phenomenal properties. This is how most successful science works: we identify what realizes a phenomenon without resolving every philosophical question about it.

The defence tries to make "facing pressure" equivalent to "being unstable." It says theories face "constant pressure: Why should we accept this as a theory of consciousness?" But facing questions is not instability. Evolutionary theory faces constant pressure ("Why should we accept common descent?"), but it's not unstable—it maintains consistent commitments while answering challenges.

More critically, the defence's own logic proves too much in exactly the way earlier objections identified. By the defence's standard, ANY theory that makes empirical predictions while leaving metaphysical questions open is "unstable." But this describes virtually all successful science:

- Genetic theory predicts DNA correlates with inheritance while remaining agnostic about whether genes are "really" DNA or DNA merely realizes genetic function
- Thermodynamics predicts temperature correlates with molecular motion while remaining agnostic about whether temperature IS mean kinetic energy or merely correlates with it
- Memory theory predicts neural patterns correlate with recall while remaining agnostic about constitutive questions

These theories are stable despite "separating empirical and metaphysical components." The defence provides no principle for why consciousness must be different.

The equivocation objection succeeds. The thesis needs theories to claim strong empirical verification of phenomenal properties to generate genuine instability, but actual theories (IIT, GNW, etc.) make only weak empirical claims about correlates. These theories can remain stable by doing what all successful science does: making empirical predictions while leaving some metaphysical questions open or underspecified.

The defence's attempt to classify this standard scientific practice as "instability" or "metaphysical retreat" is verbal legislation that makes the thesis trivial: "theories that

O3. Accepting that unconscious processing can be arbitrarily sophisticated commits you to accepting that no functional profile, no matter how complex, could ever confirm a consciousness theory.

Scores
promise 4/5 · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
Move · Target
