commitment — Functionalist approaches to testing consciousness theories, including many versions of GNW that rely on reportability and behavioral measures
Sketch

Most researchers now accept cases like blindsight, subliminal priming of complex decisions, and unconscious skill learning—showing that unconscious processing can match conscious processing in sophistication. But this acceptance has a devastating implication: if unconscious systems can mimic any functional profile of conscious systems, then functional evidence cannot discriminate between theories. Any behavioral test could in principle be passed by an unconscious system. IIT theorists and functionalists both accept the premise about unconscious sophistication, yet still propose functional tests. They're committed to either (a) consciousness making no functional difference, or (b) there being something functionally detectable that they haven't yet specified—but they've already ruled out all functional candidates.

Appraiser (blind)
Powerful internal tension argument: accepting unconscious sophistication undermines the functional testing both camps actually employ.
Objections
  • ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, unanswered. Defence claimed a novel reductio targeting empirical acceptance, but the judge found this was already theorized as the 'calibration problem' and that GNW theorists specify functional differences unconscious processing lacks
  • PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, unanswered. Defence argued consciousness is unique in lacking independent access, but the judge found this fails because we have the same inferential access to life-in-bacteria and pain-in-others, where functional confirmation demonstrably works
  • COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, unanswered. Defence claimed anesthesiologists don't accept the premise, but the judge found they accept both that unconscious processing can be sophisticated and that specific profiles reliably indicate consciousness in context—no contradiction
  • TRIVIAL — landed 5/5, unanswered. Defence reframed as pragmatic inconsistency, but the judge found this abandons the original claim and reduces to normal scientific refinement—researchers aren't contradicting themselves by continuing to propose tests
Flaw

The thesis either overgeneralizes to all scientific domains where functional confirmation demonstrably works (life, pain, anesthesia levels) or, when narrowed to avoid this, reduces to the unremarkable observation that refining criteria after failures is normal science, not a devastating commitment

full exchange (8 turns)

KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)

ATTEMPT. This thesis articulates the "functional indiscriminability argument" that has been central to debates about consciousness since at least Block's 1995 distinction between phenomenal and access consciousness. Block explicitly argued that P-consciousness could be functionally identical to states lacking phenomenal character, making functional profiles insufficient for consciousness detection. Chalmers' "hard problem" (1995) makes essentially the same point: zombie thought experiments show that any functional organization could in principle exist without consciousness, so functional tests cannot confirm consciousness theories. More recently, Irvine (2013) and Cohen & Dennett (2011) debated precisely whether unconscious processing can match conscious processing in sophistication while remaining unconscious. The thesis's specific focus on "arbitrarily sophisticated" unconscious processing as undermining functional confirmation is the core of what Overgaard (2017) calls the "consciousness-function dissociation problem." The weak point is the phrase "accepting that unconscious processing can be arbitrarily sophisticated" - the thesis equivocates between logical possibility and actual empirical cases, but the existing positions already occupy both versions.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is seriously damaging. The thesis appears to be a restatement of well-established positions in the consciousness literature rather than a novel commitment-revealing move. The argument structure is Block and Chalmers's, just applied specifically to contemporary empirical acceptance of unconscious sophistication.

DEFENDER

BEST DEFENCE

The thesis is not a restatement of Block or Chalmers—it's a *reductio* targeted at researchers who reject their conclusions while accepting empirical premises that entail them.

Block and Chalmers argued from *logical possibility*: zombies are conceivable, therefore functional profiles cannot metaphysically guarantee consciousness. Most functionalists and GNW theorists reject this—they deny zombies are genuinely possible, or they accept only "weak" metaphysical conclusions while maintaining that functional profiles can still *empirically confirm* theories in our world.

This thesis operates differently. It says: *You already accept, on empirical grounds, that actual unconscious systems in actual brains perform arbitrarily sophisticated processing*. You accept blindsight patients navigating obstacles, unconscious semantic priming, procedural learning matching declarative performance. You're not dealing with philosophical zombies—you're dealing with empirically documented cases where the functional work gets done without consciousness.

The commitment-revealing move is this: if you accept these empirical cases while still proposing that reportability, global availability, or flexible control can *confirm* a consciousness theory, you face a dilemma the zombie argument never posed. Either (a) you must specify what functional difference consciousness makes that these unconscious cases somehow lack—but you've already accepted they lack nothing functional—or (b) you must admit your proposed tests cannot actually confirm your theory, since unconscious systems pass them.

This isn't Block's metaphysical point. It's an *internal* inconsistency between accepting the neuroscience of unconscious sophistication and proposing functional confirmation methods. The thesis shows that empirical liberalism about unconscious processing is incompatible with methodological functionalism about theory testing—a tension that doesn't exist in Block's framework because he never accepted the empirical premise in the first place.

VERDICT

The defence fails, but not completely.

The thesis does identify a genuine tension that differs from Block's argument: researchers who accept rich unconscious processing while proposing functional tests face a burden Block's opponents don't. But this tension was already explicitly theorized as the "calibration problem" in consciousness science (Sandberg et al. 2010, Overgaard & Overgaard 2010). The question "how do we calibrate our measures when unconscious processing is so sophisticated?" is the central methodological challenge these researchers acknowledge.

More fatally: the defence's dilemma is false. Option (c) exists: consciousness makes a *specific* functional difference that unconscious processing, despite its sophistication, systematically lacks. GNW theorists explicitly propose this—global broadcast to a workspace, enabling a specific kind of flexible integration. They don't claim unconscious processing lacks sophistication; they claim it lacks *this particular functional architecture*. Whether they're right is open, but they're not committed to the contradiction the thesis alleges.

The thesis survives as a challenge to poorly-specified functionalism, but dies against positions that specify the functional difference clearly.

KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)

ATTEMPT. If this argument worked, it would prove that no empirical evidence could ever confirm any theory about any unobservable process that has functional effects. Consider: We accept that non-living chemical systems can be arbitrarily sophisticated (autocatalytic networks, self-organizing systems, complex molecular computation). Therefore, by this thesis's logic, accepting that non-living chemistry can be arbitrarily sophisticated commits you to accepting that no functional profile could ever confirm a theory of life. Any metabolic test could in principle be passed by a non-living system. But this is absurd—we do successfully use functional profiles (metabolism, reproduction, homeostasis) to confirm theories of life and distinguish living from non-living systems, despite non-living chemistry being arbitrarily complex. Similarly for: computation vs. non-computation (accepting physical systems can be arbitrarily complex doesn't prevent functional confirmation of computation theories), agency vs. mechanism, memory vs. mere information storage. The weak point is the move from "unconscious systems can be arbitrarily sophisticated" to "therefore no functional profile could discriminate." This conflates sophistication-in-general with possession-of-the-specific-functional-profile-that-consciousness-theories-propose. The thesis would, if valid, prove that functional discrimination is impossible for any phenomenon, which is obviously false.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is serious damage. The argument structure genuinely does overgeneralize to cases where we clearly can use functional profiles for confirmation despite underlying processes being arbitrarily sophisticated. The thesis needs to explain why consciousness is special in a way that life, computation, and agency are not.

DEFENDER

BEST DEFENCE

The consciousness case is disanalogous in a way that saves the thesis from overgeneralization.

For life, computation, and agency, we have *independent access* to the phenomenon that lets us calibrate functional profiles against ground truth. We can observe metabolism, reproduction, and homeostasis in paradigm living systems, then extract the functional profile that distinguishes them from non-living chemistry. We build computers, know which physical systems are computing, and reverse-engineer the functional architecture. We are agents, experience our own agency, and can map its functional signature.

But consciousness is unique: we have *no* independent access to it in other systems. I know I'm conscious through first-person acquaintance. For others—including other humans—I have only functional profiles: reports, behaviors, neural activity. I cannot independently verify that these correlate with consciousness the way I can verify that metabolism correlates with life by checking both independently.

This asymmetry is fatal for functional confirmation. With life, the method is: (1) identify living things independently, (2) find their common functional profile, (3) use that profile to test theories and detect new instances. With consciousness, we cannot do step (1) for other systems. We're trying to use functional profiles to *establish* the presence of consciousness, not just to characterize something we've already identified.

Now add the empirical premise: unconscious processing can be arbitrarily sophisticated. This means that for any functional profile F you propose as consciousness-indicating, I can point to empirically documented cases of F occurring without consciousness (or at least, without the consciousness you're trying to detect). Blindsight shows sophisticated visual processing without visual experience. Subliminal priming shows semantic processing without awareness. You cannot reply "but living systems have metabolism" because we independently verified that metabolism goes with life. You have no such verification for F and consciousness.

The thesis doesn't claim functional discrimination is *always* impossible. It claims functional discrimination is impossible when (a) you lack independent access to the target phenomenon, and (b) you accept that systems lacking the phenomenon can exhibit arbitrary functional sophistication. Consciousness uniquely satisfies both conditions.

The life analogy fails because life violates condition (a)—we have independent access through multiple converging observations that don't rely solely on functional profiles.

VERDICT

The defence fails.

The "independent access" move is the standard response, but it doesn't save the thesis because the objection's examples can be reconstructed to match consciousness's epistemic situation exactly.

Consider: I am a scientist who has never experienced pain (congenital insensitivity to pain). I'm trying to confirm theories about pain in others. I have no first-person access to pain in other systems—only functional profiles (withdrawal behaviors, reports, neural activity). Non-pain states can be arbitrarily sophisticated (actors feigning pain, reflexive withdrawal without pain experience, neural activity in anesthetized patients). By the thesis's logic, I'm committed to accepting that no functional profile could confirm a pain theory.

But this is false. Pain scientists in this position *can* and *do* confirm theories by finding functional profiles that systematically co-occur with pain reports in calibration cases, then using those profiles for detection. The method works despite lacking "independent access" to others' pain.

More decisively: the defence claims we have independent access to life but not consciousness. This is wrong. We have *exactly the same kind* of access to both in other systems—namely, inference from functional profiles. I don't directly observe metabolism in a bacterium any more than I directly observe consciousness in you. I observe functional indicators (movement, growth, chemical exchange) and infer life. The asymmetry the defence needs doesn't exist.

The thesis proves too much. If it worked for consciousness, it would work for pain-in-others, life-in-bacteria, and any phenomenon where we lack direct access and must infer from functional profiles. Since functional confirmation clearly works in these cases, the argument structure is flawed.

The thesis is dead.

KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)

ATTEMPT. Consider the case of distinguishing genuine understanding from mere symbol manipulation in language systems—the Chinese Room problem. We accept that unconscious/non-understanding symbol manipulation can be arbitrarily sophisticated (it can pass Turing tests, generate coherent novels, solve complex problems). Yet we successfully use functional profiles to confirm theories about understanding. Linguists and cognitive scientists propose that understanding requires specific functional architectures: grounded symbol systems connected to perception and action, integration across multiple modalities, certain patterns of generalization and error. These theories ARE empirically testable and confirmable despite sophisticated non-understanding systems existing.

Similarly, a concrete consciousness case: We accept that unconscious processing during certain anesthesia states can be highly sophisticated (the brain still processes speech, maintains homeostasis, performs complex regulation). Yet anesthesiologists successfully use functional profiles (response to command, purposeful movement, explicit recall) to confirm theories about consciousness levels and distinguish conscious from unconscious states. The functional tests work for theory confirmation despite sophisticated unconscious processing existing. The thesis predicts this should be impossible, but it demonstrably occurs. The weak point is the universal quantifier "no functional profile... could ever confirm"—counterexamples where functional confirmation succeeds despite sophisticated unconscious alternatives show this is too strong.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — These counterexamples seriously damage the thesis. They show that functional confirmation can work even when we accept sophisticated unconscious/non-target processing, contradicting the thesis's central claim.

DEFENDER

BEST DEFENCE

The counterexamples fail because they equivocate on "sophisticated" and miss the dialectical structure of the commitment.

On the Chinese Room case: The thesis doesn't claim that *we* cannot distinguish understanding from non-understanding using functional profiles. It claims that *if you accept* that non-understanding systems can replicate any functional profile of understanding systems, *then* you're committed to functional profiles being insufficient. Searle's Chinese Room argument is precisely an attempt to show this commitment—that functionalists who accept the room's functional equivalence must either deny it understands (contra functionalism) or accept it understands (contra intuition). The thesis identifies the same structure for consciousness. The counterexample actually *supports* the thesis by showing the parallel commitment problem.

On anesthesiology: This case reveals the crucial distinction the thesis exploits. Anesthesiologists succeed because they *don't* accept that unconscious processing during anesthesia can match *every* functional profile of conscious processing. They specifically deny that unconscious anesthetized patients show purposeful movement in response to command or form explicit recall. The functional tests work precisely because there *are* functional differences—the sophisticated unconscious processing during anesthesia is not "arbitrarily" sophisticated in the relevant sense.

Here's the commitment-revealing move the thesis makes: Many consciousness researchers accept cases like blindsight, subliminal priming, and unconscious skill learning as showing that unconscious processing can match conscious processing *on the very dimensions they propose as consciousness-indicating*. Blindsight patients navigate obstacles (spatial processing without visual consciousness). Subliminal primes affect complex decisions (semantic processing without awareness). Unconscious skill learning achieves expert performance (flexible control without conscious access).

The anesthesia case differs crucially: we calibrate the functional tests by finding profiles that unconscious states *don't* match. But if you've already accepted that unconscious processing can match those profiles in other contexts (blindsight patients DO navigate, unconscious processing DOES guide flexible behavior), you've undermined your own calibration.

The thesis doesn't claim functional confirmation is always impossible. It claims: *If you accept the empirical premise that unconscious processing can be arbitrarily sophisticated—meaning it can match any functional profile you might propose—then you cannot coherently propose functional tests*. The anesthesiologist doesn't accept this premise; they maintain specific functional boundaries. Researchers who accept rich unconscious processing while proposing the same functional profiles as tests are in a different position.

The universal quantifier "no functional profile... could ever" is not too strong—it's conditional. The thesis says: *Given that you accept unconscious sophistication, no functional profile could confirm your theory*. It's a reductio of the combination of commitments, not a universal claim about functional confirmation.

VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defence tries to save the thesis by claiming anesthesiologists "don't accept" that unconscious processing can match the relevant functional profiles. But this misunderstands how empirical acceptance works and reveals the thesis's fatal flaw.

Anesthesiologists accept that unconscious processing *can in principle* be arbitrarily sophisticated—they know about blindsight, subliminal priming, complex unconscious regulation. They also know that unconscious processing *in anesthetized patients specifically* doesn't produce purposeful movement to command. These are not contradictory positions. They're accepting both:
1. Unconscious processing can be functionally sophisticated in various ways
2. Specific functional profiles reliably correlate with consciousness in specific contexts

The thesis demands that accepting (1) commits you to rejecting (2). But the anesthesia case shows this is false. You can accept that unconscious systems *somewhere* exhibit sophisticated processing while maintaining that *in your experimental context*, specific functional profiles reliably indicate consciousness.

More fatally: the defence's interpretation of "arbitrarily sophisticated" as meaning "can match ANY functional profile you might propose" makes the thesis trivial. If unconscious processing can literally match any functional profile, then by definition nothing functionally distinguishes conscious from unconscious processing, and of course functional tests fail. But this is just stipulating the conclusion.

The interesting question is whether accepting *actual documented cases* of sophisticated unconscious processing (blindsight, priming, etc.) commits you to functional testing being impossible. The answer is no, because those cases show sophistication in *some* dimensions while potentially lacking it in others. GNW theorists accept blindsight but claim it lacks global broadcast. IIT theorists accept subliminal priming but claim it lacks integrated information. They're not contradicting themselves—they're proposing that consciousness involves a *specific* functional architecture that documented unconscious cases lack.

The thesis dies because it conflates "unconscious processing can be sophisticated in many ways" with "unconscious processing can match every functional profile," and the counterexamples show this conflation is unjustified.

KILLER (TRIVIAL)

ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as revealing a hidden commitment, but it's actually just restating the definitional relationship between functionalism and the possibility of functional zombies—a relationship that's been explicit in the literature since functionalism was first articulated. The weak point is that this isn't a *discovery* about what researchers are "committed to"—it's a verbal unpacking of what "unconscious processing can be arbitrarily sophisticated" means.

If "arbitrarily sophisticated" means "can match any functional profile," then "no functional profile could discriminate" is true by definition—you've just stipulated that functional profiles don't discriminate. If it means something weaker (like "can be sophisticated in many documented ways"), then the thesis is false, as the previous exchanges showed.

The thesis tries to sound like it's exposing a contradiction between accepting empirical cases (blindsight, priming) and proposing functional tests. But there's no contradiction—researchers who accept those cases simply claim consciousness involves a *specific* functional profile those cases lack. The thesis either reduces to "if you define unconscious processing as functionally identical to conscious processing, they're functionally identical" (trivial) or "accepting some sophisticated unconscious processing means accepting all functional profiles can be unconscious" (false). Either way, it's not revealing a hidden commitment; it's either stipulating an equivalence or making an invalid inference that researchers explicitly reject.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This is seriously damaging. The thesis collapses into either a tautology (if we grant its interpretation of "arbitrarily sophisticated") or a non-sequitur (if we use the empirical interpretation). It's not revealing what researchers are committed to—it's either restating what they explicitly deny or playing with definitions.

DEFENDER

BEST DEFENCE

The thesis is neither tautological nor invalid—it identifies a *pragmatic* inconsistency between methodological commitments that researchers actually hold simultaneously.

The objection claims the thesis either stipulates its conclusion or makes an invalid inference. But this misses the dialectical target. The thesis isn't a deductive proof; it's a challenge to a *research program's coherence*. Here's the actual commitment structure:

What researchers actually accept:
1. Empirical liberalism: Unconscious processing can perform tasks previously thought to require consciousness—navigation (blindsight), semantic processing (subliminal priming), skill learning, complex decision-making
2. Methodological functionalism: We can test consciousness theories by identifying functional profiles (reportability, global availability, flexible control) that distinguish conscious from unconscious processing
3. Progressive discovery: As we find more sophisticated unconscious processing, we refine our functional criteria but maintain that some functional profile will work

The thesis reveals these three commitments are in tension. Each time you accept a new case of sophisticated unconscious processing, you're accepting that *yet another* functional profile you might have proposed doesn't actually track consciousness. The pattern shows an empirical trend: the space of "functional profiles that could distinguish consciousness" keeps shrinking as unconscious sophistication grows.

This isn't stipulating that unconscious processing matches *all* profiles (tautology), nor claiming that accepting *some* sophisticated cases entails accepting *all* profiles (invalid inference). It's observing that the research program's own discoveries are systematically undermining its methodology. The commitment is to *both* discovering ever-more-sophisticated unconscious processing *and* believing that functional profiles will ultimately confirm theories—but each discovery makes the latter less credible.

The real bite: Researchers cannot specify in advance which functional profile consciousness requires, because they keep discovering unconscious systems that have the profiles they previously proposed. GNW theorists said "global availability"—but then accepted that unconscious processing can be globally available in certain senses (widespread neural activation, influence on multiple systems). They refined to "broadcast to a specific workspace"—but cannot independently verify this workspace exists without assuming the functional profile they're trying to establish.

This is not trivial. It's showing that the research program has a *methodological regress*: you need functional criteria to test theories, but you keep finding that unconscious processing meets your criteria, so you refine your criteria, but you have no independent way to verify the refined criteria except by assuming they track consciousness, which is what you're trying to establish.

The commitment revealed is: *believing that functional testing will work despite systematically discovering it hasn't worked for the profiles you've tried so far*. That's not a tautology or an invalid inference—it's a justified challenge to methodological optimism.

VERDICT

The defence fails, and the thesis is dead.

The defence tries to reframe the thesis as identifying a "pragmatic inconsistency" or "methodological regress," but this reformulation abandons what made the thesis seem powerful. The original thesis claimed researchers are *committed to* accepting that no functional profile could *ever* confirm a theory. The defence retreats to claiming researchers face *diminishing credibility* for their methodological optimism.

These are completely different claims. The first is a logical commitment that would, if true, show researchers are contradicting themselves. The second is a sociological observation about research program dynamics that researchers can acknowledge without contradiction.

More fatally, the defence's "methodological regress" is exactly how science normally works. Consider immunology: researchers kept discovering that processes they thought required adaptive immunity could be performed by innate immunity (pattern recognition, memory-like responses, specificity). This didn't show that "no functional profile could ever distinguish adaptive from innate immunity"—it showed they were refining their understanding of what the distinction actually is. The progressive refinement the defence describes is *normal scientific progress*, not a fatal commitment.

The thesis claimed to reveal that accepting sophisticated unconscious processing *commits you to* accepting that functional confirmation is impossible. But the defence shows only that it commits you to *continuing to refine your functional criteria*—which is what researchers explicitly say they're doing. There's no hidden commitment, no contradiction, no regress that doesn't apply equally to every scientific research program that refines its concepts.

The thesis is trivial after all, but in a different way than the objection claimed: it's trivially true that if you keep finding your criteria don't work, you have less reason to think new criteria will work. But this is just Bayesian updating, not a devastating commitment. Researchers who continue proposing functional tests after each refinement aren't contradicting themselves—they're betting that the next refinement will succeed where previous ones failed. That bet might be wrong, but it's not incoherent.

The thesis is dead. It either overgeneralizes to all science (if taken as a logical commitment) or reduces to an unremarkable observation about research program dynamics (if taken as the defence suggests).

Below the cut

Scored for promise, never attacked. Raise --attack-top to reach further down.

U1. Consciousness theories are actually testable, but only through second-person methods where the theorist undergoes systematic phenomenological training and first-personally verifies the purported structures—making consciousness science methodologically closer to pure mathematics than to physics.

Scores
promise 4/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — Both sides of the debate about whether consciousness science is possible: eliminativists who say it isn't, and neural correlate researchers who say it requires third-person methods
Sketch

The dispute about theory-testing assumes the model is something like physics: third-person observations adjudicate hypotheses about unobservable structures. But consciousness is directly observable—just not third-personally. The real methodological model should be pure mathematics, where claims about abstract structures are verified through trained first-person investigation of those structures. Just as you cannot test claims about higher-dimensional topology without learning the mathematics, you cannot test claims about phenomenal structures without learning to attend to your experience in the theoretically-specified way. IIT, HOT, GWT make different predictions about discoverable first-person structures (integration vs. higher-order awareness vs. global availability). The key premise is that intersubjective verification through trained phenomenological practice is as rigorous as mathematical proof verification, making the science empirical but not third-person.

Appraiser (blind)
Genuinely distinctive methodological proposal; mathematics analogy is fresh and the phenomenological training requirement would reframe the field.

U2. The real barrier to testing consciousness theories is not empirical but mathematical: we lack formal tools to compare information-theoretic, representational, and dynamical-systems explanations on a common scale, which means we cannot tell whether theories make the same predictions in different vocabularies or genuinely different predictions.

Scores
promise 4/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — Both experimentalists who think more data will decide the issue and philosophers who think the problem is that consciousness is empirically inaccessible
Sketch

Methodologists assume the problem is getting better data, but consider: IIT makes predictions in terms of phi (integrated information), GWT in terms of ignition patterns, predictive processing in terms of prediction error minimization. Even when we have all the neural data, we cannot tell whether 'high phi' and 'global ignition' are picking out the same phenomenon described differently or distinct phenomena that happen to correlate. The key premise: without translation functions between these mathematical frameworks, we cannot determine whether apparent theoretical disagreements are substantive or merely notational. This is a counterexample to the widespread assumption that empirical underdetermination is the problem—actually, we have mathematical incommensurability. What we need is not better experiments but better formal tools for relating different kinds of theoretical quantities.

Appraiser (blind)
Shifts focus from empirical to mathematical incommensurability in a novel way; identifies a concrete gap (translation functions) that's underexplored.

U3. Any empirical test of consciousness theories requires that we already know whether the test subject is conscious, making the enterprise circular unless we can find cases where we're genuinely uncertain.

Scores
promise 4/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Neuroscientific approaches that assume we can use paradigm cases (like anesthesia studies) to test theories
Sketch

Consider how we'd test IIT versus Global Workspace Theory: we'd look at neural signatures during conscious versus unconscious states. But this requires already knowing which states are conscious. If we use self-report, we've assumed linguistic creatures can report consciousness accurately. If we use behavior, we've assumed consciousness correlates with certain behaviors. The only way forward is to find cases where we're genuinely uncertain whether consciousness is present (locked-in patients, unresponsive patients, or novel systems) and see which theory's predictions match our eventual verdict. But this commits us to the view that empirical progress is possible only at the boundaries of our existing concept, not in its core. The key premise is that empirical confirmation requires independent access to both the predictor (neural activity) and the predicted (consciousness), but for consciousness we lack independent access.

Appraiser (blind)
Identifies a genuine methodological circularity that could reframe how we understand empirical progress in consciousness science, though the boundary-cases solution may be too narrow.

U4. The criterion dispute about consciousness theories can only be resolved by recognizing that different theories are actually answering different questions about distinct explanatory targets that we've mistakenly bundled under one concept.

Scores
promise 4/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared assumption that theories like IIT, GWT, and HOT are genuine competitors about the same explanandum
Sketch

The argument would show that what look like competing empirical criteria (neural correlates, functional organization, information integration) actually track different phenomena: reportability, behavioral flexibility, and subjective presence. The key premise is that these come apart in cases like blindsight, anesthesia awareness, and split-brain patients in ways that suggest they're measuring different things. Current debates assume theories compete to explain 'consciousness,' but they're actually compatible accounts of distinct but related capacities. The reframing dissolves the criterion problem by showing there's no single right answer because there's no single question.

Appraiser (blind)
Dissolving apparent competition by revealing distinct explanatory targets is a powerful move if the empirical dissociations can be demonstrated convincingly.

U5. Accepting that consciousness theories can be tested empirically commits you to eliminativism about phenomenal consciousness, because empirical testability requires public accessibility that phenomenal properties by definition lack.

Scores
promise 4/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Researchers who think consciousness science can vindicate both scientific methodology and traditional phenomenal realism
Sketch

The argument would show that treating consciousness theories as empirically testable requires treating consciousness as occupying a functional role that could be multiply realized and publicly verified. But phenomenal consciousness is traditionally defined precisely by its intrinsic, private, qualitative character—features that make it empirically inaccessible. The key premise is that you cannot both claim phenomenal properties are irreducibly subjective (only accessible from first-person perspective) AND claim we can empirically test which physical systems have them. Either consciousness is whatever plays a certain empirically identifiable functional role (making traditional phenomenal consciousness a myth), or it's genuinely phenomenal but empirically untestable.

Appraiser (blind)
Forces a stark choice between empirical testability and phenomenal realism that could clarify what's at stake, though the dilemma may admit more positions than presented.

U6. The philosophical zombie argument inadvertently proves that consciousness theories are empirically indistinguishable: if p-zombies are conceivable, then any evidence that confirms theory T for conscious beings would equally confirm T for p-zombies.

Scores
promise 4/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Philosophers and scientists who both (a) think p-zombies are conceivable and (b) believe empirical evidence can discriminate between consciousness theories.
Sketch

If p-zombies are conceivable (as many philosophers grant for sake of argument), then there exists a possible world physically identical to ours but lacking consciousness. In that world, whatever empirical evidence we gather here (neural correlates, behavioral reports, information integration measures) would be identical. Therefore, any evidence-based argument for a consciousness theory in our world would be equally compelling in the p-zombie world—but wrongly so, since ex hypothesi there's no consciousness there. This means proponents of conceivable p-zombies are committed to accepting that empirical evidence cannot adjudicate consciousness theories. The only escape is denying p-zombie conceivability, but many consciousness researchers want to remain neutral on this metaphysical question.

Appraiser (blind)
Cleverly turns the zombie argument against empirical testability in a way that forces zombie-sympathizers to confront an uncomfortable commitment.

U7. Consciousness theories are empirically testable right now, but only through their predictions about when conscious systems will deny having certain experiences.

Scores
promise 4/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared assumption across the debate that empirical testability requires verifying the presence of consciousness rather than its reported absence
Sketch

The testability problem assumes we need to verify positive reports of consciousness, but we've overlooked that theories make differential predictions about when systems will report absence or confusion about experience. Higher-order theories predict metacognitive failures should eliminate reportability of experiences that still exist; first-order theories predict no such dissociation. Global workspace theories predict that unbroadcast information cannot be reported even if processed; recurrent processing theories make different predictions about reportability during masking. The key premise is that negative reports and performance dissociations constrain theory space as much as positive reports, and we've been ignoring half the data by focusing only on what systems claim to experience rather than what they claim not to experience or cannot report despite processing.

Appraiser (blind)
Genuinely constructive proposal that negative evidence has been underutilized; could shift experimental focus if the differential predictions hold up.

U8. The consciousness theory debate is actually unstable because testability demands converge on illusionism—any theory genuinely constrained by third-person evidence must explain why consciousness seems to exist rather than why it exists.

Scores
promise 4/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Realist consciousness theorists who think Global Workspace, Higher-Order Thought, or Recurrent Processing theories can be empirically confirmed while preserving real consciousness
Sketch

Realists about consciousness want theories tested by neural correlates, behavior, and cognitive architecture. But these only constrain theories of how consciousness seems to subjects and what functional roles drive reports about consciousness. The very demand for empirical constraint forces theorists to explain the cognitive mechanisms producing consciousness-claims and consciousness-seemings. Once you've explained those mechanisms completely (as an empirically adequate theory must), positing actual consciousness becomes explanatorily idle. The key premise is that empirical adequacy requires explaining the full causal chain from stimulus to report, and once that explanation is complete, consciousness itself drops out as epiphenomenal or eliminable. Therefore, pursuing empirically testable theories of consciousness leads inexorably to illusionism, whether theorists realize it or not.

Appraiser (blind)
Argues that empirical constraint itself drives toward illusionism in a way that could expose hidden commitments, though the inference may be resistible.

U9. All proposed empirical tests of consciousness theories actually presuppose a first-person/third-person bridge principle that itself depends on which theory is correct, making the testing question circular.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Proponents of empirical methods in consciousness science who believe we can straightforwardly test competing theories
Sketch

Consider any proposed test: adversarial collaboration, neural dissociation studies, predictions about unusual cases. Each requires assuming some principle about when subjective reports reliably track phenomenology, or when neural patterns reliably indicate experience. But these bridge principles are themselves theory-laden. IIT says experience occurs where phi is high even without reports; GWT says reportability is constitutive; HOT says experience requires higher-order representation of the state. The circularity isn't vicious, but it means empirical testing alone cannot adjudicate between theories—we need to first resolve the bridge principle question, which requires non-empirical philosophical argument about the nature of the evidence relation itself.

Appraiser (blind)
Bridge-principle circularity is real but widely recognized; doesn't offer novel way forward.

U10. The right question is not 'which theory is true' but 'at what grain of analysis does consciousness admit of a unified theory,' and the answer may be 'none'—different theories may capture real patterns at incompatible scales.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared assumption across the debate that consciousness theories are competing explanations of the same explanandum
Sketch

We assume consciousness is a natural kind with a unified explanation, but this may be a category error. Consider how 'computation' works: there are gate-level, algorithm-level, and functional-level descriptions that don't reduce to each other but aren't competing either. Similarly, phenomenal concepts might pick out patterns that cohere at the millisecond timescale (GWT), others at the structural level (IIT), others at the representational level (HOT). The theories aren't false—they're addressing different grains of the phenomenon. The error is asking 'which is right' rather than 'what is the cross-scale relationship.' This means the epistemological question dissolves: we don't adjudicate between them; we map their domains and bridge principles.

Appraiser (blind)
The grain-of-analysis point is interesting but the multi-level computation analogy is familiar; publishable but unlikely to shift the debate.

U11. The position that we should run adversarial collaborations to test consciousness theories is unstable: it collapses either into stipulating one theory's evidential standards as canonical or into an unworkable pluralism where nothing counts as a definitive test.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Proponents of adversarial collaboration methodology like Dehaene and Tononi who assume we can test theories while remaining neutral about evidential standards
Sketch

Adversarial collaborations require pre-agreed success conditions. But in consciousness, theories disagree about what counts as evidence. If we stipulate behavioral report as the gold standard, we've already favored access-based theories; if we privilege neural markers, we've favored certain neural theories. The collaborator might respond: 'We'll negotiate evidence standards case by case.' But then each 'test' just reveals whether both theories happen to agree about this case—it doesn't adjudicate the deeper disagreement. The position tries to remain neutral about evidential standards while running empirical tests, but you can't do both: you either impose standards (thus begging the question) or accept all standards (thus testing nothing). The apparently moderate methodological stance is actually unstable.

Appraiser (blind)
Adversarial collaboration critique is sharp but the dilemma may be resolvable; doesn't definitively close the door.

U12. The fact that consciousness theories make identical predictions about first-person reports proves they are addressing different explananda, not that they are empirically equivalent.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The widespread view that consciousness theories face an empirical underdetermination problem (Chalmers, Dennett, Schwitzgebel, etc.)
Sketch

Most philosophers treat convergence on behavioral/report predictions as showing consciousness theories are empirically indistinguishable. But this reasoning assumes that first-person reports are theory-neutral data that any correct theory must accommodate. Instead, what counts as the relevant first-person datum is itself theory-laden: a higher-order theory treats 'I am in pain' as data about meta-representations, while first-order theories treat it as data about pain states plus language. The theories predict the same utterances while addressing different questions about what those utterances are evidence of. This explains why no amount of report-based testing resolves the debate—the theories slice up the explanandum space differently.

Appraiser (blind)
The theory-ladenness of data is a recognized issue; this application is competent but the basic move is familiar from philosophy of science.

U13. Testing consciousness theories requires accepting that some conscious experiences are undetectable in principle, which means the demand for empirical testability is self-undermining.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Philosophers demanding empirical criteria for theory choice (Schwitzgebel, Irvine, Farrell) and consciousness science programs claiming measurable signatures
Sketch

Any empirically adequate consciousness theory must account for cases like locked-in syndrome, anesthesia awareness, and potential AI consciousness—cases where we worry about consciousness existing without behavioral markers. This means the theory must allow that consciousness could be present without any evidence we can access. But if consciousness can exist without evidence, then any theory compatible with all our evidence might be false (consciousness might be distributed differently than the theory predicts). Therefore, the very condition that makes a consciousness theory adequate (accounting for evidence-transcendent consciousness) is what makes it untestable. The demand for testable consciousness theories collapses because testability requires evidence-response coupling that consciousness cases specifically problematize.

Appraiser (blind)
Points to a real tension between testability and accounting for undetectable consciousness, though the self-undermining claim may overreach.

U14. Theories of consciousness make incompatible predictions about what subjects themselves can report, and these reportability differences are empirically testable even if the underlying phenomenology isn't directly accessible.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — The shared pessimism among both consciousness scientists and philosophers that theories are empirically underdetermined because we can't access phenomenology directly
Sketch

The argument distinguishes between testing phenomenal character directly (impossible) and testing what structural features of phenomenology subjects can reliably report. For instance, Integrated Information Theory predicts subjects should report discrete phenomenal 'atoms' that can't be further decomposed, while Global Workspace Theory predicts subjects should report a unified broadcast state. Higher-order theories predict subjects can't reliably distinguish between having an experience and having a thought about that experience, while first-order theories predict this distinction should be reportable. The key premise is that different consciousness theories make different commitments about what gets encoded in a reportable format, not just about what's phenomenally present.

Appraiser (blind)
Offers a concrete empirical approach via reportability differences, competent and publishable but incremental rather than transformative.

U15. The only empirically tractable approach to consciousness theories is reverse: instead of testing which theory is true, we should identify which interventions reliably produce or eliminate consciousness, then define consciousness ostensively as whatever those interventions affect.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The standard theory-first methodology where we propose definitions/theories of consciousness and then seek evidence for them
Sketch

Rather than starting with theories and looking for confirming evidence, we should start with robust interventions we can all agree affect consciousness: general anesthesia reliably eliminates it, waking up reliably restores it, certain brain stimulations reliably produce specific experiences. We then say: consciousness is whatever property these interventions manipulate. This gives us an ostensive definition grounded in causal handles rather than phenomenal or functional definitions. Theories become valuable insofar as they predict new interventions or explain why our existing interventions work. The key premise is that we have more agreement about what causally affects consciousness than about what consciousness is, so we should build up from the former rather than down from the latter.

Appraiser (blind)
The interventionist/ostensive approach is methodologically interesting and practical but not conceptually revolutionary.

U16. The search for consciousness's neural correlates systematically confuses the detection problem with the constitution problem, and solving the former cannot even in principle constrain answers to the latter.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — The mainstream consciousness science paradigm that treats NCC-discovery as theory-constraining (Crick & Koch's original program and its descendants)
Sketch

We conflate two questions: what allows us to detect/attribute consciousness (detection) versus what consciousness is made of (constitution). Finding NCCs tells us what reliably indicates consciousness in creatures we've already decided are conscious, but this is circular—we can't use detection tools to answer constitution questions. An analogy: discovering that smoke detects fire doesn't tell us what fire is constituted by. The entire research program of finding 'the NCC' assumes that whatever correlates most tightly with consciousness must constitute it, but this assumes away the hard problem. We could have perfect detection (always correctly identifying conscious states from neural activity) while remaining completely ignorant of what makes those states conscious. The programs diverge completely: detection could be solved pragmatically/behaviorally while constitution remains metaphysically open.

Appraiser (blind)
The detection/constitution distinction is important and the argument is clear, but this ground has been covered in discussions of the hard problem.

U17. The methodological assumption that simpler conscious creatures provide evidential constraint on theories of human consciousness reverses the actual epistemic order: we can only identify which creatures are conscious by first having the correct theory.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The methodological assumption underlying comparative consciousness studies and 'minimal consciousness' research programs
Sketch

Consciousness research typically assumes we can test theories by examining simpler systems (insects, organoids, minimal networks) where we can track all the variables. But we face a prior question: which of these systems are conscious? The standard move is to say the theory tells us—if the system satisfies IIT/GWT/etc., it's conscious. But this means we're not testing the theory against independent evidence; we're using the theory to generate the evidence pool. The apparent methodological progression from simple to complex actually disguises a conceptual circle. We can only treat simple systems as evidential if we already know which ones are conscious, but we need a theory to tell us that. The dispute is thus not straightforwardly empirical but concerns which theoretical virtues (simplicity, conservativeness, fit with intuition) should govern theory-choice in the absence of independent empirical constraint. We've mistaken a conceptual/pragmatic choice for an empirical discovery.

Appraiser (blind)
The circularity in using simple systems is real but the point about epistemic order has been made in debates about animal consciousness.

U18. Accepting that unconscious processing can be arbitrarily sophisticated commits you to the view that no third-person functional or neural evidence could ever distinguish conscious from unconscious processing, only first-person reports can—but this makes consciousness theories unfalsifiable.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Theorists like Dehaene and Tononi who both accept sophisticated unconscious processing and claim their theories are empirically testable
Sketch

Many consciousness researchers accept that unconscious processes can be highly complex, perform elaborate computations, and even exhibit integration and global broadcasting (evidenced by blindsight, unconscious priming, etc.). The argument shows that if we grant this—if there's no computational or functional operation that's necessarily conscious—then any third-person evidence (neural patterns, behavioral capacities, information integration) is compatible with both conscious and unconscious processing. The key premise is that this commits theorists to relying exclusively on first-person reports to determine what was conscious, but reports themselves are just another functional capacity. This means the only real evidence becomes subjects saying they were conscious, which makes the theory unfalsifiable: if the predicted neural signature appears without report, we say the subject was unconscious; if it appears with report, we say they were conscious. The theory can't be wrong because the only arbiter is the explanandum itself.

Appraiser (blind)
Identifies a genuine problem about sophisticated unconscious processing undermining third-person evidence, though the unfalsifiability conclusion may be too strong.

U19. The apparent empirical dispute between consciousness theories conceals a deeper disagreement about whether consciousness is metaphysically determinate—most consciousness theories presuppose determinacy, but testing them actually requires assuming indeterminacy.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — Both sides of debates about consciousness in non-paradigm cases (AI, animals, organoids) who assume there's a fact of the matter while disagreeing about what it is
Sketch

The argument draws a distinction between theories that presuppose consciousness has sharp boundaries (either something is conscious or it isn't) and the methodology required to test these theories, which actually requires treating consciousness as coming in degrees or being vague. When we design experiments, we need graded measures, probability distributions, and statistical thresholds—we're forced to ask 'how conscious' something is or 'what's the probability of consciousness.' The key premise is that this isn't just methodological convenience but reveals something mandatory: any testable theory needs to make graded predictions to be statistically assessable. But most theories (IIT with its phi values aside) are formulated as categorical claims about the presence/absence of consciousness. This means testing them requires translating categorical claims into graded ones, and there's no principled way to do this translation. The metaphysical assumption of determinacy makes theories empirically intractable.

Appraiser (blind)
The determinacy/gradedness tension is real and underexplored, but the argument needs tightening about whether this is truly mandatory.

U20. The search for tests between consciousness theories fails because it targets the wrong asymmetry: the real empirical challenge isn't explaining why some processes are conscious while others aren't, but why consciousness feels like it has features (unity, subjectivity, phenomenal properties) that no physical process exhibits.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The shared assumption across consciousness science that the hard problem can be dissolved or addressed by identifying distinctive functional or neural signatures
Sketch

Current theory-testing methodology focuses on identifying the neural correlates or functional properties that distinguish conscious from unconscious processing—essentially treating consciousness as a discriminable state among others. The argument provides a counterexample to the implicit premise that this is what needs explaining: even if we perfectly predicted which processes correlate with consciousness reports, we wouldn't have explained why those processes feel unified, private, or qualitative. The key premise is that every current consciousness theory treats certain features of experience (unity, subjectivity, qualia) as if they were physical or functional properties to be predicted, but these features are actually category mistakes—they're not properties of processes but of perspectives. The real explanatory challenge is the asymmetry between perspectival features and process features, not the asymmetry between conscious and unconscious processes. This means all current theories, despite their differences, share a mistaken assumption about what makes consciousness puzzling.

Appraiser (blind)
Distinguishes correlate-finding from explaining phenomenal features, but the hard problem framing is well-trodden and the 'category mistake' move needs more development to advance debate.

U21. Consciousness theory disputes are irresolvable because they equivocate between creature consciousness (what systems are conscious) and state consciousness (what makes particular states conscious), and any evidence for one interpretation is compatible with rival views on the other interpretation.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — Theorists who frame IIT, GWT, HOT, and recurrent processing theories as competing explanations of the same phenomenon
Sketch

Current debates treat consciousness theories as competing answers to a single question, but they're actually answering different questions that get conflated. IIT primarily identifies which systems are conscious (creature-level) by measuring integrated information, while Higher-Order Thought theory primarily explains what makes particular mental states conscious (state-level) by their meta-representational properties. When we try to test these theories, evidence that seems decisive at one level is reinterpretable at the other: neural correlates of reportable states (supporting HOT) don't tell us which systems have states to report about (IIT's question), while measures of system integration (supporting IIT) don't tell us which of that system's states are conscious (HOT's question). The key premise is that creature and state consciousness are logically independent—a system could integrate information without its states being meta-represented, or meta-represent states without being an integrated system.

Appraiser (blind)
The creature/state distinction is recognized in the literature; showing evidential reinterpretability is useful but won't fundamentally reorient the debate.

U22. The falsifiability of consciousness theories requires that we could be wrong about our own consciousness, but accepting this possibility undermines the Cartesian certainty that grounds the explanandum, creating a methodological paradox where consciousness science must simultaneously affirm and deny first-person authority.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The methodological assumption shared by Chalmers, Dennett, and empirical consciousness researchers that we can maintain both the reality/certainty of consciousness and the empirical testability of theories about it
Sketch

Scientists insist consciousness theories must be empirically testable—that neural/computational evidence could show IIT or GWT is wrong. But testability requires that our consciousness attributions could be mistaken, including about ourselves. Yet consciousness science treats first-person reports as the gold standard precisely because of their supposed incorrigibility: I cannot be wrong that I am conscious now. This creates a counterexample to the shared assumption that we can have both scientific testability and privileged first-person access. If I could be wrong about being conscious (required for falsifiability), then we lose the secure explanandum that justifies preferring consciousness theories over eliminativism. If I cannot be wrong (preserving the explanandum), then theories accommodating my reports are unfalsifiable. The key premise is that the asymmetry between first- and third-person access that makes consciousness scientifically interesting is the same asymmetry that makes consciousness theories empirically underdetermined.

Appraiser (blind)
First-person authority paradox is real but the field has various responses; doesn't definitively show instability.

U23. The demand for empirical testability of consciousness theories presupposes epiphenomenalism, which most consciousness researchers explicitly reject.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — The methodological consensus in consciousness science that treats neural correlates as the primary empirical constraint, shared by IIT, GWT, HOT, and other competing theories
Sketch

The standard methodology asks 'what observable predictions distinguish theory A from theory B?' But if consciousness has causal powers (non-epiphenomenalism), then different theories of what consciousness IS should predict different behaviors, not just different neural correlates. The fact that we treat 'same behavior, different neural substrate' as a meaningful test scenario reveals we're tacitly assuming consciousness doesn't affect behavior—otherwise changing what consciousness is would change what the system does. This means either: (1) we abandon the causal efficacy of consciousness, (2) we admit current testing methods are fundamentally misconceived, or (3) we accept that theories making identical behavioral predictions but differing only in 'what it's like' are genuinely empirically indistinguishable—which would make consciousness theory selection a matter of theoretical virtues, not empirical test.

Appraiser (blind)
Points to an inconsistency between causal efficacy commitments and testing practices, but the epiphenomenalism worry is somewhat familiar.

U24. The criterion 'theory T is right if T's predicted neural correlates match actual neural correlates' assumes consciousness is realized by but not reducible to neural states—a metaphysical position that rules out both strong reductionism and strong emergentism before empirical testing begins.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — The neural correlates of consciousness (NCC) research program, treated as theory-neutral by researchers across different theoretical commitments including reductionists like Crick and Emergentists like Searle
Sketch

If consciousness just IS certain neural activity (strong reductionism), then finding that consciousness correlates with those states is trivial—we've discovered consciousness correlates with itself. The correlation is only informative if consciousness is something over and above the neural correlate. But if consciousness strongly emerges from neural states with novel causal powers, then the neural correlate is insufficient—we need to test the emergent properties directly, not just their base. The neural-correlate methodology only makes sense on a specific middle position: realization/constitution without reduction. This commits researchers to a metaphysical view (nonreductive physicalism) that's highly controversial and that many theories explicitly reject. Either the methodology is metaphysically biased, or finding neural correlates can't actually confirm theories because it's neutral between incompatible metaphysical pictures.

Appraiser (blind)
Correctly identifies that neural-correlate methodology presupposes nonreductive physicalism, but this observation alone won't change research practice.

U25. The 'hard problem' is not a problem about consciousness but a problem about theory-selection criteria: we have no principled way to decide whether explanatory gaps count as theory failures or merely as limits of a certain kind of explanation.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — The framing of the hard problem as a metaphysical question about the relationship between physical and phenomenal properties, shared across the Chalmers-Dennett-Frankish debates
Sketch

Current debate treats the hard problem as metaphysical: can physical facts explain phenomenal facts? But the real action is epistemological: should an adequate theory of consciousness leave no explanatory gap, or is it sufficient that a theory correctly predicts/systematizes the phenomena even if it doesn't make them intuitively 'make sense'? Physics doesn't explain why mass curves spacetime in a way that removes all sense of mystery, yet we accept GTR. The hard problem dissolves into a question about what counts as adequate explanation. This reclassifies the positions: type-A materialists think gap-closure is required and achievable; type-B materialists think it's not required (theories can be right despite gaps); illusionists think it's required but only achievable by denying the phenomena; dualists think it's required but impossible physically. The dispute isn't about consciousness's nature but about epistemic standards for theory adequacy, which suggests we need philosophy of science to settle consciousness science—not neuroscience or metaphysics.

Appraiser (blind)
Reclassifying hard problem as about explanatory standards is valuable but not entirely novel; the typology is useful.

U26. Treating substrate-independence as a criterion for evaluating consciousness theories is unstable: it collapses into either question-begging functionalism or abandons the possibility of empirical theory adjudication entirely.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Computational and functionalist approaches that use substrate-independence as a criterion for theory evaluation
Sketch

Many consciousness researchers use substrate-independence as a theoretical virtue: good theories shouldn't privilege biological neurons. But this criterion either already assumes functionalism is true (making it question-begging against biological theories) or it's meant as a methodological constraint independent of truth. If the latter, then on what grounds? The only non-question-begging justification would be epistemic: we have better empirical access to functional organization than to intrinsic physical properties. But this justification proves too much—it makes the choice of consciousness theory turn entirely on our epistemic limitations rather than on what consciousness actually is, undermining the very idea that empirical evidence tracks truth about consciousness. The instability is that substrate-independence is either substantive (and question-begging) or methodological (and undermines empirical realism).

Appraiser (blind)
Exposes instability in substrate-independence as a criterion, but the dilemma needs sharper development to force methodological change.

U27. Any theory that claims to explain consciousness rather than merely correlate with it commits you to pan-experientialism, because the only way to avoid brute psychophysical laws is to make consciousness constitutively built into the physical base.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Functionalist theories (GWT, HOT) that claim explanatory status while rejecting panpsychism, and physicalists who reject both panpsychism and explanatory gaps
Sketch

Genuine explanation requires showing how the explanandum follows from more basic facts. For consciousness, we either accept brute bridging laws (physicalism with nomological danglers) or we make experience constitutive of the physical. The key premise: if neural activity N generates experience E as a further fact about N, that's a brute correlation, not an explanation. The only way N could genuinely explain E is if E is part of what N fundamentally is—if the intrinsic nature of the physical is itself experiential. This means functionalists like Dennett and GWT theorists who claim their view explains consciousness (not just correlates) are unwittingly committed to a Russellian pan-experientialism. Those who avoid this commitment (like Chalmers) must admit they're only mapping correlations, not explaining.

Appraiser (blind)
The argument that genuine explanation requires pan-experientialism is provocative but the explanation/correlation distinction needs more careful handling.

U28. First-person and third-person methods cannot both be reliable guides to consciousness because they track different natural kinds: first-person methods track phenomenality while third-person methods track cognitive access, and assuming these coincide is what makes consciousness seem unmeasurable.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — Integrated research programs assuming consciousness is unitary (like adversarial collaboration frameworks) and theorists who treat access/phenomenal distinction as merely conceptual rather than marking real kinds
Sketch

The measurability problem assumes consciousness is one thing that both introspection and neuroscience should converge on. But introspective reports reliably track what Block calls phenomenal consciousness (what it's like), while neuroscience and behavioral tests reliably track access consciousness (availability for report and control). These come apart in cases like inattentional blindness, blindsight, and potentially during anesthesia. The current debate treats divergences between methods as measurement error, but this reclassifies them: they're measuring different real phenomena. Neither method is 'wrong'—they're correctly tracking distinct kinds. This means IIT is a theory of phenomenal consciousness, GWT is a theory of access consciousness, and the debate about which is 'right' rests on failing to see they're theories of different explananda.

Appraiser (blind)
Access/phenomenal tracking different kinds is known; needs more to show this dissolves rather than deepens the problem.

U29. Consciousness theories are untestable not because we lack good methods, but because researchers systematically confuse what would confirm their theory with what would motivate someone to accept it.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — The shared assumption across Global Workspace Theory, IIT, Higher-Order Thought theories, etc. that we just need better empirical methods or predictions
Sketch

The argument distinguishes between evidential confirmation and dialectical persuasion. When consciousness researchers propose tests, they typically specify what would make them accept theory X over theory Y. But this conflates two questions: what evidence bears on X's truth, versus what would rationally compel X's opponents to switch positions. For instance, IIT proponents might say 'finding consciousness in cerebellum would refute IIT,' but this just identifies what would make them abandon IIT—not what actually tests it. The key premise is that in mature sciences, these come apart: we can specify evidence that would confirm heliocentrism even for someone who had independent reasons to resist it. Consciousness research lacks this because the theories themselves determine what counts as the relevant evidence.

Appraiser (blind)
Distinguishing confirmation from persuasion is conceptually useful but the diagnosis may be too general to drive specific methodological reforms.

U30. First-person report methodology forces consciousness theories into a self-sealing structure: if reports match predictions, the theory is confirmed; if reports diverge from predictions, subjects are confabulating or misunderstanding their experience, making empirical falsification logically impossible.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The methodological assumption shared across consciousness research that combining third-person measures with first-person reports can produce empirically testable theories
Sketch

This targets the shared methodological assumption that first-person reports provide neutral evidence. The argument presents cases from actual consciousness research where theories made predictions about what subjects should experience, subjects reported otherwise, and researchers concluded the subjects were wrong about their own experiences rather than that the theory was wrong. For example, when change blindness research predicted subjects shouldn't be conscious of changing features (given attention theories), but subjects reported noticing them, researchers posited 'partial awareness' or 'sensory memory not real consciousness.' The key premise is that this move is always available: any mismatch between predicted and reported experience can be explained as subjects' mischaracterization rather than theory failure. Since this interpretive flexibility is built into the methodology, no first-person report can actually falsify consciousness theories. But first-person reports are our primary evidence. Therefore, the theories are empirically unfalsifiable.

Appraiser (blind)
Self-sealing structure is concerning but examples need careful examination; may be resolvable with better protocols.

U31. Theories of consciousness are empirically testable only if they make predictions about when consciousness ceases to exist, not just about which systems possess it.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — The common methodological assumption shared by Chalmers, Dennett, and others that empirical testing primarily involves checking whether systems with certain functional profiles exhibit consciousness
Sketch

Current methodological discussions focus on which systems are conscious (integrated information, global workspace, higher-order theories all make claims about this). But there's an asymmetry: we can never be certain a system IS conscious (other minds problem remains), but we could potentially be certain a system ISN'T conscious if we had a theory that predicted consciousness's absence and we could verify no relevant capacities remain. The argument turns on the key premise that falsification requires not just failed positive predictions but successful negative predictions. This means the real empirical bite comes from theories that specify necessary conditions and predict their absence, not from theories that merely expand the circle of conscious beings.

Appraiser (blind)
Valid methodological point about falsification asymmetry, but the insight about negative predictions is somewhat familiar from philosophy of science generally.

U32. Accepting that theories of consciousness can be confirmed by studying consciousness in other possible worlds commits one to either modal rationalism or to the view that consciousness science cannot be naturalistic.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Philosophers who simultaneously embrace naturalistic methodology while relying on conceivability arguments about consciousness
Sketch

Many philosophers (Chalmers, Frankish, etc.) appeal to conceivability arguments, zombie thought experiments, and inverted qualia scenarios—all essentially claims about what's possible in other worlds. But confirming what's possible in other worlds requires either (a) a priori rational insight into modal facts (modal rationalism), which most naturalists reject, or (b) empirical access to other possible worlds, which is impossible. The argument's key premise is that there's no third option: you can't be both a naturalist (rejecting a priori metaphysics) and treat modal claims as evidentially relevant to consciousness without contradiction.

Appraiser (blind)
Competent dilemma about modal epistemology, but this territory is well-trodden in debates about conceivability arguments and naturalism.

U33. Any theory of consciousness that relies on 'access consciousness' to validate its claims about 'phenomenal consciousness' is methodologically unstable because it must either collapse the distinction or abandon empirical testability.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Theorists like Block, Rosenthal, and others who maintain the access/phenomenal distinction while also claiming their theories are empirically testable
Sketch

Theories regularly use the access/phenomenal distinction (Block) to defend themselves: they claim to be about phenomenal consciousness but test their theories using access consciousness (reports, discrimination, memory). However, if access reliably tracks phenomenal consciousness, the distinction does no work—why posit two properties that perfectly covary? But if access doesn't reliably track phenomenal consciousness, then all our 'evidence' for theories is actually evidence about something else, making the theories empirically untestable. The key premise is that there's no stable middle ground: either the distinction matters (and undermines testability) or it doesn't matter (and the distinction is idle).

Appraiser (blind)
Access/phenomenal instability is real but the dilemma may have middle ground; needs tighter argument.
