U34. The project of testing consciousness theories presupposes that consciousness has a uniform nature across cases, but our only evidence for this presupposition would come from already having confirmed a theory—making the entire enterprise question-begging.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The shared methodological assumption across all theorists (IIT, GWT, HOT, etc.) that they're seeking THE theory of consciousness rather than theories of consciousness-kinds
Sketch

Every proposed test assumes that if theory T is right, it's right for all instances of consciousness—my visual experiences, your pain, a bat's echolocation experience all have the same underlying nature. But what if consciousness is like jade—multiple distinct natural kinds that we group under one folk concept? We have no pre-theoretical reason to expect uniformity; the only way to confirm uniformity would be to test and confirm a single theory across cases. But we can't confirm a theory IS correct across cases without already assuming what we need to prove: that there's one thing to be right about. The key premise is that our intuition that consciousness is uniform is just that—an intuition—not something we can know independently of having a confirmed theory.

Appraiser (blind)
The jade analogy is apt and the circularity real, but the uniformity assumption has been questioned before; competent but not groundbreaking.

U35. The demand for empirical criteria to adjudicate between consciousness theories presupposes that consciousness theories are causal-explanatory hypotheses, but most theories of consciousness are actually normative proposals about which physical systems we ought to grant moral status to.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared assumption across Chalmers, Dennett, Koch, Dehaene, and Tononi that consciousness science is continuous with other empirical sciences
Sketch

The argument turns on examining what practitioners actually do when defending consciousness theories versus what they claim to do. When IIT proponents defend phi as the measure of consciousness, their arguments bottom out in intuitions about which systems 'deserve' to be called conscious rather than predictions about behavior or function. Similarly, when GWT theorists exclude feed-forward networks from consciousness, they're not making a discovery but a stipulation. The key premise is that if these were genuinely empirical theories, proponents would be willing to accept empirically absurd conclusions (like IIT's grid being more conscious than humans), but instead they treat such results as reductios. This reveals the theories are actually encoding ethical intuitions about moral considerability while wearing the costume of neuroscience.

Appraiser (blind)
The normative-disguised-as-empirical point has been made; the reductio observation is interesting but not sufficiently developed to be transformative.

U36. The undetectable-zombie scenario is treated as showing that consciousness is empirically inaccessible, but it actually shows the opposite: if zombies are empirically indistinguishable from conscious beings, then our concept of consciousness fails to carve nature at the joints, which means the right theory of consciousness is eliminativism.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — Both dualists like Chalmers who use zombies to argue for non-physical consciousness and physicalists like Frankish who accept zombies are conceivable but think this is unproblematic
Sketch

Philosophers use zombie conceivability to argue that consciousness transcends the physical or that we need something beyond third-person methods. But this gets the dialectic backwards. In every other domain, if our concept allows for empirically indistinguishable cases that supposedly differ in their fundamental nature, we conclude the concept is confused or that the supposed distinction is bogus (phlogiston, vital force, etc.). The key premise is a parity argument: consciousness should not get special epistemic treatment. If zombie scenarios show that consciousness could be absent with no empirical difference, this is a reductio of consciousness-realism, not a reductio of physicalism. The correct response is to eliminate the concept of consciousness in favor of the empirically tractable phenomena (reportability, integration, metacognition, etc.) that we can actually study. This counterexample targets the shared assumption that zombie conceivability poses a problem for physicalist theories rather than for consciousness-talk itself.

Appraiser (blind)
The parity argument is interesting and the reversal clever, but eliminativism as response to zombie arguments has been defended before.

U37. Any theory of consciousness that cannot explain why it cannot be empirically confirmed or disconfirmed is thereby disconfirmed.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Both sides of the physicalism/dualism debate who treat the 'hard problem' as revealing something about consciousness rather than revealing something about their own theories
Sketch

Meta-epistemological helplessness about consciousness is not a neutral datum but a constraint that consciousness theories themselves must explain. If functionalism is true, we should be able to tell whether something is conscious by examining its functional organization—yet functionalists admit we cannot. If phenomenal realism is true, there should be some epistemic story about why private qualia are nevertheless trackable—yet phenomenal realists typically embrace the explanatory gap. The argument turns on this premise: a theory that entails its own unverifiability has thereby provided evidence against itself, because correct theories should predict the epistemic situation we actually find ourselves in. Any adequate theory must explain not just what consciousness IS, but why our epistemic access to it has exactly the structure it does.

Appraiser (blind)
The self-defeat argument is valid and the meta-epistemological demand reasonable, but similar reflexive requirements have been discussed in epistemology of consciousness.

U38. If consciousness is real, then there must be facts about its distribution that are both determinate and unknowable to us; this combination is unstable and collapses either into eliminativism or mysterianism, revealing that moderate physicalism about consciousness is not a stable position.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — The 'moderate physicalist' position held by most consciousness researchers who think consciousness is physical and scientifically tractable while acknowledging we currently cannot solve the hard problem
Sketch

Moderate physicalists (most working consciousness scientists) want to affirm both that consciousness is a real physical phenomenon and that we can study it scientifically. But consider: either facts about consciousness distribution are determinate (there is a fact about whether this fetus/AI/organoid is conscious) or indeterminate. If indeterminate, consciousness is not robustly real—it's more like 'being a heap.' If determinate, either we can know these facts or we cannot. If we can know them, show me the method—but there isn't one forthcoming. If we cannot know them (which is where we are), then we're positing a realm of determinate but unknowable facts, which is the mysterian position moderate physicalists reject. The argument turns on the premise that you cannot coherently claim something is both a determinate physical fact and systematically epistemically inaccessible.

Appraiser (blind)
Determinate-but-unknowable instability is interesting but moderate physicalists have responses; doesn't definitively collapse position.

U39. Any empirical test that could adjudicate between consciousness theories must rely on conscious reports, but accepting conscious reports as evidential commits you to the very functionalist assumptions that non-functionalist theories deny.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Non-functionalist theories like IIT that claim to make testable predictions while denying that consciousness necessarily involves reportability or cognitive access
Sketch

Consider how we'd actually test predictions from IIT versus GNW in practice: we need dependent variables, which inevitably involve subjects reporting experiences or exhibiting report-linked behaviors. But treating these reports as evidence about consciousness requires assuming that certain functional states (being disposed to report, forming metacognitive judgments) are reliably connected to consciousness. This is precisely what non-functionalist theories deny—they claim consciousness could exist without these functional signatures. The argument shows that the experimental methodology itself presupposes functionalism. Non-functionalists face a dilemma: either accept that reports track consciousness (contradicting their theory) or reject reports as evidence (making their theory untestable).

Appraiser (blind)
Correctly identifies that experimental methodology presupposes functionalist assumptions, but this circularity concern has been raised in various forms before.

U40. The position that consciousness science can remain neutral between realist and instrumentalist interpretations of consciousness theories is unstable—it collapses into eliminativism about consciousness as a natural kind.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Methodological agnostics like Crick and Koch's 'astonishing hypothesis' approach and modern consciousness science's claimed methodological naturalism
Sketch

Many consciousness researchers adopt what they see as a safe methodological stance: we can test theories, refine predictions, build models, while remaining neutral about whether consciousness is 'real' or whether theories are tracking an actual phenomenon versus just organizing behavioral/neural data. The argument shows this collapses: if you're genuinely neutral about whether there's a phenomenon being tracked, you cannot justify theory choice on evidential grounds rather than purely pragmatic ones. The moment you say 'theory T is right because the evidence supports it,' you've abandoned neutrality—you're claiming the evidence reveals something about consciousness itself. Maintaining true neutrality means treating consciousness talk as merely instrumental, which is just eliminativism with extra steps. The unstable middle position tries to have empirical discovery without an object of discovery.

Appraiser (blind)
Shows that methodological neutrality collapses into eliminativism; the argument is sound but the instability of instrumentalism is a familiar philosophical point.

U41. First-person experimental protocols (like meditation training studies or Dennett's heterophenomenology) cannot adjudicate consciousness theories because they face an unnoticed calibration problem: we have no theory-neutral way to determine if subjects are becoming better reporters or changing what they're conscious of.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — Both neurophenomenology advocates like Varela and Lutz, and analytical phenomenologists like Bayne who think refined first-person methods can constrain theory
Sketch

Advocates across the spectrum argue that sufficiently sophisticated first-person methods could resolve theoretical disputes—meditators making finer discriminations, subjects trained in phenomenological reflection, etc. This assumes improved first-person methodology gives us better data about consciousness. But this conflates two possibilities: (1) training makes subjects conscious of features that were always present but unreported, versus (2) training changes the structure of consciousness itself. Without a prior theory of consciousness, we cannot distinguish these. Yet both options presuppose answers to the very theoretical questions at issue. If consciousness is HOT-theory-style, training might create new conscious states. If it's IIT-style, training might just improve access to pre-existing integrated information. The calibration problem: our measuring instrument (first-person reports) is necessarily entangled with the phenomenon measured.

Appraiser (blind)
Better-reporter vs. changed-consciousness calibration problem is real but may not be fatal to first-person methods.

U42. Commitment to the possibility of testing consciousness theories requires accepting that consciousness has essential bridge laws connecting it to physical states, which commits you to a priori physicalism—making the empirical testing circular.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — The view held by many consciousness scientists that empirical work is metaphysically neutral and can discover whether physicalism is true
Sketch

Testing theories empirically requires that we can move between evidence (physical/functional states) and conclusions about consciousness. This requires bridge principles: 'when physical state P obtains, consciousness state C obtains.' But these bridges can't themselves be empirically discovered without prior bridges, generating regress. They must be knowable a priori or stipulated. If stipulated, theory choice isn't empirical discovery. If a priori knowable, we've accepted that there are necessary connections between physical and phenomenal—which is a substantive metaphysical claim that rules out logical possibility of zombies, inverted qualia, etc. The argument shows that taking consciousness science as genuinely empirical (not mere stipulation) commits you to rejecting the conceivability arguments before you start. The methodology presupposes physicalism rather than discovering it.

Appraiser (blind)
The bridge law regress is real and the circularity valid, but this is recognizable from debates about psychophysical laws and the explanatory gap.

U43. Any experimental paradigm that could actually distinguish consciousness theories must rely on subjects' reports about their experiences, but relying on reports presupposes a global workspace architecture for accessing and reporting mental states, therefore no experiment can adjudicate between global workspace theory and its rivals without begging the question.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — The methodology of contrastive analysis and neural correlates research that treats itself as theory-neutral
Sketch

The argument starts from the observation that all proposed experimental tests—contrastive analysis, perturbational complexity, no-report paradigms—ultimately rely on either direct reports or behavioral indicators that presuppose reportability-in-principle. Even 'no-report' paradigms use report-independent measures of attention or memory, but these capacities themselves require global availability of information. When we use a subject's report of seeing versus not seeing as our ground truth for consciousness, we're implicitly treating global accessibility as definitive of consciousness. This means experiments aren't neutral arbiters—they're designed in a way that builds in GWT's core claim that consciousness requires global availability. A theory like IIT, which allows for consciousness without access, cannot be confirmed by methods that treat access as the mark of consciousness. The commitment works both ways: if you accept report-based methodology, you've already accepted that consciousness requires the kind of broadcasting GWT describes, making the experimental comparison circular.

Appraiser (blind)
Report-methodology presupposing GWT is concerning but 'no-report' paradigms may provide more independence than claimed.

U44. First-person/third-person asymmetry is not the obstacle to testing consciousness theories; rather, the obstacle is that consciousness theories are unfalsifiable-in-practice because they can always relocate consciousness to whatever neural correlates we fail to manipulate.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — Both mysterian anti-physicalists and confident physicalists who agree that the hard problem is about first/third person asymmetry
Sketch

The standard view is that consciousness is special because of the epistemic gap between first and third-person access. But consider: when we lesion area A and consciousness persists, GWT can say 'the workspace relocated to area B'; when we disrupt feedback connections and consciousness persists, higher-order theory can say 'there are alternative feedback routes'; when we reduce phi and consciousness persists, IIT can say 'we miscalculated which complex had maximal phi'. The key premise is that these moves are no different from how phlogiston theory relocated phlogiston, or how Ptolemaic astronomy added epicycles. The problem isn't metaphysical (subjectivity) but methodological (unfalsifiability). This explains why even materialist consciousness theories remain untestable.

Appraiser (blind)
Competent point about unfalsifiability but the phlogiston/epicycle analogy is familiar and the diagnosis not particularly novel.

U45. We can only test which theory of consciousness is right by testing them on artificial systems where we can build in the relevant mechanisms, but doing so commits us to accepting that any system with those mechanisms is conscious—making the test self-defeating for those who believe consciousness requires biological substrate.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Researchers like Seth or Dehaene who advocate for testing theories on artificial systems while remaining agnostic about machine consciousness
Sketch

Many propose that the way forward is building artificial systems that satisfy different theories' criteria and seeing which ones are conscious. But the test only works if we can determine whether the artificial system is conscious—and if we accept it is conscious when it meets Theory T's criteria, we've already presupposed T is correct. The key premise is sharper though: the only evidence we could have that the artificial system is conscious would be that it satisfies the theoretical criteria, but this makes the test circular. The alternative—requiring biological substrate—means artificial tests can never work. So either the test is circular or biological theories are untestable. Either way, holding that consciousness theories are testable commits you to positions you likely reject.

Appraiser (blind)
Identifies a real tension but the circularity problem with artificial systems has been noted; limited new insight.

U46. The demand for 'getting the extension right' in consciousness science is incoherent because consciousness lacks borderline cases in the right way—instead it has systematic ambiguity about which dimension we're measuring.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — Theorists like Schwitzgebel and Birch who frame theory-choice around correctly handling borderline cases and graded consciousness
Sketch

Philosophers often frame theory-choice as 'which theory correctly classifies borderline cases (foetuses, split-brains, AIs)?' This assumes consciousness has vague boundaries like 'bald.' But these 'borderline cases' aren't dimensionally similar to clear cases but informationally impoverished—we lack access to whether there's something-it's-like because we can't occupy their perspective. The appearance of borderline cases emerges from evidential limitations, not metaphysical vagueness. What looks like 'getting the extension right' is actually choosing which behavioral/functional proxy to privilege when first-person evidence is unavailable. The key premise is that genuine borderline cases would admit of precisification along the same dimension as clear cases, but alleged consciousness borderline cases introduce new dimensions (degrees of integration vs. degrees of access vs. degrees of self-representation).

Appraiser (blind)
Interesting distinction between vagueness and evidential limitation, but unclear how it changes the practical debate about theory-choice.

U47. Higher-order theories and first-order theories are not actually competitors because they solve inverse problems: HOT explains how consciousness becomes epistemically accessible to subjects, while first-order theories explain what makes states have phenomenal character, and both are required for a complete account.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The framing of the HOT vs. first-order debate as a genuine competition, as seen in Lau, Rosenthal, Block, and Lamme
Sketch

The debate treats these as rival explanations of consciousness, with neural evidence meant to crown a winner. But examine their actual explanatory achievements: first-order theories (like recurrent processing) explain why certain neural states have qualitative character—why red looks like *that*. Higher-order theories explain how subjects come to know they're in conscious states—the structure of self-awareness. These are different explananda with a natural dependency order: you need phenomenal character (first-order) before there's anything to be aware of (higher-order). Neural evidence supports both because both are correct about different aspects. The apparent competition emerges from conflating 'what consciousness is' with 'what makes consciousness accessible to introspection.' The key premise is that phenomenal character and epistemic accessibility are distinct explanandum requiring different sufficient conditions.

Appraiser (blind)
HOT/first-order solving different problems is plausible but whether both are 'correct' needs more argument about explanatory completeness.

U48. The reason we cannot adjudicate consciousness theories is that they make the same predictions about all measurable outcomes, but differ only in their metaphysical overlay—what they say consciousness 'is' given identical functional profiles.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The assumption that competing consciousness theories make empirically distinguishable predictions, targeted at researchers who think more data will settle the debate
Sketch

Consider that GNW, HOT, IIT, and recurrent processing theories all predict similar patterns: reportability, integration, attention, specific neural signatures. Their apparent disagreement dissolves when we see they're attaching different metaphysical interpretations to the same functional architecture. GNW says 'global broadcasting IS consciousness,' HOT says 'higher-order representation OF those states is consciousness,' IIT says 'the integrated information realized IN that system is consciousness.' The key premise is that no purely empirical test can distinguish 'consciousness is X' from 'consciousness is Y' when X and Y are reliably co-instantiated. We've mistaken a metaphysical dispute for an empirical one.

Appraiser (blind)
Reasonable claim about metaphysical overlay but doesn't provide clear criteria for distinguishing genuine from merely verbal disputes.

U49. Any test that could distinguish consciousness theories must rely on consciousness-dependent capacities (discrimination, report, memory), but this creates a detection problem: we cannot tell whether a theory fails because it's wrong about consciousness or because consciousness is present but undetectable by the chosen probe.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Non-functionalist theories (especially phenomenal realism and quale-based views) that nonetheless claim empirical testability
Sketch

Suppose we test whether a patient in a particular brain state is conscious by checking for behavioral discrimination. If they fail to discriminate, theories disagree about whether consciousness is absent or merely unexpressed. But every probe we might use—verbal report, motor response, memory formation, attention effects—assumes consciousness can be expressed through that channel. Accepting any test methodology commits you to the claim that consciousness necessarily involves the capacity for that type of expression. The argument shows that theorists who endorse empirical testability are thereby committed to functionalist constraints they often explicitly reject. The key premise: if X is undetectable in principle by all possible probes, X is explanatorily idle.

Appraiser (blind)
The detection problem is real but the argument doesn't sufficiently advance beyond familiar underdetermination worries.

U50. We can only empirically test consciousness theories if consciousness has effects distinguishable from its physical correlates, but the only way to establish such distinguishable effects requires already knowing which theory is correct—making empirical adjudication impossible without prior metaphysical commitment.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — Optimistic empiricists like Dehaene or Tononi who believe their experimental programs can definitively establish their theories
Sketch

Consider testing whether integrated information or global broadcasting is the correlate of consciousness. To determine this empirically, we need cases where they come apart: high integration without broadcasting, or vice versa. But identifying which case involves consciousness requires knowing what consciousness does—its causal profile. If we say 'whichever allows reportability is conscious,' we've presupposed functionalism. If we say 'whichever feels like something,' we've presupposed first-person authority. The key premise: there is no theory-neutral empirical signature of consciousness because what counts as evidence depends on which causal powers you attribute to consciousness, and that attribution is precisely what's in dispute. This counterexamples the widespread assumption that accumulating neural correlates data will eventually force theoretical convergence.

Appraiser (blind)
Identifies circularity in establishing causal profiles but the argument structure is somewhat familiar from theory-ladenness debates.

U51. Theories of consciousness that cannot explain why particular qualia feel the way they do (rather than merely that they exist) are not theories of consciousness at all, but theories of meta-consciousness or self-awareness.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — The widespread assumption that Global Workspace Theory, Integrated Information Theory, Higher-Order Thought theories, etc. are competing theories of the same phenomenon
Sketch

The argument turns on distinguishing between explaining that there is something-it-is-like (which Global Workspace Theory, Higher-Order Thought theories, etc. attempt) and explaining why pain feels painful rather than how sweetness feels. Current 'consciousness' theories systematically address only the former, treating quale-specific character as a dangling input. But explaining phenomenal presence while treating phenomenal character as a brute given is analogous to explaining why we have perceptual beliefs while treating perceptual content as given. Just as we'd reject a 'theory of perception' that only explained that we perceive, not what, we should recognize that current theories address only a meta-level awareness-of-experience, not experience itself. This redraws the map: we lack even candidate theories of consciousness proper.

Appraiser (blind)
Distinguishes meta-consciousness from consciousness proper, but the that/what distinction has been explored; limited additional traction.

U52. Defending any theory of consciousness commits you to explaining why organisms with that mechanism deserve moral consideration, but all current theories make moral status either too liberal (including thermostats) or too restrictive (excluding clear moral patients).

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — All major theories of consciousness, but especially those claiming empirical adequacy (IIT, GWT, Recurrent Processing Theory)
Sketch

The argument turns on forcing proponents to connect their theory to moral status. If consciousness matters morally because it grounds moral patiency, then identifying consciousness with information integration (IIT) commits you to either granting moral status to any integrated system (extremely liberal, including artifacts with no plausible moral standing) or adding ad-hoc restrictions that have nothing to do with the theory itself. Global Workspace theorists face a parallel problem: if global broadcasting suffices, then simple robots with broadcast architectures qualify, which seems wrong; but if you add restrictions (must be biological, must be sufficiently complex), these do the moral work, not the theory. Higher-Order Thought theories exclude animals without meta-representation (possibly very young humans), which conflicts with considered moral judgments. Every theory thus reveals an unnoticed commitment: it must either revise commonsense morality radically or admit that its account of consciousness omits what makes consciousness matter.

Appraiser (blind)
Connects theories to moral status in a way that reveals commitments, but the too-liberal/too-restrictive dilemma is somewhat predictable.

U53. Testing consciousness theories requires determining whether consciousness can be absent when subjects insist it's present, but any evidence we could accept for this would show our concept of consciousness is actually empty.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The shared assumption across theories that consciousness is both essentially subjective (defined by what-it's-like) and empirically investigable as a natural phenomenon
Sketch

The argument targets the shared assumption that we can discover consciousness-mechanism dissociations. Consider: what would convince us that subjects reporting rich visual phenomenology actually have none? Not neural evidence alone—correlations underdetermine which is epiphenomenal. Not behavioral evidence—if they act like it's present, that's exactly what presence predicts. Not conceptual analysis—that begs the question. The only remaining option is privileged first-personal access, but that's exactly what's being questioned (if I'm wrong about phenomenology's presence, first-personal authority fails). So either we can never have evidence for report-phenomenology dissociation (making some theories untestable), or we can have such evidence, but then we've abandoned the idea that consciousness is what-it's-like-for-the-subject (since the subject can be massively wrong about it). Either horn shows that our concept—inherently tied to first-personal authority—doesn't pick out a discoverable natural kind. This counterexample undermines the shared premise that consciousness is both subjectively defined and empirically discoverable.

Appraiser (blind)
Interesting dilemma about report-phenomenology dissociation but doesn't clearly resolve which horn to take or what follows.

U54. Any proposed empirical test for consciousness theories commits you to either behaviourism (if the test is third-personal) or mysterianism (if it requires first-personal access we can't systematically share).

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Methodological optimists like Doerig, Schurger & Herzog who think adversarial collaboration and clever experiments can settle which theory is right
Sketch

Consider what any test would look like. If it's purely third-personal (neuroimaging, behaviour, report-dispositions), then you're committed to consciousness being constituted by whatever the test measures - but that's definitionally behaviourist, reducing consciousness to its outward signs. If instead you insist the test must involve first-personal verification (we each check our own phenomenology against predictions), then there's no way to make this systematic or shared - you've made consciousness empirically inaccessible in the way mysterians claim. The key premise is that this exhausts the options: tests are either third-personal-and-therefore-behaviourist or first-personal-and-therefore-unshareable. Most methodologists want neither behaviourism nor mysterianism, but their proposals for testing consciousness theories collapse into one or the other. This means the quest for empirical tests rests on an unstable methodological foundation.

Appraiser (blind)
Behaviorism-or-mysterianism dilemma is stark but may be false dichotomy; needs to rule out middle positions more carefully.

U55. Consciousness theories are empirically distinguishable in principle but not in practice, because the only cases where they make different predictions are cases where we have no independent access to whether consciousness is present.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — Both optimists who think we'll find the right test and pessimists who think theories are empirically equivalent - they're both wrong because they miss the distinction between distinguishable and decidable
Sketch

Draw a distinction between 'in-principle empirical distinguishability' (theories make different predictions about some cases) and 'practically testable distinguishability' (we can actually check which prediction is right). IIT and GNW make different predictions about whether the cerebellum could be conscious, whether feed-forward sweeps involve consciousness, whether offline processing is conscious. But in all these cases, we have no independent way to verify which prediction is correct - we can't ask the cerebellum, we can't catch ourselves in the 50ms between feed-forward and recurrence. The key premise is that theories diverge exactly where phenomenology becomes inaccessible to report, memory, and introspection. This isn't a coincidence - theorists construct their theories to match clear cases and only diverge on unclear cases. This means the theories ARE empirically distinct (they make different predictions) but not empirically decidable (we can't access the evidence that would decide them).

Appraiser (blind)
The in-principle versus in-practice distinction is useful but the observation that theories diverge where evidence is inaccessible is somewhat recognized in the literature.

U56. The methodological dispute about consciousness is really a dispute about whether we should treat first-person reports as more like observation reports or more like theoretical posits, and recognizing this reveals that the empirical adequacy criteria different theories employ are question-begging.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared assumption across the Frankish/Dennett deflationists, Rosenthal's HOT theory, and Block's phenomenal realism that we can settle the debate by seeing which theory is empirically adequate
Sketch

When higher-order theorists demand that a theory explain our judgments about consciousness, and phenomenal realists demand it explain consciousness itself, they're importing different evidential standards from philosophy of science. The former treats phenomenal reports like theoretical claims (which theories can revise), the latter like observation reports (which constrain theory). But choosing an evidential standard already presupposes an answer to what consciousness is—whether it's constituted by our cognitive access to it or independent of such access. This means the apparently empirical question 'which theory best explains the data?' covertly presupposes metaphysical commitments about what counts as data in the first place, making the debate more methodologically circular than participants realize.

Appraiser (blind)
Observation-report vs. theoretical-posit distinction is useful but circularity claim needs tighter argument about unavoidability.

U57. The demand that a theory of consciousness tell us 'which things are conscious' commits us to treating consciousness as a natural kind with sharp boundaries, but if consciousness is instead a cluster concept or determinable property, then multiple incompatible theories could each be correctly describing different legitimate ways of carving up the space.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — The widespread assumption that consciousness is a single explanandum that different theories compete to explain, shared by most participants
Sketch

Most consciousness research assumes there's a fact of the matter about whether, say, a bee or a fetus or a patient in a vegetative state is conscious, and that the right theory will give us the answer. But this assumes consciousness is a natural kind with determinate boundaries—that there's a unified phenomenon to theorize about. The key premise is that if consciousness is instead more like 'health' or 'intelligence'—a cluster of features that come apart in edge cases—then different theories might be tracking different legitimate clusters. IIT might track one cluster (integrated information), GWT another (broadcast availability), etc. This would explain why the theories seem incommensurable: they're not competing to describe the same phenomenon, but rather proposing different precisifications of a loose folk concept. Recognizing this reveals that demanding 'the right theory' commits us to an unjustified unification assumption.

Appraiser (blind)
The cluster concept possibility is worth exploring but the argument that theories might track different legitimate precisifications is already discussed in some form.

U58. The development of brain-computer interfaces that allow us to artificially induce the neural signatures that different theories identify with consciousness—without the usual functional or informational properties—will show that all current theories conflate consciousness with its typical causes or effects, since subjects will report consciousness is absent despite the signatures being present.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — Theories that identify consciousness with neural or functional patterns (GWT, IIT, recurrent processing theories)
Sketch

Suppose we can artificially create the 'global broadcasting' of information that GWT identifies with consciousness, or the integrated information that IIT quantifies, without the normal context—say, directly stimulating the relevant neural patterns. The key empirical premise is that when we do this, subjects will report that nothing is like anything, that they're not conscious of any content, even though the theory's proposed neural/functional signature is present. This would show that these theories have mistaken reliable correlates for constitutive features. The argument reveals that what seems like consciousness-constituting features are actually just typical causes or effects, and that we can only discover this through intervention, not mere observation. This means the right methodology is experimental manipulation, not looking for correlations.

Appraiser (blind)
An empirical prediction about future BCI experiments; interesting but depends on contingent empirical outcomes and the conflation claim is already debated.

U59. If we take seriously the possibility of theory-neutral consciousness measures, we discover that any such measure must either presuppose phenomenal realism (making it question-begging) or reduce to measuring confidence in self-reports (making it compatible with illusionism), revealing that the intermediate position—that consciousness is real but scientifically tractable—is unstable.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — The 'functionalist realism' position held by many working consciousness scientists who think consciousness is real, measurable, but not mysteriously non-physical
Sketch

Many researchers seek theory-neutral ways to measure consciousness—say, using perturbational complexity or machine learning on neural data. But consider what validates such a measure. Either (a) we validate it by checking whether it matches what subjects are 'really' conscious of, which presupposes that phenomenal states are what we're measuring (assuming phenomenal realism); or (b) we validate it by checking whether it predicts subjects' reports and behaviors, which means we're just measuring reportability/confidence (compatible with illusionism). The key premise is that there's no third option: any validation must appeal either to phenomenal facts or to functional/behavioral facts. This means the moderate position—consciousness is real and objective but functionally grounded—collapses into either phenomenal realism or illusionism depending on how we try to validate our measures. Those who think we can be empirical realists about consciousness without being either phenomenal realists or illusionists are confused.

Appraiser (blind)
Theory-neutral measures collapsing into realism-or-illusionism is interesting but the exhaustiveness of options needs defense.

U60. Every proposed empirical test of consciousness theories actually requires assuming the correct theory of consciousness to interpret the results, making the testing problem impossible rather than merely hard.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Methodological optimists who think the testing problem is tractable with better experimental design
Sketch

Consider any proposed test—say, testing whether integrated information correlates with consciousness. To run this test, we must first identify which systems are conscious and which aren't (to establish ground truth). But we can only make these identifications by assuming some theory of consciousness is correct. If we assume IIT, we 'discover' IIT is right; if we assume GWT, we 'discover' GWT is right. The key premise is that there is no theory-neutral way to establish which states are conscious, because 'being conscious' is precisely what the theories disagree about defining. This means the position 'we just need better experiments' is unstable—it collapses into 'we need to already know which theory is right in order to test which theory is right.' The testing project requires what it's trying to discover.

Appraiser (blind)
The circularity of needing ground truth is noted in the literature, though the impossibility claim rather than difficulty claim adds some force.

U61. Committing to consciousness being multiply realizable commits you to consciousness being empirically undetectable in principle, not just in practice.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Functionalists and most physicalists who accept multiple realizability while maintaining consciousness is scientifically tractable
Sketch

Most participants in the consciousness debate accept multiple realizability: consciousness could be realized in biological neurons, silicon chips, or other substrates. The argument turns on this premise: if consciousness is multiply realizable, then there is no physical signature that all and only conscious systems share. But if there's no such signature, then any proposed empirical test that succeeds for one implementation could fail for another implementation of the same conscious state. This means accepting multiple realizability commits you to accepting that no empirical test could reliably detect consciousness across all possible realizations. Proponents of multiple realizability are therefore committed to a form of empirical inaccessibility they typically reject, creating an unnoticed tension in views like functionalism that are both physicalist (seemingly empirical) and multiply realizable.

Appraiser (blind)
Multiple realizability entailing undetectability is provocative but the inference may be too quick; needs careful modal argument.

U62. Any consciousness theory that makes testable predictions necessarily confuses consciousness with its cognitive consequences, while theories that avoid this confusion make no testable predictions.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — Theories like GWT and IIT that claim to both identify consciousness correctly and make testable predictions
Sketch

Draw a distinction current theories blur: between consciousness itself (the qualitative character of experience) and its cognitive consequences (what the system does because it's conscious—report, reason, remember). Testable theories like GWT predict things about processing, integration, broadcasting—all cognitive consequences. But these predictions don't tell us about consciousness itself, only about what accompanies it. Meanwhile, theories that carefully distinguish consciousness from cognition (like pure qualia theories) correctly identify the target but generate no predictions, since consciousness-without-consequences is empirically invisible. The key premise is that testability requires causal consequences, but consciousness qua consciousness has no causal consequences—only the underlying mechanisms do. This creates a forced choice the literature hasn't recognized: be testable but study the wrong thing, or study the right thing but be untestable. Current theories try to have it both ways by equivocating on what 'consciousness' refers to.

Appraiser (blind)
The forced choice between testability and studying the right thing is sharp, though the consciousness-versus-consequences distinction is somewhat familiar.

U63. Testing consciousness theories requires solving the other-minds problem first, and any method that solves the other-minds problem would make consciousness theories empirically redundant.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — The research program that treats empirical validation of consciousness theories as both necessary and achievable
Sketch

The argument proceeds by examining what would actually count as confirming a consciousness theory. To confirm that integrated information (or global workspace, etc.) correlates with consciousness, we need independent access to when consciousness is present. But the other-minds problem shows we lack such access—we can only observe behavior and neural activity, never consciousness itself in others. If someone proposes a solution (say, 'these neural signatures reliably indicate consciousness'), they've already answered the empirical question the theories were meant to answer, making the theories explanatorily idle. The instability: either we can't test the theories (no solution to other-minds), or we don't need them (we already have a working consciousness detector).

Appraiser (blind)
The dilemma is clear—either can't test or theories are redundant—but the other-minds connection to theory-testing has been discussed before.

U64. First-person report reliability creates an experimenter's regress: any evidence that subjects accurately report their phenomenology already presupposes a theory about which neural states constitute consciousness.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Methodologies that rely on first-person reports as neutral evidence, particularly in contrastive analysis paradigms
Sketch

When researchers use subjective reports to validate consciousness theories, they must decide when to trust these reports. If a theory predicts consciousness is present but the subject reports none (or vice versa), researchers face a choice: reject the theory or doubt the report. This choice requires criteria for report reliability—but these criteria inevitably smuggle in commitments about what consciousness is and when it occurs. For instance, treating reports as reliable only when subjects are 'paying attention' commits you to a view where attention is constitutive of consciousness. The regress: to validate theory T, you need reliable reports, but determining reliability requires already accepting some theory T*, which itself needs validation. Proponents of empirical validation are committed to either circular reasoning or an unargued first theory.

Appraiser (blind)
The regress about report reliability is worth articulating but the circularity concern about validating theories through reports is recognized.

U65. Consciousness theories are empirically distinguishable only to the extent they make architectural claims rather than consciousness claims, revealing that the theories are actually competing models of cognitive architecture that merely use 'consciousness' as a marker.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — The framing that treats these as theories of consciousness rather than theories of information-processing that stipulate what counts as consciousness
Sketch

The key distinction: between a theory's architectural claims (information is integrated in this way, broadcasting occurs through these networks) and its consciousness claim (this architecture constitutes/produces consciousness). The architectural claims are testable—we can measure integration, track broadcasts, map predictions. But once we confirm the architecture, the consciousness claim remains an interpretive addition. IIT's integrated information is measurable, but 'Φ > 0 means consciousness' is not additionally tested. This reveals that calling something a 'consciousness theory' is really staking a claim about which measurable architectural features deserve the label 'consciousness.' The theories differ empirically only in their architecture, not in their account of consciousness per se.

Appraiser (blind)
The architecture versus consciousness distinction is useful but the observation that architectural claims are what's actually tested is somewhat acknowledged.

U66. Accepting that consciousness could be multiply realizable commits you to accepting that no evidence could distinguish between substrate-neutral theories, making empirical testing impossible in principle.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Functionalists and computational theorists who accept multiple realizability but propose empirical testing programs based on neuroscience
Sketch

Multiple realizability means that consciousness doesn't require specific physical implementation—silicon could work as well as neurons. But if substrate doesn't matter, then any functional isomorphs share phenomenology. This commits multiple-realizability theorists to the view that structurally identical systems must have identical consciousness regardless of substrate. However, this means we can never test whether artificial systems are conscious by comparing them to biological ones—any difference in verdict must stem from structural differences, not substrate. The key premise is that multiple realizability entails that substrate-level evidence is definitionally irrelevant, yet substrate-level evidence (neuroimaging, neurotransmitter studies) is our primary empirical tool.

Appraiser (blind)
Competent argument about multiple realizability's epistemic consequences, but the tension between substrate-neutrality and substrate-based evidence is already recognized in the literature.

U67. The distinction between 'access consciousness' and 'phenomenal consciousness' makes theories empirically testable by the same methods that distinguish them, but only if we abandon verbal report as privileged evidence.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — Both deflationists who reject the distinction and phenomenal realists who use it to argue that P-consciousness is empirically inaccessible
Sketch

Block's A-consciousness/P-consciousness distinction is typically used to show why certain experiments fail (they test access, not phenomenology). But flip this: the distinction itself suggests a testing methodology. If A and P come apart, then cases where subjects cannot report their phenomenology (due to access limitations, not absence of phenomenology) become crucial test cases. The key premise is that theories make different predictions about P-consciousness in the absence of A-consciousness (overflow in inattention, unconscious perception, locked-in states). The methodological upshot: abandon the assumption that verbal report is our epistemic ground, and instead triangulate from behavior, neural dynamics, and structural features in cases where report is impossible. This reclassifies the debate from 'epistemically impossible' to 'methodologically demanding.'

Appraiser (blind)
Interesting methodological flip on Block's distinction, but the proposal to triangulate without verbal report faces well-known challenges already discussed.

U68. Every current theory of consciousness assumes that facts about consciousness supervene on facts capturable in a completed neuroscience, but this assumption is a counterexample to itself—if true, it would be a fact about consciousness that no neuroscientific evidence could establish.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The shared naturalistic assumption across Dennett, Churchland, higher-order theories, and global workspace theory that empirical neuroscience suffices for theory choice
Sketch

Both physicalist and functionalist theories grant that consciousness supervenes on physical/functional organization. This supervenience claim is itself a claim about the nature of consciousness. But establishing supervenience requires showing that varying the base necessitates varying consciousness. The key premise: demonstrating necessity requires access to metaphysically possible variations (zombie worlds, inverted spectra), not just actual correlations. No amount of neuroscientific evidence—which only reveals actual correlations—can establish that the correlation is necessary rather than contingent. Therefore, the supervenience assumption that grounds the testability of consciousness theories is itself empirically untestable. This is self-undermining: the meta-claim that makes theories testable is itself not testable by the methods it authorizes.

Appraiser (blind)
Supervenience assumption being untestable is philosophically interesting but may not undermine practice as much as claimed.

U69. Any empirical test that could definitively adjudicate between consciousness theories would require solving the other minds problem for systems we currently treat as test cases.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Theorists who claim their consciousness theory is empirically testable (Tononi, Dehaene, Rosenthal)
Sketch

Consider testing IIT against GWT using brain organoids or split-brain patients. To know which theory correctly predicts consciousness in these cases, we'd need independent grounds for knowing whether the system is conscious—but this is precisely what's in dispute. We can't bootstrap from behavioral criteria (organoids don't behave) or introspective reports (split-brain patients give conflicting reports across hemispheres). The commitment to empirical testability thus commits theorists to either (a) already solved other minds problems for edge cases, or (b) restricting tests to cases where we have independent certainty, which circularly validates theories against our prior intuitions rather than testing them. The demand for empirical testability therefore collapses into either question-begging or requires achieving what most consider impossible.

Appraiser (blind)
The other minds problem for edge cases is recognized; this formulation is clear but doesn't add substantially new considerations.

U70. Every proposed empirical test of consciousness theories presupposes functionalism, making it impossible for such tests to vindicate non-functionalist theories.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The widespread methodological assumption that empirical neuroscience can adjudicate between consciousness theories including non-functionalist ones
Sketch

Empirical tests necessarily rely on observable inputs and outputs—brain states, behaviors, reports, or computational processes. But non-functionalist theories (like certain qualia realist views or biological naturalist positions) claim consciousness depends on intrinsic properties that don't supervene on functional organization. Any test showing that system X with functional profile F is conscious while system Y with different profile G is not would vindicate functionalism itself, not any particular functionalist theory. This undermines proposals to test between theories empirically: we can only test functionalist theories against each other, while non-functionalist alternatives are empirically untestable by design. The methodological naturalist consensus thus builds in a substantive theoretical commitment.

Appraiser (blind)
The point that empirical methods presuppose functionalism is known; competent articulation but limited novelty in consciousness literature.

U71. Consciousness theories are stable only when theorists equivocate between type-identity and token-identity with neural states, but enforcing consistency on this point makes every major theory collapse into either eliminativism or panpsychism.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Functionalist consciousness theories that claim to avoid both eliminativism and panpsychism
Sketch

Consider what GWT or HOT theorists claim when they say 'consciousness is global broadcast' or 'consciousness is higher-order representation'. Type-identity (conscious states as types are identical to functional types) leads to liberalism problems—thermostats have global broadcast, simple feedback systems have higher-order representation. To avoid this, theorists retreat to token-identity (this particular conscious state is this particular neural token). But token-identity provides no theoretical generalization—it's just eliminativism about consciousness-as-a-kind. The middle position requires saying consciousness is multiply realizable but only in systems 'sufficiently like us', which is either unprincipled anthropocentrism or sneaks in panpsychism (all matter has proto-conscious properties that reach threshold in complex systems). Every attempt to characterize the restriction on multiple realizability either becomes arbitrary or expands to everything.

Appraiser (blind)
Type/token equivocation forcing eliminativism-or-panpsychism is sharp but the dilemma may have escape routes needing examination.

U72. Any theory of consciousness that can be empirically confirmed must fail to be a theory of consciousness, because empirical confirmation requires operational definitions that eliminate the very subjectivity consciousness theories claim to explain.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — The entire project of empirical consciousness science as currently conceived, especially naturalistic theories claiming both scientific status and explanatory adequacy for phenomenal consciousness
Sketch

For a consciousness theory to make testable predictions, it must specify observable conditions that correlate with consciousness. But any such specification requires treating consciousness as identical to some functional/behavioral/neural profile, which converts phenomenal properties into functional ones. The argument shows that theories either (a) remain unconfirmable by refusing operational definitions, preserving subjectivity but losing empirical traction, or (b) adopt operational definitions that make them confirmable but thereby eliminate subjectivity in favor of objective proxies. The key premise: operational definitions work by substituting intersubjectively accessible properties for private ones, which is precisely what makes them inadequate for genuinely subjective properties. Attempts to have both confirmability and genuine subjectivity therefore reveal internal tension in the project itself.

Appraiser (blind)
Operational definition eliminating subjectivity is a familiar tension in philosophy of mind; competent but well-trodden ground.

U73. The search for 'the' right theory of consciousness presupposes a false uniformity: different aspects of consciousness (access, phenomenal character, unity, subjectivity) may require different and mutually irreducible explanatory frameworks.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — The assumption shared by competing theories that they're offering rival accounts of the same unified explanandum
Sketch

Current debates assume consciousness is a natural kind requiring a unified theory. But examining what makes access consciousness puzzling versus what makes phenomenal character puzzling reveals these target different explananda requiring different resources. Access consciousness poses a cognitive architecture problem answerable by information-processing theories like GNW. Phenomenal character poses a metaphysical problem about intrinsic qualities requiring different resources (perhaps Russellian monism). Unity raises mereological questions. Subjectivity raises perspective-dependence issues. The argument turns on showing these aren't separable aspects of one phenomenon but genuinely distinct phenomena that happen to co-occur. Looking for 'the theory of consciousness' is like looking for 'the theory of living things' when we need separate theories of metabolism, reproduction, and evolution.

Appraiser (blind)
Pluralism about consciousness aspects is already advocated by several theorists; this articulates it clearly but isn't breaking new ground.

U74. Holding that consciousness science requires solving the hard problem commits you to rejecting that we can currently know anyone else is conscious—a commitment most consciousness researchers implicitly deny when designing experiments.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Hard problem theorists who also engage in empirical consciousness research, particularly Chalmers and those who take the hard problem seriously while still doing consciousness science
Sketch

Many consciousness researchers (especially those skeptical of functionalism) maintain that solving the hard problem is necessary for a complete theory of consciousness. But experimental consciousness research requires assuming certain beings (awake humans, not anesthetized ones) are conscious to establish ground truth for training classifiers, validating measures, or testing predictions. This assumption requires already having reliable indicators of consciousness—precisely what solving the hard problem would provide. The argument shows that researchers cannot coherently both (a) claim we need to solve the hard problem to have a theory of consciousness and (b) design experiments that depend on already knowing who's conscious. The key premise: if phenomenal consciousness can't be read off functional/behavioral/neural profiles, then our experimental attributions of consciousness to ground-truth cases are epistemically unjustified.

Appraiser (blind)
Tension between hard problem commitment and experimental practice is recognized; solid point but doesn't substantially advance the debate.

U75. Theories of consciousness are empirically testable only when paired with specific bridge principles about reportability, but the choice of bridge principle already presupposes a theory of consciousness, making the entire testing enterprise circular.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — The shared assumption across empirical consciousness research (Dehaene, Tononi, Prinz, Block) that we can test theories by finding their distinctive empirical predictions
Sketch

Arguments proceed by showing that every proposed way to test consciousness theories (neural correlates studies, dissociation paradigms, lesion studies) requires assuming what counts as evidence of consciousness—whether subjects must be able to report experiences, whether unconscious processing counts, whether attention is necessary. For instance, testing whether phenomenal consciousness overflows access consciousness requires deciding whether unreportable experiences exist, but this is precisely what overflow theories claim and anti-overflow theories deny. The key premise is that bridge principles aren't theoretically neutral auxiliary hypotheses but are themselves substantive claims about consciousness that different theories reject. This means the apparently stable research program of 'testing theories against data' collapses into theory-choice based on prior commitments about what would count as data.

Appraiser (blind)
Bridge principle circularity is discussed in consciousness methodology; this is clear but doesn't offer new resolution or insight.

U76. Every proposed test for consciousness theories actually tests our concept of consciousness rather than consciousness itself, which means what appears to be theory comparison is actually conceptual negotiation about what 'consciousness' should mean.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The realist assumption shared across debates that there's a fact of the matter about what's conscious, which our theories approximate
Sketch

When researchers debate whether a patient in a vegetative state with covert task-related fMRI activity is conscious, they're not discovering a fact but negotiating whether 'consciousness' should be tied to reportability, behavioral responsiveness, or neural signatures. Similarly, debates over whether integrated information theory makes the cerebellum massively conscious don't turn on empirical discovery but on whether we should bite that bullet or revise the theory. The key premise is that consciousness has no theory-independent essence to anchor these disputes—our pre-theoretic concept underdetermines extension to novel cases (AI, organoids, split-brain, locked-in). What looks like testing theories against reality is actually testing conceptual proposals against intuitions, making this closer to stipulative definition than empirical discovery.

Appraiser (blind)
Conceptual negotiation versus empirical discovery is a recognized issue; competent but the insight is familiar from debates about natural kinds.

U77. Commitment to finding 'the' correct theory of consciousness commits you to epiphenomenalism, because only epiphenomenal properties can have multiple realizability space large enough to adjudicate between current theories.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Researchers who both reject epiphenomenalism and claim we can empirically determine which neural/computational substrate realizes consciousness
Sketch

Current theories disagree about what consciousness is realized in (integrated information vs. global broadcasting vs. higher-order states, etc.). For there to be a fact about which is correct, consciousness must be multiply realizable across these different substrates—otherwise the question is empirically empty. But the key premise is that the only properties with this degree of multiple realizability are those that don't constrain their physical realizers—i.e., epiphenomenal properties. If consciousness actually did causal work, it would constrain its realization narrowly enough that we couldn't meaningfully test between such diverse physical theories. Therefore, those seeking to empirically adjudicate between theories are committed to consciousness being epiphenomenal, even though most explicitly reject epiphenomenalism.

Appraiser (blind)
Interesting connection between multiple realizability and epiphenomenalism, but the argument that causal efficacy constrains realization narrowly is contestable.

U78. The assumption that consciousness admits of degrees commits us to the conclusion that no discrete theory can be correct, which eliminates all current leading theories.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The shared assumption that current leading discrete-mechanism theories are viable candidates
Sketch

Nearly everyone in the field accepts that consciousness admits of degrees (partial awareness, degrees of clarity, vegetative states, etc.). But all leading theories—IIT, GWT, HOT, recurrent processing theory—specify discrete architectural or computational features as necessary and sufficient for consciousness (either you have integrated information above phi-min, or global broadcasting, or higher-order representation, or you don't). The key premise is that discrete on-off mechanisms cannot smoothly generate graded phenomena without arbitrary threshold-setting. If consciousness genuinely comes in degrees, then theories specifying discrete mechanisms fail as explanations, leaving only continuous theories (perhaps something like degree of information integration without a threshold). But this counterexample eliminates the very theories that are supposed to be empirically testable.

Appraiser (blind)
Points to real tension between gradedness and discrete mechanisms, though the 'eliminates all current theories' conclusion overstates the case.

U79. Theories of consciousness are empirically distinguishable in principle but only at the surgical moment of creating or destroying consciousness, which our ethical commitments make permanently inaccessible to us.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — Pessimists who think consciousness theories are empirically indistinguishable even in principle, and optimists who think current neuroscience can resolve the debate
Sketch

Against the shared assumption that we can't ethically test consciousness theories, consider that we actually could distinguish them empirically: theories differ on whether consciousness requires global broadcasting (GWT), integrated information above threshold (IIT), or certain recursive structures (HOT). These predict different conditions are sufficient/necessary for consciousness. We could test them by systematically creating minimal systems that satisfy one theory's requirements but not others, or by surgically lesioning/stimulating to remove specific capacities, then checking for presence/absence of consciousness. The key premise is that consciousness is either present or absent in these cases—there's a fact of the matter. The problem isn't epistemic or methodological but purely ethical: the experiments that would distinguish theories require creating conscious entities to test them, destroying consciousness in existing subjects, or both. We've chosen not to know the answer.

Appraiser (blind)
Novel to frame the problem as purely ethical rather than epistemic, though the claim that theories are distinguishable 'in principle' needs more support.

U80. Any empirical test sufficient to adjudicate between consciousness theories would require solving the grain problem for neural correlates—but solving the grain problem already presupposes a theory of consciousness.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Researchers like Crick, Koch, and Chalmers who treat finding NCCs as an empirically tractable path to theory confirmation
Sketch

The grain problem asks: which spatiotemporal scale of neural activity constitutes the NCC—individual neurons, columns, hemispheres, or some other level? Every consciousness theory makes claims about NCCs at some grain, but we need independent evidence about the right grain to test theories against neural data. However, our only evidence for the correct grain comes from which grain best correlates with consciousness—creating a tight circle. IIT says integrated complexes at one scale matter; GWT says global broadcasts at another. The key premise is that 'finding the NCC' requires already knowing what size/duration events to look for, but this is precisely what competing theories disagree about, so empirical NCC evidence cannot be theory-neutral.

Appraiser (blind)
Identifies real circularity in grain problem, though this has been noted before; the systematic application to theory-testing adds some value.

U81. First-person reports can only adjudicate consciousness theories if we already know which cognitive processes subjects have introspective access to—but this is precisely what consciousness theories disagree about.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Methodological assumptions shared across camps that first-person reports provide theory-neutral data (assumed by both Schwitzgebel and mainstream consciousness science)
Sketch

Higher-order theories claim subjects can only report states they have higher-order thoughts about; first-order theories claim direct access to first-order states; illusionist theories claim reports are unreliable confabulations. Each theory thus predicts different reliability patterns for introspective reports. If we use reports to test theories, we must already assume some theory of introspective access to interpret whether a report's presence/absence confirms or disconfirms a prediction. The key premise is that treating reports as evidence commits you to a specific theory of reportability—you cannot neutrally collect 'what subjects say' without presupposing an account of what their saying it indicates. This means researchers who think phenomenology can constrain theory choice are already smuggling in theoretical commitments through their interpretation of reports.

Appraiser (blind)
Solid point about circularity in using reports, but this concern is familiar in debates about introspection and higher-order theories.

U82. The standard methodology of comparing conscious versus unconscious conditions commits researchers to treating consciousness as an on/off switch, but this commitment is incompatible with gradualist theories that most researchers explicitly endorse.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The methodological consensus in consciousness science exemplified by studies using binocular rivalry, masking, and attentional paradigms
Sketch

The dominant experimental paradigm uses contrastive analysis: compare trials where subjects report seeing a stimulus versus trials where they don't (masking, suppression, inattention). This methodology assumes consciousness comes in discrete present/absent states for a given content at a given time. However, most theorists explicitly endorse gradualism—consciousness varies continuously in degree, clarity, or richness. IIT assigns consciousness a continuous phi value; GWT allows partial broadcasts; most researchers accept that consciousness fades gradually in sleep onset. The key premise is that if consciousness is truly gradual, then comparing 'conscious' versus 'unconscious' trials is like comparing 'tall' versus 'short' people to study height—it imposes artificial categories on continuous variation, potentially finding correlates of the arbitrary threshold rather than consciousness itself.

Appraiser (blind)
Valid observation about methodology/theory mismatch, though the problem is somewhat recognized; systematic articulation has value but limited novelty.

U83. The zombie argument's conceivability premise commits functionalists to the conclusion that no empirical evidence could ever distinguish between their theory and epiphenomenalism, because any evidence that could settle this question would require consciousness to play a causal role that functionalists must attribute to physical function alone.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Functionalists who grant zombie conceivability while maintaining that consciousness can be empirically studied
Sketch

Functionalists typically grant that zombies are conceivable but not metaphysically possible, while epiphenomenalists grant both conceivability and possibility. But this disagreement cannot be empirically settled. Any behavioral or neural evidence we gather will be evidence about functional organization, not about whether that organization is accompanied by consciousness. To empirically distinguish functionalism from epiphenomenalism, we'd need consciousness itself to make a causal difference independent of its functional role—but functionalists are committed to denying this. If consciousness just IS functional organization, then all our evidence is equally compatible with epiphenomenalism (same functions plus causally idle qualia). The functionalist who grants zombie conceivability has thus committed themselves to their theory being empirically indistinguishable from a view they reject.

Appraiser (blind)
Interesting that granting zombie conceivability commits functionalists to empirical indistinguishability from epiphenomenalism, but argument needs tightening.

U84. The search for neural correlates of consciousness (NCCs) covertly assumes that consciousness is a brain-local property, but if extended or relational theories of consciousness are correct, then the entire NCC research program is looking for correlates in the wrong location space—it's a category mistake, not a hard empirical problem.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The mainstream neuroscience assumption that consciousness is realized in or by the brain
Sketch

The NCC framework assumes consciousness correlates with something inside the skull. But this assumption is contradicted by extended theories (where consciousness includes loops through environment/body) and relational theories (where consciousness is constituted by relations to external objects). These aren't just exotic positions—they're motivated by cases like tool-use integration and transparent perception. If I'm consciously seeing a tree, and the relationalist is right that this state is partly constituted by the tree itself, then looking for the NCC entirely within my brain is like looking for the correlate of 'being married' entirely in one person's body. The argument's key premise: research programs can fail not because they lack sophisticated enough tools, but because they're searching in an ontologically inappropriate domain. This means current methodology doesn't just need better technology—it needs reconceptualization of what location means for consciousness.

Appraiser (blind)
Extended/relational theories challenging NCC location assumptions is valuable, though the 'category mistake' framing may overreach.

U85. Any empirically adequate consciousness theory must make false predictions about at least one actual case, because consciousness comes in kinds with incompatible detection signatures.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — The shared assumption across IIT, GNW, HOT, and other theories that there is a unified phenomenon of consciousness to be explained by a single theory
Sketch

We assume that the correct theory of consciousness will correctly classify all actual conscious systems and correctly predict what interventions would create/destroy consciousness.
