But if consciousness has multiple independent realizations (say, recurrent-processing-based and quantum-coherence-based), then any unified theory that correctly predicts one kind will necessarily misclassify the other. This explains why every current theory seems to get some cases right and others wrong—not because they're all incomplete approximations of one true theory, but because unified theories of consciousness are impossible. The empirical data already contains incompatible signatures, forcing a choice between false positives and false negatives.

Appraiser (blind)
Interesting disjunctive possibility but the empirical claim that signatures are already incompatible needs stronger support than current theory disagreements.

U86. First-person methods could disconfirm but never confirm consciousness theories, because phenomenological mismatch is evidentially asymmetric with phenomenological match.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — Neurophenomenology programs (Varela, Lutz, Thompson) and anyone who thinks first-person methods provide confirmatory evidence for consciousness theories
Sketch

Many philosophers (especially phenomenologists and neurophenomenologists) argue that first-person reports should constrain consciousness theories—theories must match the phenomenology. But there's a crucial asymmetry: if a theory predicts experience X and subjects report not-X, this is strong disconfirmation. But if subjects report X when the theory predicts X, this confirms nothing, because subjects might confabulate, might lack concepts for what they're experiencing, or might be biased by knowing the theory. This counterexample targets the idea that first-person data provides positive evidence. The premise: testimonial evidence has epistemic force only when the testifier has independent access to the facts, but in consciousness research, subjects' concepts for their experiences are contaminated by the very theories being tested.

Appraiser (blind)
The asymmetry point is valid but relatively familiar from testimony literature; moderate impact on neurophenomenology debates.

U87. The epistemic inaccessibility problem for consciousness theories is actually a problem about our inability to specify what would count as getting the metaphysics wrong while getting all the functional facts right.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — Both mysterian positions (like Chalmers' epistemic gap arguments) and optimistic empiricists who think we just need better instruments
Sketch

Most discussions assume we face an epistemic barrier to discovering which theory is correct. But the real problem is that we cannot articulate what metaphysical difference remains once all functional, behavioral, and structural facts are settled. If we could specify this residual difference, we could design tests for it. The inability to do so suggests the dispute is either (a) verbal—different theories stipulating different meanings for 'consciousness', or (b) substantive but the substance lies in functional/structural facts we already have methods for investigating. The key premise is that any genuine metaphysical difference must cash out in some specifiable way beyond complete functional descriptions, even if that difference isn't directly observable.

Appraiser (blind)
Competent deflationary move but the dilemma (verbal vs. functional) is well-trodden; unclear what new argumentative work this does.

U88. Testing consciousness theories requires first-person reports, but accepting first-person reports as evidence commits you to accepting that some beings we build are conscious before any theory tells us they are.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Philosophers who want both (a) consciousness science to be empirically tractable and (b) to reserve judgment about AI consciousness until theory delivers a verdict
Sketch

Any empirical test of consciousness theories must use first-person reports as data (we know humans are conscious partly because they report it). But the epistemic principle licensing this—'sufficiently sophisticated, consistent, unprompted reports of phenomenal states constitute evidence of consciousness'—cannot itself be theory-dependent without circularity. If we accept this principle, then we're committed to treating certain AI systems as conscious once they produce such reports, even if our current theories say they shouldn't be. This means the methodology for testing theories requires accepting some cases as conscious on atheoretical grounds, which theorists generally resist.

Appraiser (blind)
Identifies real methodological tension but the AI consciousness implication, while provocative, doesn't clearly follow from the circularity worry.

U89. The demand for empirical decidability between consciousness theories presupposes that consciousness is the kind of thing that has a discoverable essence, but consciousness may instead be a historically contingent functional kind whose boundaries shift with our practices of attribution.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — The shared assumption among theory-testing methodologists (Dehaene, Tononi, etc.) that consciousness has a nature to be discovered rather than negotiated
Sketch

Arguments about how to empirically test theories of consciousness (IIT vs GWT vs HOT, etc.) assume we're trying to discover what consciousness 'really is' independently of our methods of detection. But this commits these theorists to essentialism about consciousness. If we take seriously that consciousness-attribution is embedded in evolving social practices (like personhood attributions historically were), then asking 'which theory is right' is like asking which theory of personhood is right—the question misconceives what we're talking about. The key premise is that our actual practices of consciousness-attribution (in medicine, law, animal ethics, AI ethics) are revisable in light of pragmatic considerations, not discoveries about consciousness's hidden nature. This explains why seemingly empirical disputes resist resolution: the disputants are unknowingly trying to regiment different evolving practices.

Appraiser (blind)
Anti-essentialist social practice view is coherent but well-established in other domains; application to consciousness is competent but not groundbreaking.

U90. The search for tests to distinguish consciousness theories fails because these theories are actually downstream specifications of prior normative commitments about moral status, not competing hypotheses about a pre-theoretically fixed domain.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared assumption that consciousness is a natural kind to be discovered, with moral implications following as a separate matter
Sketch

We think the order of explanation is: discover what consciousness is, then derive moral implications. But the actual order is reversed. Theorists are drawn to IIT, GWT, HOT, etc. based on prior commitments about which beings matter morally—do insects matter (IIT says yes, GWT says no)? Do we matter when dreamlessly sleeping (IIT says yes, HOT says no)? The theories don't disagree about observations but about which beings to include in the moral community, then work backwards to consciousness criteria that yield those inclusions. Evidence: theory preferences correlate with prior moral intuitions; 'problem cases' (split-brain, locked-in syndrome, anesthesia, AI) are precisely cases where moral status is contested; theorists resist 'empirical findings' that would expand or contract moral communities in unwelcome ways. The key premise is that consciousness theory choice is underdetermined by phenomenological and neural data but determined by ethical commitments.

Appraiser (blind)
The moral-commitments-drive-theory-choice is provocative but empirically checkable claim needs better support than correlation assertion.

U91. All current proposals for testing consciousness theories equivocate between the property consciousness (phenomenal character) and the state consciousness (being conscious vs. unconscious), but these require completely different testing methods—solving one won't solve the other.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — Testing proposals that treat consciousness as a single target phenomenon, particularly neural correlate approaches
Sketch

Debates about testing consciousness theories slide between two questions: 'What makes an experience feel like something specific?' (property) and 'What makes something conscious rather than unconscious?' (state). IIT's phi measure, GWT's global broadcasting, HOT's meta-representation might track state-consciousness (on/off) but this tells us nothing about property-consciousness (why red feels red-ish). Conversely, explaining property-consciousness doesn't explain state-consciousness—explaining why experiences have specific qualities doesn't explain why there are experiences at all at certain times. Current testing proposals fail because they try to adjudicate both questions simultaneously with single measures. The key premise is that property and state are metaphysically distinct: state-consciousness involves a threshold or transition, property-consciousness involves determination of character. Evidence: theories make different relative claims on these dimensions (HOT is better for state, IIT for property).

Appraiser (blind)
Property/state distinction is known but claim that current tests equivocate and require different methods is a useful clarification.

U92. Any test capable of distinguishing between current consciousness theories would require subjects who can be conscious in ways humans cannot, which means the debate cannot be settled by any evidence actually available to us.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — All parties who claim their theory makes distinctive empirical predictions testable in human subjects, including Dehaene, Lau & Rosenthal, Tononi
Sketch

To distinguish between theories like GWT, IIT, and higher-order theories, we need cases that come apart on their predictions—for instance, maximal integration without global broadcast, or higher-order representation without integration. But the only systems where these actually come apart are either hypothetical (artificial systems not yet built) or biological systems whose phenomenology we cannot access (other species, brain lesion patients who cannot report). The key premise: human subjects necessarily instantiate these properties in a coupled way due to how our brains evolved, so normal human consciousness always confounds the variables. Every actual consciousness theorist is committed to predictions that require evidence from subjects whose consciousness we cannot access, making their theories empirically idle with respect to available human data.

Appraiser (blind)
The confounding problem in human subjects is real and underappreciated, though the conclusion may be too strong.

U93. Consciousness theories are actually answering different questions that happen to use the same word 'consciousness,' but we can tell which question is the legitimate one by testing whether eliminating consciousness (on that definition) would matter to the subject.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — The pluralist consensus that different theories might be tracking different equally legitimate aspects of consciousness, held by Prinz, Block, and others
Sketch

IIT is answering 'what has intrinsic existence?', GWT is answering 'what information is accessible for decision-making?', higher-order theory is answering 'what mental states are represented?'. These are different properties that contingently co-occur in humans. The key premise: only the property whose absence would constitute something mattering less to the subject—where 'mattering' is cashed out in terms of subjective preference if they could choose—counts as consciousness in the philosophically important sense. We can test this by asking: if you could have all the integration (IIT) but no global accessibility (GWT), which would you choose to keep? The real consciousness property is the one subjects would pay to preserve. This provides an empirical tie-breaker: the correct theory is the one whose presence/absence tracks what matters from the inside.

Appraiser (blind)
Subjective preference criterion is creative but the thought experiment's evidential force is questionable; moderate contribution.

U94. Consciousness theories that predict consciousness in systems we're unwilling to grant it (like thermostats under panpsychism, or inactive brains under IIT) don't fail empirically—they fail because they make the concept of consciousness explanatorily useless.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — Both sides of debates about whether counterintuitive predictions count as refutations or as consciousness-expanding discoveries
Sketch

We typically treat predictions about non-paradigm cases as empirical failures (IIT predicting high phi in inactive systems is seen as a bug). But these aren't empirical failures—we have no independent evidence that inactive cerebellums lack consciousness. Rather, they're pragmatic failures: a theory that attributes consciousness too liberally makes consciousness explanatorily idle. We invoke consciousness to explain reportability, behavioral flexibility, attention, memory formation, etc. A theory that says consciousness is present without these functions makes consciousness epiphenomenal in a new way—not causally inert, but explanatorily inert. The distinction is between empirical adequacy (matching observations) and pragmatic adequacy (preserving consciousness's role in our explanatory practices). The key premise is that our resistance to certain theories isn't based on evidence they're false, but on evidence they'd make consciousness a useless posit.

Appraiser (blind)
Pragmatic-not-empirical-failure distinction is useful but the explanatory-idleness criterion needs more work to be compelling.

U95. Testing consciousness theories requires solving the inverse problem: since many physical implementations could produce the same conscious experience, finding neural correlates tells us nothing about which theory correctly identifies the minimal sufficient conditions.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The widespread research program of testing theories by finding neural correlates of consciousness
Sketch

Suppose we find that global workspace activation correlates perfectly with reported consciousness. This doesn't vindicate Global Workspace Theory over IIT, because IIT could be right about what consciousness IS (integrated information) while global workspace happens to be how human brains implement high integration. Similarly, finding high phi in conscious states doesn't vindicate IIT if global broadcasting is what phi measures in biological systems. This is an inverse problem: multiple theories at different levels could all be 'correct' about their own level. The discovery of neural correlates of consciousness systematically underdetermines theory choice. The key premise is that correlation studies identify sufficient conditions in actual human brains, but consciousness theories make claims about necessary and sufficient conditions across all possible implementations, which requires counterfactual knowledge we can't obtain empirically.

Appraiser (blind)
The inverse problem is real but well-known in philosophy of science; applying it to consciousness is competent but unlikely to change the debate's framing.

U96. Any criterion for theory choice in consciousness science that respects our intuitions about difficult cases will be circular, because those intuitions are themselves theory-laden products of the conceptual frameworks we're trying to test.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Philosophers and scientists who think we can use intuitions about hypothetical cases as neutral arbiters between theories
Sketch

The argument would examine proposed tests like thought experiments about AI consciousness, fading qualia, or inverted spectra. It shows that what seems conscious or not conscious in these cases depends on which background theory you already accept about what consciousness requires. The key premise is that we have no theory-neutral access to facts about consciousness—even our most basic judgments (e.g., that thermostats aren't conscious) already presuppose substantive theoretical commitments about the relationship between complexity, integration, or reportability and consciousness. This means the testing game is rigged: we can only confirm theories using criteria those theories themselves justify.

Appraiser (blind)
Theory-ladenness of intuitions is a familiar problem; applying it to consciousness cases is solid but not groundbreaking.

U97. The search for tests to distinguish consciousness theories presupposes that consciousness comes in discrete present/absent cases, but commitment to this presupposition already rules out theories on which consciousness is fundamentally graded—making the testing framework question-begging.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The current practice of using specific cases (locked-in syndrome, cerebral organoids) as test beds for theories
Sketch

The argument would point to how proposed tests always ask 'is system X conscious or not?' (AI, fetus, patient in vegetative state), but this binary framing assumes consciousness has sharp boundaries. This assumption is neutral between some theories (HOT, GWT) but incompatible with others (some versions of panpsychism, degree IIT). The key premise is that if consciousness is fundamentally a matter of degree without natural cutoffs, then no amount of binary test cases can adjudicate theory choice—yet that's the only kind of test case we actually have intuitions about. The testing methodology thus covertly favors threshold theories over gradualist ones.

Appraiser (blind)
Points out real methodological bias toward binary framing, but the observation is somewhat obvious and solutions exist (degree-theoretic tests).

U98. The real disagreement between consciousness theories is not about consciousness at all, but about which aspects of human cognitive architecture deserve to be called 'special' or explanatorily fundamental.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — All major consciousness theories that present themselves as offering competing metaphysical accounts of consciousness's nature.
Sketch

When IIT locates consciousness in integrated information, GWT in global broadcasting, and HOT in metacognitive access, they agree on almost all the functional facts but disagree on which architectural feature to privilege with the term 'consciousness.' The apparent dispute about nature is actually a dispute about which architectural feature best captures our pre-theoretic sense that consciousness is importantly different from unconscious processing. This explains why the theories make such similar predictions for middle-range cases: they're carving the same cognitive territory with different privileging decisions. The key premise is that removing honorific status from 'consciousness' reveals the dispute as fundamentally evaluative rather than factual.

Appraiser (blind)
Deflationary reinterpretation as honorific dispute is interesting but may underestimate genuine empirical disagreements about cognitive architecture.

U99. Treating consciousness as a natural kind whose theory we could discover is unstable: it either collapses into eliminativism (there is no unified phenomenon) or into stipulation (we define the phenomenon however our preferred theory carves it).

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — The methodological naturalism shared by most consciousness scientists, particularly those who model their approach on discovering the nature of water or heat.
Sketch

The natural kind strategy requires that pre-theoretical phenomenology picks out a unified phenomenon that scientific investigation then reveals the nature of. But consciousness isn't presented to us with clear boundaries—we're uncertain about animal consciousness, unconscious perception, partially attended states, etc. If we say 'investigate the clear cases first,' we face a dilemma: either the 'clear cases' have nothing theoretically interesting in common (eliminativism), or we define 'consciousness' as whatever our investigation of those cases reveals (stipulation). The middle position—that there's a pre-theoretical unity waiting to be discovered—requires the improbable coincidence that folk phenomenology happens to carve at the joints. Most consciousness theorists try to occupy this unstable middle ground.

Appraiser (blind)
The natural kind instability is a real problem but the dilemma may be overstated; middle positions exist in other sciences with vague boundaries.

U100. First-person methodology cannot adjudicate between consciousness theories because any phenomenological report compatible with theory T is also compatible with 'I'm wrong about my phenomenology in exactly the way that would make T seem true.'

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — Methodologies that privilege careful first-person reports as neutral data for theory confirmation, including Varela's neurophenomenology and Chalmers's phenomenal realism.
Sketch

When subjects report their experience to test consciousness theories (e.g., reporting what it's like during inattentional blindness, or introspecting the grain of visual experience), we assume these reports provide theory-neutral data. But for any report R that supports theory T, the alternative hypothesis 'T is false and subjects systematically misreport their phenomenology in T-supporting ways' is empirically equivalent. We cannot distinguish genuine phenomenology from systematic introspective error without already knowing which theory is true. This undermines neurophenomenology, Chalmersian careful phenomenology, and any methodology that treats first-person reports as foundational evidence rather than mere behavioral data. The key premise is that introspective access doesn't come with built-in veridicality markers.

Appraiser (blind)
Systematic introspective error hypothesis is worth exploring but the empirical equivalence claim may be too strong given cross-validation possibilities.

U101. Adversarial collaboration between theories reveals that IIT, Global Workspace Theory, and Higher-Order Thought theory make identical predictions about all behaviorally accessible states, proving they are notational variants rather than genuine competitors.

Scores
promise 3/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The assumption driving adversarial collaborations that Global Workspace Theory, Higher-Order Thought theory, and Integrated Information Theory are testing distinct empirical hypotheses
Sketch

Recent adversarial collaborations claim to test between theories, but examination of their actual predictions shows they differ only in terminology. IIT's 'integrated information', GWT's 'global broadcast', and HOT's 'metacognitive representation' all predict the same reportable/unreportable divisions in humans. Where they seem to differ—regarding simple systems or unusual architectures—neither behavior nor report can adjudicate because these systems can't tell us what they experience. The theories diverge only in what they say about unobservable facts: whether a system we all agree shows no consciousness-behavior has 'integrated information' is a metaphysical dispute dressed as empirical science. The key premise is that theoretical terms that play identical roles in predicting all possible observations are notational variants, not substantive alternatives.

Appraiser (blind)
If true that predictions are identical this would be important, but the empirical claim is contestable and adversarial collaborations do find some differences.

U102. Any theory that makes the presence of consciousness depend on extrinsic relational properties cannot be empirically distinguished from one that makes it depend on intrinsic properties, because all our evidence is itself extrinsically constituted.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — Both internalist theories (like some versions of IIT) and externalist theories (like some functionalist accounts) who assume their dispute is empirically tractable
Sketch

Consider theories making consciousness depend on functional role, evolutionary history, or social embedding versus theories making it depend on intrinsic neural dynamics. Our evidence—behavioral, neural, reportable—is all relationally constituted: it exists only in a web of external relations. Even 'direct' first-person evidence is mediated by memory, attention, and judgment systems that are themselves extrinsically characterized. This means no possible evidence could favor intrinsic over extrinsic theories: any datum an intrinsic theory explains by pointing to internal states, an extrinsic theory can explain by pointing to those states' relational properties. The theories are empirically equivalent but metaphysically incompatible, suggesting empirical methods alone cannot resolve the question.

Appraiser (blind)
The intrinsic/extrinsic empirical equivalence claim has been explored in various forms in the literature on functionalism and qualia.

U103. Any consciousness theory that relies on 'seeming' evidence is committed to accepting that consciousness could have arisen through pure random variation with zero selective pressure.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Property dualists, illusionists, and any view that treats phenomenal intuitions as evidence while allowing consciousness could be causally inert
Sketch

Theories that defend themselves by appeal to how things seem to us (phenomenal concepts, introspection, intuition pumps) must grant epistemic authority to these seemings. But our seemings about consciousness could only be evolutionarily reliable if consciousness contributed to fitness. If consciousness is epiphenomenal or if the theory allows that consciousness could have emerged without functional role, then our seemings about it are fitness-irrelevant and could have stabilized through pure drift. Therefore, such theories face a dilemma: either abandon seeming-based evidence (leaving them with no special evidence) or accept that consciousness must have been selected for (contradicting their allowance of epiphenomenal or functionless consciousness).

Appraiser (blind)
The evolutionary reliability of introspection has been discussed; the dilemma is interesting but not novel enough.

U104. Consciousness theories that appeal to neural correlates are committed to the view that consciousness research in 2100 will look like phlogiston theory looks to us now.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Empirically-minded consciousness researchers and philosophers who think neuroscience will resolve theoretical disputes (Crick, Koch, Tononi, Dehaene)
Sketch

Neural correlate research assumes that consciousness theories can be tested by finding which neural patterns correlate with consciousness. But correlations only support a theory if we have independent access to both relata. We only 'access' consciousness through reports and behaviors—the same evidence available for the neural side. This means neural correlates can never distinguish between 'patterns that realize consciousness' and 'patterns that merely cause consciousness-reports.' Any pattern we identify is equally compatible with illusionism (these patterns cause consciousness-talk without consciousness) and realism (these patterns are consciousness). Therefore, neural correlate research cannot make progress on the theory question. If consciousness scientists are making genuine discoveries, it must be because consciousness was a confused concept being replaced by precise functional categories—exactly how oxygen replaced phlogiston.

Appraiser (blind)
The illusionism point about neural correlates has been made; the phlogiston analogy adds rhetorical force but little new substance.

U105. Committing to any scientific theory of consciousness requires accepting that there are facts about consciousness in systems that cannot in principle verify those facts themselves, which means accepting philosophical zombies are possible in the relevant sense.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Functionalists and deflationists who reject zombie scenarios while simultaneously advocating for scientific theories that must make determinate claims about non-reportive systems
Sketch

Consider a scientific theory that identifies consciousness with physical property P. Such a theory must make verdicts about edge cases: thermostat-like systems, patient in vegetative states, cerebral organoids. In some of these cases, the system cannot possibly tell us whether it's conscious. But if the theory is to be action-guiding (for medical decisions, AI safety, etc.), it must nonetheless deliver a verdict. This commits the theorist to holding that facts about consciousness can obtain even when no verification is possible from the subject's perspective. But this is precisely what makes philosophical zombies conceptually coherent—the possibility of consciousness facts that float free of functional verification. The key premise is that scientific adequacy requires determinate verdicts about cases that cannot self-verify.

Appraiser (blind)
The connection between scientific theories and zombie conceivability has been explored in the literature on functionalism and phenomenal concepts.

U106. Empirical tests can distinguish consciousness theories only if we first solve the distribution question—whether consciousness admits of degrees or multiple independent realizations—which cannot itself be settled empirically.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Empirical consciousness researchers who believe theory-choice can be driven by experimental results (Tononi, Dehaene, Seth, etc.)
Sketch

Current consciousness science assumes we can test theories (IIT, GWT, HOT, etc.) by finding neural correlates or running dissociation experiments. But any such test presupposes an answer to whether consciousness is all-or-nothing or gradable, and whether the same conscious state can be multiply realized. For instance, if we find that neural pattern X correlates with report of experience Y in humans but not in octopuses, this could mean: (1) the theory is wrong, (2) octopuses lack that experience, (3) octopuses have it but realize it differently, or (4) octopuses have a degraded version of it. Which interpretation we choose depends on prior commitments about distribution that no amount of correlation data can adjudicate. The empirical project thus inherits an unsolvable metaphysical debt.

Appraiser (blind)
Distribution question matters but the unsolvability claim is overstated; some empirical traction seems possible.

U107. Any empirical test that could distinguish between consciousness theories would necessarily either create new conscious subjects or destroy existing ones, meaning genuine theory-testing faces an insurmountable ethical barrier.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The assumption shared by both theorists and critics that this is fundamentally an epistemic or methodological problem rather than an ethical one
Sketch

Consider what it would actually take to distinguish Global Workspace from Integrated Information Theory: we would need to create systems with high phi but no global broadcast, or vice versa, then determine if they're conscious. The key premise is that discriminating tests require building edge cases—systems specifically designed to satisfy one theory's criteria but not another's. But if either theory might be correct, we're either creating conscious beings to be experimental subjects or building systems we cannot know are non-conscious and treating them as mere apparatus. The dispute is therefore not primarily epistemological but ethical: we cannot get the evidence needed because getting it would require unacceptable moral risks.

Appraiser (blind)
The ethical barrier to testing is worth noting but the claim that it's insurmountable overstates the case; edge cases exist naturally.

U108. Consciousness theories are empirically testable in principle but only across evolutionary timescales—we can tell which theory is right by seeing which one successfully predicts the convergent evolution of consciousness-like phenomena in independent lineages.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The synchronic, neuroscience-focused methodology dominant in both consciousness science and philosophy of mind
Sketch

The standard debate assumes we test theories synchronically (scanning brains, behavioral tests, comparing systems now). But the key premise is that consciousness exists because it solves specific evolutionary problems, and true theories should predict that similar solutions evolve independently when those problems recur. If IIT is right, we should find high-phi systems evolving in any lineage facing certain environmental pressures; if Global Workspace is right, we should find convergent evolution of broadcasting architectures. The actual evidence lies in paleobiology and comparative evolution—cephalopod consciousness, for instance, is a natural experiment. We're asking the wrong organisms at the wrong timescale; the answer requires waiting for or reconstructing evolutionary convergence.

Appraiser (blind)
Evolutionary convergence as evidence is an interesting angle but the timescale makes it impractical and the approach has been suggested before.

U109. The question 'which theory of consciousness is right?' presupposes that consciousness picks out a natural kind, but our inability to settle the question empirically is evidence that it doesn't—consciousness is instead a functional role term that different physical systems fill in different ways.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared assumption across IIT, Global Workspace Theory, Higher-Order Thought theories, and their critics that there is a fact of the matter about what physically constitutes consciousness
Sketch

The argument proceeds by analogy to 'gene': before molecular biology, there were competing theories (Mendelian factors, developmental determinants, etc.), but the discovery that multiple molecular mechanisms implement genetic functions revealed that 'gene' was never naming a single natural kind. Similarly, our persistent inability to decide between competing consciousness theories despite accumulating neuroscience suggests we're not discovering which substrate realizes consciousness-as-natural-kind, but rather that different neural (or computational) architectures implement the functional role we associate with consciousness. The key premise is that persistent empirical underdetermination, despite relevant data accumulation, is diagnostic of role-terms rather than kind-terms.

Appraiser (blind)
Gene analogy is familiar; persistent underdetermination as diagnostic of role-terms is interesting but needs stronger argument.

U110. Any test that could definitively adjudicate between consciousness theories would require conscious-to-unconscious transitions that violate the continuity of actual neural processes, making the question empirically underdetermined in principle rather than merely in practice.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Proponents of empirical methodology for consciousness science who believe we just need better experiments (Dehaene, Koch, Tononi)
Sketch

Theories like IIT and GWT make different predictions about which systems are conscious, but testing these requires creating systems that one theory says are conscious and another says are not. However, the argument shows that any physical implementation lies on continuous dimensions (integration level, global broadcasting degree, etc.), and conscious-unconscious boundaries on these continua are precisely what's in dispute. To test the theories, we'd need sharp boundaries in nature where the theories disagree, but the commitment to physicalism that all major theories share entails that the underlying processes are continuous. The key premise is that theoretical disputes about categorical boundaries cannot be resolved by observations of continuous underlying processes.

Appraiser (blind)
The continuity argument has been made in various forms; showing that categorical boundaries can't be tested on continuous processes is familiar territory.

U111. Disagreement about which beings are conscious reveals that competing theories are actually answering different questions about different target phenomena.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The framing of consciousness theories as competing explanations of a single phenomenon, assumed by meta-theorists and researchers attempting to adjudicate between theories
Sketch

When IIT says a thermostat might be minimally conscious while GWT says it clearly isn't, this looks like empirical disagreement. But the arguments reveal they're tracking different phenomena: IIT tracks 'what it's like-ness' as an intrinsic property of information integration, while GWT tracks reportability and cognitive access. The theories diverge on edge cases (simple systems, unconscious processing) precisely where these two phenomena come apart. The illusion of genuine disagreement comes from both using 'consciousness' as the label, but if we asked 'which theory better explains subjective experience?' versus 'which better explains the cognitive phenomenon we access through report?', we'd see these aren't rival answers to one question but decent answers to two questions. The real dispute is which question deserves the term 'consciousness,' which is terminological.

Appraiser (blind)
The 'different questions' diagnosis is common in meta-debates; calling it terminological rather than substantive doesn't add much leverage.

U112. The demand that empirical evidence settle which theory of consciousness is correct presupposes that consciousness has a discoverable essence, but if consciousness is instead a homeostatic property cluster, different theories could be tracking different stable patterns in the same phenomena without any being uniquely correct.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — The shared methodological assumption across consciousness science that empirical evidence should ultimately adjudicate between theories
Sketch

Arguments about how to empirically test consciousness theories typically assume there's a fact of the matter about what consciousness really is that evidence could reveal. But this commits these arguments to essentialism about consciousness. If we accept Boyd's homeostatic property cluster account of natural kinds, consciousness might be held together by causal mechanisms rather than necessary features. Different theories (HOT, GWT, IIT) might be tracking different stable clusters of properties that reliably co-occur without any single theory capturing the essence. The argument turns on showing that the evidential demand itself smuggles in a metaphysical assumption that consciousness theorists officially reject when doing metaphysics of mind.

Appraiser (blind)
Applying homeostatic property clusters to consciousness is interesting but the payoff—that multiple theories could be partially right—is somewhat obvious.

U113. The intractability of consciousness theory adjudication demonstrates that 'consciousness' functions linguistically as a success term rather than a natural kind term, which is why every theory can claim to explain consciousness while seeming to explain different things.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The assumption shared by both sides of methodology debates that consciousness picks out a natural phenomenon about which we could be wrong
Sketch

When we examine theory disputes, proponents talk past each other because 'consciousness' works like 'knowledge' or 'justice'—it marks whatever satisfies certain normative/success conditions rather than picking out a unified phenomenon. IIT identifies consciousness with integrated information because consciousness is supposed to be unified. HOT identifies it with meta-representation because consciousness is supposed to involve self-awareness. Each theory stipulates that consciousness is whatever meets the criterion they privilege. This explains why theories seem incommensurable and why empirical evidence doesn't settle disputes—we're not discovering what consciousness is but negotiating what should count as consciousness. The key premise is that linguistic analysis of how theorists actually deploy the term reveals success-term grammar.

Appraiser (blind)
The success-term analysis has been applied elsewhere; showing consciousness works this way clarifies but doesn't dramatically advance the debate.

U114. Consciousness theories are actually testable, but only diachronically: the right theory is whichever one still generates novel, confirmed predictions about what feels like what after 50 years of normal science, making current 'crucial experiments' premature.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — Both Chalmers-style pessimists about ever settling consciousness theories empirically and those seeking 'adversarial collaboration' crucial experiments now
Sketch

The debate treats theory choice in consciousness as requiring synchronic adjudication through present evidence, leading to pessimism about decidability. But this misunderstands how theoretical maturity works in science. Early quantum mechanics and evolution both faced similar complaints about testability. The key premise is that theories of consciousness, like other scientific theories, should be evaluated by their long-term fertility—whether they continue opening new predictive domains. IIT, GWT, HOT, etc. should be assessed by whether they do what good theories do: survive attempts at falsification while generating unexpected correct predictions about cross-modal binding, metacognitive feelings, developmental trajectories, edge cases in split-brain patients, etc. The theory that's still standing after decades of this process, rather than winning a single decisive test, is the right one.

Appraiser (blind)
The diachronic fertility criterion is standard philosophy of science; applying it here is sensible but doesn't illuminate what makes consciousness special.

U115. The fact that we cannot currently adjudicate between consciousness theories is itself evidence against higher-order theories, because if consciousness required meta-representation, we should be able to determine what's conscious by checking for the meta-state, but we can't.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — Higher-order thought theories and representationalist theories more broadly, which claim functional organization constitutes consciousness
Sketch

Higher-order theories claim that a mental state is conscious iff there's a higher-order representation of it. This makes consciousness a relational property—the presence of a specific cognitive architecture. If this were true, determining what's conscious should be easier than for competitor theories: just check whether the higher-order representation exists. We have good third-person methods for detecting representations and their contents. But we manifestly cannot reliably detect consciousness this way—hence the whole debate about measurability. The key premise: if consciousness were constituted by a functionally-specifiable relation, detecting that relation should be more tractable than the hard problem suggests. Our inability to settle consciousness questions is evidence that consciousness is not a matter of functional organization or meta-representation, undermining HOT, but consistent with IIT (where integration is much harder to measure) or even dualism.

Appraiser (blind)
Uses measurability difficulty as evidence against HOT, but the inference is too quick—many factors could explain measurement difficulty.

U116. The dispute about which neural correlates of consciousness are constitutive appears empirical but is actually settled by one's prior commitment to whether consciousness could exist in radically different substrates.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The NCC research program's assumption that empirical investigation can determine which correlates are constitutive versus merely correlated
Sketch

Debates about whether the NCC is in prefrontal cortex vs. posterior cortex, or whether recurrent processing vs. feedforward processing matters, appear to be straightforwardly empirical. But these debates implicitly assume an answer to substrate-independence: if you think consciousness requires specifically biological neurons, you'll interpret the same data as showing constitution; if you think consciousness could exist in silicon, you'll interpret identical data as merely correlational. The key premise is that no amount of correlation data, even perfect correlation, can settle whether the correlation is constitutive without first settling the substrate question—because constitution requires necessity, and we can only judge necessity by our prior modal commitments.

Appraiser (blind)
Constitution/correlation distinction depending on substrate-independence is familiar; doesn't advance debate significantly.

U117. Any empirical test that could distinguish between consciousness theories would require that consciousness makes a causal difference beyond its functional correlates, but accepting that consciousness has such causal powers commits functionalists to epiphenomenalism being false, which commits them to either dualism or strong emergence—both of which they explicitly reject.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Functionalist philosophers who participate in consciousness science (Dennett, Frankish, Humphrey) while maintaining consciousness is 'just' functional organization
Sketch

Functionalists like Dennett claim consciousness is identical to certain functional states, which means consciousness has whatever causal powers those functional states have—no more, no less. But to empirically test between theories, we need consciousness to leave some distinctive causal trace that its functional correlates alone wouldn't produce. If we find such a trace, it proves consciousness has causal powers beyond the functional, contradicting functionalism. If we don't find one, we have no way to test theories empirically. The key premise is that 'causal difference beyond functional correlates' is the only way to generate differential empirical predictions. This means functionalists who want testable theories of consciousness are committed to denying functionalism.

Appraiser (blind)
The dilemma for functionalists has been extensively discussed; the causal exclusion problem and epiphenomenalism worries are familiar terrain.

U118. The correct theory of consciousness is whichever one best predicts the boundary conditions where consciousness disappears, not the one that best explains paradigm cases of consciousness.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared methodology in consciousness studies that treats theoretical virtues (parsimony, explanatory power for clear cases, scientific naturalism) as primary evidence
Sketch

Philosophers treat consciousness theories as competing explanations of clear cases (wakeful humans, perhaps some animals), to be evaluated by theoretical virtues like parsimony and integration with science. But this gets the epistemology backwards: we already know consciousness exists in paradigm cases regardless of which theory is right. The real discriminatory power lies at the margins—determining whether octopuses are conscious, whether split-brain patients have one or two streams of consciousness, whether locked-in patients without reportability are conscious, whether progressively simplified neural networks remain conscious. A theory earns its keep by getting these boundary conditions right, and we can check its predictions against our evolving intuitive verdicts in edge cases far more reliably than we can adjudicate abstract theoretical considerations.

Appraiser (blind)
Prioritizing boundary conditions over paradigm cases is methodologically sound but not particularly novel; this is how many already approach theory testing.

U119. We can determine which consciousness theory is correct by examining which theory's proponents update their views most rationally when presented with borderline cases they initially got wrong.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The assumption shared across debates that we must evaluate theories synchronically rather than diachronically
Sketch

Instead of trying to identify which theory best fits the data, we should examine the meta-level question: which theoretical framework generates a research program with better error-correction mechanisms? The key premise is that theories are not static but evolve, and we can evaluate their epistemic credentials by tracking how they respond to recalcitrant cases. When Global Workspace Theory faced evidence of unconscious working memory, or when Higher-Order Theories confronted cases of phenomenology without meta-awareness, did the frameworks accommodate these findings through principled modifications or ad hoc patches? This shifts the question from 'which theory is right now?' to 'which theory-tradition has the methodological resources to become right?'—and that question is empirically tractable.

Appraiser (blind)
Meta-level evaluation of research programs is Lakatosian territory; applying it to consciousness is reasonable but the methodological move is established.

U120. The correct theory of consciousness is whichever one makes the fewest predictions about consciousness distribution, because any prediction about whether X is conscious that goes beyond what we can verify is unfalsifiable metaphysics masquerading as science.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The shared assumption that theories of consciousness should be evaluated like standard scientific theories, where broader scope and more predictions count in favor of a theory
Sketch

Consciousness researchers typically prefer theories that make bold, determinate predictions (IIT says this photodiode has non-zero consciousness, GWT says this patient in minimally conscious state lacks consciousness, etc.). But this is a counterexample to the standard scientific virtue of preferring more predictive theories. The key premise: in domains where we have no independent verification method, making more predictions is a vice not a virtue—it simply multiplies your unfalsifiable commitments. A theory that says 'consciousness exists in paradigm cases and I cannot tell you about edge cases' is more epistemically honest than one that confidently pronounces on every edge case. Therefore, consciousness researchers should prefer the most conservative theory—perhaps a restricted HOT or attention schema theory that makes minimal commitments—rather than ambitious frameworks like IIT or Global Workspace Theory.

Appraiser (blind)
The conservatism recommendation is defensible but the argument that predictiveness is a vice in unverifiable domains, while interesting, goes against scientific intuitions without sufficient payoff.

U121. Testing consciousness theories is impossible in principle because any behavioral or neural evidence that could confirm a theory would equally confirm that the theory identifies the functional role that evolution selected for playing the consciousness-broadcasting role in social contexts.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Non-functionalist consciousness researchers (like Chalmers or IIT theorists) who think their theories are empirically testable
Sketch

The argument turns on the premise that consciousness theories generate predictions only via their claims about functional/computational organization. But any functional organization that makes a system genuinely conscious would ALSO make it adaptive for that system to behave as if conscious in social settings (report experiences, act on the basis of them, etc.). This means evolution would select for the same functional organization whether it produces consciousness or merely plays the consciousness-role in the social game. Therefore, holding that we can empirically test consciousness theories commits you to holding that consciousness is whatever plays the consciousness-role—a form of functionalism that non-functionalists explicitly reject.

Appraiser (blind)
The evolutionary argument has been made in various forms; the specific formulation adds little new leverage.

U122. Any empirical test that could distinguish consciousness theories must rely on behavioral reports, but the reliability of such reports is itself theory-laden in a way that makes crucial experiments impossible: theories that predict consciousness without report can always dismiss negative behavioral evidence, while theories that tie consciousness to report cannot be challenged by behavioral data.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Philosophers like Dennett and Frankish who think careful behavioral experiments can be theory-neutral arbiters, and scientists who design experiments assuming reportability is neutral evidence
Sketch

Consider testing whether phenomenal consciousness requires global workspace broadcasting. If subjects don't report experiences in cases of local processing, GWT claims no consciousness occurred, while first-order theories claim consciousness occurred but wasn't reportable. If subjects do report, both theories are compatible with it. The asymmetry: GWT makes behavioral evidence decisive (no report = no consciousness), immunizing it against behavioral counterexamples. First-order theories allow consciousness without report, but then what behavioral evidence could challenge them? They become empirically unfalsifiable by the very methods consciousness science employs. The key premise is that behavioral report is our only window into consciousness in others, but theories differ on whether report is constitutive of or merely evidential for consciousness, and this difference makes the evidential relation itself theory-dependent in a vicious way.

Appraiser (blind)
The theory-ladenness of behavioral evidence is well-established; this application to consciousness adds little conceptual novelty.

U123. Testing consciousness theories requires first-person access to phenomenology, yet this very requirement guarantees that any empirical test will be question-begging against theories that deny the evidential status of first-person reports.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — The shared assumption across IIT, GNW, HOT, and other empirical programs that we can adjudicate theories through prediction-testing against known conscious/unconscious cases
Sketch

The standard methodology assumes we validate consciousness theories against paradigm cases of conscious/unconscious states identified via first-person report. But theories like illusionism or strong representationalism reject that first-person reports reliably track consciousness itself—they track only functionally-defined states that seem conscious. So any empirical test must either: (a) presuppose that first-person reports are veridical about consciousness (begging the question against illusionism), or (b) use only third-person measures (but then we've abandoned the explanandum). The argument turns on showing that the calibration problem—how we know what counts as a paradigm conscious state—cannot be solved neutrally between these theories.

Appraiser (blind)
The calibration problem against illusionism has been discussed; this formulation doesn't add sufficient new structure.

U124. The debate over testing consciousness theories falsely assumes a single target phenomenon, when 'consciousness' refers to at least three empirically dissociable processes (arousal/wakefulness, attention/selection, and phenomenal presence), and theories are actually correctly describing different phenomena.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — The framing of the consciousness theory landscape as competing explanations of a unified explanandum
Sketch

GNW excels at explaining reportability and access; IIT captures something about intrinsic integration present even in unconscious states; HOT theories track a metacognitive capacity that comes apart from both. Rather than competing explanations of one thing, they're adequate explanations of different things we've confused under one label. The argument turns on showing empirical dissociations: preserved integration without access (vegetative states), access without integration (split attention), phenomenal states without either (unattended experiences). The inability to test which theory is 'right' is a category mistake—it's like asking whether theories of vision or memory are 'right' about perception.

Appraiser (blind)
Multiple-phenomena diagnosis is familiar; empirical dissociations are known; doesn't sufficiently advance understanding.

U125. Any empirical test designed to adjudicate between consciousness theories will presuppose the correctness of one of them, because consciousness is only observable through behavioral or neural proxies whose connection to consciousness is precisely what's disputed.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Methodological optimists like those proposing adversarial collaborations (Mudrik, Koch, et al.) and 'consciousness science' as empirically tractable
Sketch

The argument proceeds from a circularity diagnosis: To test whether theory T correctly identifies consciousness with process P, we need independent access to when consciousness occurs. But if we use reports, we presuppose that reportability tracks consciousness (favoring Global Workspace Theory). If we use attention, we presuppose attention suffices (favoring Attention Schema Theory). If we use integration measures, we presuppose IIT. The seemingly stable methodological position—'we should design empirical tests'—collapses because every test design smuggles in the conclusion. This affects even sophisticated proposals like adversarial collaboration or critical experiments, since choosing which dissociations matter (report without phenomenology? phenomenology without report?) already takes sides. The position 'we can empirically adjudicate this debate' is thus unstable: it necessarily assumes what it claims to test.

Appraiser (blind)
General circularity diagnosis that's been made before; this version doesn't add sufficient new structure or examples.

U126. The debate over consciousness theories is actually a debate over whether subjective reports are authoritative about their own causes, disguised as a debate about mechanisms.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — Participants who think this is a straightforwardly empirical debate about which brain processes constitute consciousness (Koch, Dehaene, Lau, etc.)
Sketch

The argument reframes by showing that methodological disagreements do all the work. Higher-Order theorists treat subjective reports as potentially mistaken about what makes them conscious (you might report phenomenology without having it). Global Workspace and first-order theorists treat reports as authoritative (if you report it, it's conscious). These commitments about report-authority determine which dissociations count as evidence: whether unconscious information processing plus HOT yields phenomenology (HOT says yes, first-order says no) just is the question of whether reports can be mistaken about phenomenology's presence. The apparent dispute about mechanisms (workspace vs. hierarchy vs. recurrence) is downstream from this methodological disagreement. Once we see this, we recognize the debate is about epistemic authority, not metaphysics. This explains why evidence never settles it: each side interprets dissociations through their prior about report-reliability.

Appraiser (blind)
Report-authority as fundamental disagreement is insightful but needs development to show it's really doing all the work.

U127. Theories of consciousness become empirically distinguishable only when they make mistakes about the extension of consciousness, so the correct theory must be the one that gets phenomenology right everywhere but is maximally conservative about novel cases.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared methodological assumption that we need some revolutionary new empirical test or that current theories are empirically equivalent pending future neuroscience
Sketch

Most debate assumes we need some 'special test' to distinguish theories. But actually, theories already make empirical predictions - they predict what has consciousness. Higher-order theories say the right-order representations suffice; IIT says the right information integration suffices; GNW says the right broadcasting suffices. The theories only seem empirically equivalent because proponents keep adjusting their theories when faced with counterexamples (refusing to bite bullets about edge cases). The key premise is that our actual evidence IS the full range of what does and doesn't have phenomenology - including our judgments about whether foetuses, dreamless sleep, split-brain patients, etc. are conscious. The theory that's correct is the one that wouldn't need post-hoc adjustment when we get clearer on these cases. This shifts the question from 'what future test could help?' to 'which theory is already accommodating vs. predicting our full phenomenological landscape?'

Appraiser (blind)
The conservative extension-fitting approach has been proposed; this doesn't add enough to the existing methodological discussion.

U128. If a theory of consciousness can't be wrong about p-zombies being metaphysically possible, then it can't be the kind of thing empirical evidence could show to be right.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Both physicalist theories (IIT, GNW, HOT) that claim to be empirical while remaining neutral on metaphysical zombie scenarios, and debates that treat empirical evidence as relevant while maintaining zombie intuitions
Sketch

Most scientific theories can in principle be empirically wrong about modal questions (evolution could have been wrong about whether life could arise from non-life; physics could be wrong about whether perpetual motion is possible). But consciousness theories are typically formulated to be compatible with either position on zombie possibility - physicalists say their theory shows zombies are impossible, dualists say it shows they're possible, but the empirical content is meant to be neutral. The key premise is that empirical confirmability requires modal commitments - a theory must stake out positions on what's possible that could turn out to be false. If your 'theory' is compatible with zombies being possible and with them being impossible, it's not making the kind of claim that evidence can bear on; it's more like a conceptual framework than a scientific hypothesis. This means most consciousness theories, as currently formulated, aren't in the game of being empirically testable at all.

Appraiser (blind)
The connection between modal commitments and empirical testability has been explored; this restates familiar worries about consciousness theories' metaphysical flexibility.

U129. Any empirical test that could distinguish consciousness theories must rely on behavioral or neural patterns that one theory predicts and another doesn't, but every major theory can be trivially modified to predict any such pattern by adjusting what it says about the relationship between consciousness and its signatures, making the theories empirically equivalent in principle.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Empirically-oriented consciousness researchers (Koch, Tononi, Dehaene) who believe their frameworks make distinctive empirical predictions
Sketch

Consider any proposed test—say, showing that certain neural patterns correlate with reports of consciousness. IIT could say 'those patterns indicate high phi'; GWT could say 'those patterns indicate global broadcast'; HOT could say 'those patterns indicate higher-order representation.' Each theory has enough theoretical degrees of freedom to accommodate any correlation we find. The key premise is that consciousness theories necessarily include bridge principles connecting their posited conscious states to measurable outputs, and these bridge principles can always be adjusted without changing the core theory. This means what look like distinct empirical theories are actually empirically equivalent cores plus adjustable auxiliary hypotheses—the debates are therefore primarily conceptual, not empirical, and those who claim we can empirically test our way to the answer are confused about the logical structure of consciousness theories.

Appraiser (blind)
The Duhem-Quine problem applied to consciousness theories; the adjustable bridge principles point is familiar from philosophy of science generally.

U130. Theories of consciousness are empirically testable only if they make predictions about reportability breakdowns, not about consciousness itself.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared assumption among IIT, GWT, HOT theorists that their disagreement is straightforwardly empirical
Sketch

The argument turns on the premise that any empirical test requires a measurable dependent variable, and the only measurable variables we have access to are behavioral/neural markers of reportability. When theories make predictions about 'what it's like' independently of any reportability measure, they've left the domain of empirical testability. The debate about testing theories of consciousness is actually a debate about whether unreportable consciousness exists—if it doesn't, all testable theories are really theories of access/report, not phenomenal consciousness. This reframes the testing problem: we're not trying to find better experiments to adjudicate between theories; we're facing a conceptual issue about whether the target phenomenon is even the kind of thing that could distinguish competing theories.

Appraiser (blind)
The point that we only measure reportability is recognized; reframing as about whether unreportable consciousness exists doesn't add sufficient novelty.

U131. The assumption that consciousness is a natural kind is what makes theory-testing seem tractable, but consciousness is actually a motley class unified only by historical linguistic practice.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The natural kind assumption shared by both eliminativists who deny consciousness exists and realists who affirm it
Sketch

The shared premise among theory-testing optimists is that consciousness is a natural kind—a unified phenomenon carved at nature's joints—such that discovering the right theory is like discovering the nature of water or heat. The counterexample: consider that 'consciousness' groups together phenomenal states as diverse as color qualia, pain, thoughts about mathematics, and the sense of self. These may have nothing scientifically important in common beyond our labeling them 'conscious.' Just as 'jade' turned out to be two different minerals (jadeite and nephrite), consciousness might be multiple unrelated phenomena. If so, there is no single correct theory to test for—IIT might be right about some cases, GWT about others, and HOT about still others. The testing problem is intractable not because consciousness is mysterious, but because we're trying to find a unified explanation for a disunified phenomenon.

Appraiser (blind)
The jade analogy and disunity claim have been made; suggesting consciousness isn't a natural kind is already a live position in the debate.

U132. If consciousness theories cannot currently be empirically distinguished, this shows they are addressing different phenomena rather than competing explanations of the same thing.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The widespread assumption that consciousness theories are competitors in a race that empirical evidence will eventually decide
Sketch

The argument turns on the premise that genuine scientific theories about the same phenomenon must eventually make divergent predictions, even if we currently lack the technology to test them. When putative theories remain empirically indistinguishable in principle (not just in practice), this is diagnostic of them implicitly operationalizing different dependent variables. For instance, IIT's focus on integrated information, HOT's focus on metacognitive access, and GWT's focus on broadcast availability likely track distinct (though correlated) features of mental life. The persistence of the 'hard problem' reflects our treating these as competing when they're actually orthogonal dimensions requiring a multi-factor account.

Appraiser (blind)
The claim that persistent empirical equivalence indicates different phenomena is interesting but the multi-factor conclusion is already suggested by some researchers.

U133. The empirical tractability of consciousness theories depends on consciousness being modular rather than holistic, but consciousness theories themselves generally entail holism.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
counterexample — The shared assumption across neuroscience and philosophy that consciousness theories make testable predictions via contrastive analysis
Sketch

Scientific testing requires isolating variables and identifying minimal sufficient conditions—the modular assumption that you can have consciousness-of-X without the full cognitive system. Yet major theories imply holism: IIT requires system-wide integration, global workspace requires broadcast to multiple subsystems, predictive processing makes consciousness emerge from whole-brain hierarchical prediction. This generates a counterexample to the shared assumption that these theories are empirically testable: testing requires decomposition, but the theories deny decomposability. You cannot test whether 'just' the global workspace suffices because removing other systems changes what the workspace is. The commitment to empirical methods presupposes modularity, while the theories' own logic entails holism.

Appraiser (blind)
Modularity/holism tension is real but theories may have resources to handle it; doesn't definitively show untestability.

U134. Theories of consciousness are empirically distinguishable only if they make different predictions about borderline cases we haven't built yet, not about normal human consciousness.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared assumption across Chalmers, Dennett, and Tononi that existing neuroscience or introspection could adjudicate between theories
Sketch

The debate assumes theories can be tested against paradigm cases (wakeful humans, dreamless sleep, etc.), but all major theories were constructed to accommodate exactly these cases. The argument turns on the premise that theories constructed to fit the same data necessarily make the same predictions about that data. What distinguishes them are predictions about edge cases: partial brain emulations, split-brain patients with one hemisphere anesthetized, systems with structural but not biological similarity to brains, or artificial systems we haven't built. The dispute about 'how to tell which theory is right' has been misconstrued as epistemological when it's actually about which experimental programs to prioritize.

Appraiser (blind)
That theories were constructed to fit paradigm cases and differ on edge cases is recognized; the reframing as about research prioritization is modest.

U135. The position that 'we need better measures of consciousness' is unstable because improving a measure requires already knowing which theory is correct.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — The IIT research program and other measurement-focused approaches that claim to remain neutral between theories while developing metrics
Sketch

Many researchers propose developing better measures (phi, global workspace metrics, etc.) to test theories. But measure-improvement requires validation: checking the measure against ground truth. In consciousness science, what serves as ground truth? Either verbal reports (but theories disagree on when reports track consciousness) or theoretical commitments about what has consciousness. The key premise is that measure validation is inherently circular in consciousness science—you calibrate your measure against cases where you already know the answer, but knowing the answer presupposes a theory. This position collapses into either 'assume a theory to test theories' (incoherent) or 'accept verbal report as definitive' (which most theorists explicitly reject for cases like locked-in syndrome, attention, or animal consciousness).

Appraiser (blind)
Measure-improvement circularity is concerning but may be manageable through iterative refinement; not clearly fatal.

U136. Theories of consciousness make empirically identical predictions because they disagree about what counts as an explanation rather than about what needs explaining.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared methodological assumption that consciousness theories are competing empirical hypotheses about the same explanandum
Sketch

The apparent empirical underdetermination between theories like Global Workspace, Higher-Order Thought, and Integrated Information Theory dissolves when we recognize they're answering different questions disguised as the same question. GWT asks 'what computational architecture enables broadcast?', HOT asks 'what makes a state a conscious state rather than an unconscious one?', and IIT asks 'what physical systems have irreducible causation?'. Each theory specifies different adequacy conditions for explanation—computational sufficiency, conceptual necessity, or mathematical optimality. The disagreement is meta-theoretical: they dispute what philosophical work a theory of consciousness must do. This explains why they agree on much phenomenological data while seeming incompatible.

Appraiser (blind)
The 'different questions' diagnosis is familiar; doesn't sufficiently explain why this matters for methodology or what follows.

U137. The appearance of competing consciousness theories is an artifact of conflating what consciousness is with what consciousness does, and no theory can be wrong about both.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — The assumption that theories like IIT, GWT, and PP are mutually exclusive alternatives
Sketch

Current theories equivocate between constitutive claims (what consciousness is made of) and functional claims (what role consciousness plays). IIT makes primarily constitutive claims about integrated information; GWT makes primarily functional claims about cognitive access; Predictive Processing makes claims about computational role. When theories seem to conflict, they're actually answering different questions: IIT specifies the substrate, GWT specifies the function, PP specifies the algorithm. A theory can be wrong about substrate while right about function (perhaps consciousness requires integrated information but doesn't serve global broadcast) or vice versa. This reclassifies the debate: instead of competing theories, we have partial specifications along different dimensions that could be combined or independently evaluated.

Appraiser (blind)
Constitution/function/algorithm distinction is useful but whether theories are really compatible along these dimensions needs work.

U138. The dispute over whether consciousness has a determinate neural basis is actually a disguised normative dispute about whether we should extend moral consideration only to biological organisms or also to artificial systems.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — Both biological naturalists (Searle, Edelman) and functionalists (Dennett, Chalmers on information theories) who present their views as empirically driven
Sketch

The argument traces how biological theories (like Tononi's integrated information requiring physical causation, or Graziano's attention schema requiring evolutionary context) versus functionalist theories (like global workspace allowing silicon implementation) track deeper commitments about machine consciousness. Proponents choose their empirical positions to support prior ethical intuitions: those who think AI can't be conscious gravitate toward biological constraints; those open to AI consciousness favor substrate-independence. The key premise is that no empirical finding could force someone to extend moral status to machines if they're determined not to—they'll always reinterpret the data through a biological lens. The debate looks like disagreement about neural mechanisms but functions to coordinate moral boundaries.

Appraiser (blind)
Reducing scientific disputes to disguised normative commitments is a familiar debunking move; the specific application here feels forced rather than illuminating.

U139. Commitment to testing consciousness theories through third-person methods commits you to the claim that consciousness could have been absent from a world physically identical to ours, making consciousness epiphenomenal—yet researchers who accept the methods deny epiphenomenalism.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Empirical consciousness researchers who are neither eliminativists nor epiphenomenalists but who claim consciousness is measurable through physical properties
Sketch
