The argument shows that if consciousness is testable through physical measurements alone (brain scans, behavior, information integration), then consciousness supervenes on the physical. But if it supervenes on the physical and is still a separate property requiring theory (not just identical to physical states), then we can conceive of the physical substrate without the consciousness—making consciousness causally idle. The key premise is that you can't have it both ways: either consciousness is nothing over and above the measurable physical facts (eliminativism), or it's something additional that makes no difference to those facts (epiphenomenalism). Researchers like Tononi and Dehaene reject both options but their methodology commits them to one or the other.

Appraiser (blind)
The dilemma between eliminativism and epiphenomenalism for non-reductive physicalism is standard fare in philosophy of mind.

U140. The dispute over consciousness theories is actually a dispute over which explanandum counts as consciousness, and empirical testing only reveals which theory matches which operational definition.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared assumption across the field that empirical neuroscience will eventually vindicate one theory of consciousness over others
Sketch

The argument proceeds by showing that when theorists propose empirical tests (neural correlates, behavioral markers, etc.), they invariably presuppose an answer to 'what counts as consciousness' that already favors their theory. For instance, IIT proponents test for phi because they've already decided consciousness is integrated information; GWT proponents look for global broadcasting because that's what they think consciousness is. The key premise is that there's no theory-neutral way to identify the explanandum: what we're trying to explain (reportability? subjective character? unity?) determines which theory will fit. This means empirical testing can't adjudicate between theories because each theory succeeds on its own terms by defining consciousness to match what it can explain.

Appraiser (blind)
Theory-ladenness of explanandum identification is widely discussed; this formulation doesn't add sufficient novelty to the existing literature.

U141. Testing consciousness theories requires solving the inverse problem in neuroscience, which is provably unsolvable for consciousness specifically because we lack the direct measurement that makes inverse problems tractable in other domains.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — Methodological assumptions underlying adversarial collaboration approaches and neuroscience-based testing protocols
Sketch

In neuroscience, inverse problems involve inferring hidden causes from observed effects (e.g., inferring neural sources from EEG). These are mathematically underdetermined but practically tractable when we can validate inferences through direct measurement of the hidden causes. The key distinction is between inverse problems where the hidden variable is eventually directly measurable (like recording from the inferred neural population) and those where it is not. Consciousness occupies a unique position: we can never directly measure another system's phenomenal experience to validate our inverse inferences from behavioral/neural data to conscious states. This means the inverse problem for consciousness lacks the validation loop that makes such problems scientifically tractable elsewhere. Therefore, any empirical testing program is structurally different from normal scientific inference.

Appraiser (blind)
The inverse problem point has been made in various forms; the uniqueness claim about consciousness lacking validation loops is not sufficiently novel.

U142. The search for distinguishing empirical evidence misconceives consciousness theories as empirical hypotheses when they are actually competing packages of conceptual norms for what should count as a consciousness ascription.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — Both sides of the empirical-vs-armchair divide who assume there's a fact of the matter about 'what consciousness really is' that transcends our taxonomic choices
Sketch

The key premise is that disagreements between theories like illusionism, higher-order theory, and phenomenal realism aren't about discovering which model nature instantiates, but about which concept of consciousness we should use—roughly analogous to competing proposals for what 'game' or 'language' should mean rather than competing theories about a pre-theoretically fixed target. Evidence about brain function underdetermines theory choice because theories differ in their criteria for ascription: one counts only reportable states, another counts richly structured experience, another counts nothing but dispositions to judge. These aren't competing descriptions of the same phenomenon but competing stipulations about what phenomenon we're tracking. The debate looks empirical only because philosophers mistake conceptual legislation for empirical discovery.

Appraiser (blind)
Conceptual-norms-not-empirical-hypotheses is a known position; needs sharper development of what makes consciousness special here.

U143. First-person asymmetry—that only the subject has direct access to their phenomenology—guarantees that any purportedly distinguishing evidence must rely on third-person observable proxies, which means consciousness theories are only testable by eliminating the first-person dimension they were introduced to explain.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — The entire methodology of consciousness science that assumes we can test phenomenal theories through neural/behavioral evidence
Sketch

The argument shows that consciousness theories face a dilemma: either (1) they make claims only about third-person observable patterns (neural activity, reports, behaviors), in which case they're not really theories of phenomenal consciousness but of its correlates, or (2) they make claims about first-person phenomenology itself, in which case they cannot be tested by anyone except the subject, and the subject cannot distinguish 'my theory is correct about my experience' from 'I'm misinterpreting my experience to fit my theory.' Option (1) abandons the explanandum; option (2) makes testing impossible. Any middle path that tries to use third-person evidence to confirm first-person claims requires a bridge principle (e.g., 'reports track phenomenology'), but that bridge principle is itself what the theories disagree about, returning us to circularity.

Appraiser (blind)
The first-person/third-person asymmetry problem is well-trodden territory in consciousness studies; this formulation adds little new.

U144. The dispute over consciousness theories is actually a dispute over which engineering specifications to adopt for artificial systems, not about what consciousness fundamentally is.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared assumption across consciousness science that we're investigating a natural kind with discoverable essence, rather than negotiating boundaries for a normatively-loaded category
Sketch

The argument turns on the observation that every major consciousness theory (IIT, GWT, higher-order theories, etc.) generates different design criteria for artificial systems, and theorists' intuitions about 'real consciousness' track which engineering outcomes they find normatively acceptable. When Tononi insists consciousness requires integrated information, he's specifying what ought to count as a morally considerable system. When Dennett resists qualia-based theories, he's rejecting a specification that would make consciousness detection intractable. The key premise is that consciousness attribution is inherently normative—it determines moral standing—so the 'factual' dispute masks a specification debate about what features we should demand before granting this status.

Appraiser (blind)
The engineering/normative reading of consciousness theories has been explored; framing as 'specification debate' is not sufficiently distinct.

U145. The demand for a decisive test between consciousness theories presupposes that consciousness is a natural kind with sharp boundaries, but if consciousness admits of borderline cases, then theory-choice in consciousness studies is actually concept-negotiation disguised as empirical discovery.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared assumption among consciousness researchers that there is a fact of the matter about which theory is correct that could in principle be empirically settled
Sketch

The argument turns on the premise that our inability to devise decisive tests stems not from methodological limitations but from the metaphysics of consciousness itself. If there are genuinely borderline cases of consciousness (perhaps simple organisms, or marginal brain states), then competing theories aren't making incompatible empirical predictions about a fixed domain—they're proposing different precisifications of a vague phenomenon. The standard methodology assumes we're discovering which precisely-bounded natural kind our term 'consciousness' tracks, but we may actually be stipulating boundaries while mistaking this for discovery. This explains why debates persist despite empirical progress: the theorists are talking past each other because they've carved the conceptual territory differently, not because evidence is hard to obtain.

Appraiser (blind)
The vagueness/borderline cases point has been made; distinguishing concept-negotiation from discovery is not new to consciousness debates.

U146. First-person methodological solipsism is actually committed to third-person verification being impossible in principle, which means researchers who advocate for both subjective report validation and objective theory-testing hold contradictory methodological commitments.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Mainstream consciousness science that treats both subjective reports as authoritative and neural correlates as objective evidence
Sketch

Many consciousness researchers accept both: (a) first-person reports are authoritative for determining whether someone is conscious and what they're conscious of, and (b) we can objectively test theories by finding third-person correlates that track consciousness. But if (a) is true because consciousness is only accessible from the first-person perspective, then any third-person measure can at most correlate with reports, never validate them independently. This commits you to the view that third-person evidence can never confirm a theory of consciousness independently of first-person testimony—it can only confirm that we've found a good predictor of what people will report. The commitment is: if you take first-person authority seriously enough to ground your judgments about what is conscious, you've already conceded that third-person evidence alone cannot establish facts about consciousness, making objective theory-testing impossible.

Appraiser (blind)
The tension between first-person authority and third-person validation is familiar; this formulation doesn't add sufficient new insight.

U147. The methodological dispute about testing consciousness theories is actually a disguised first-order dispute about whether consciousness has a computational or biological essence.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — Philosophers like Doerig, Schurger, and Herzog who think we can develop theory-neutral tests for consciousness theories, and also those who think methodological progress is possible without settling first-order metaphysics
Sketch

Philosophers treat the question 'how could we test consciousness theories?' as methodologically neutral—a question about evidence that doesn't prejudge the nature of consciousness. But the debate covertly smuggles in substantive assumptions. Those who demand behavioral/functional tests tacitly assume consciousness is computational (thus multiply realizable, testable via functional profiles). Those who insist on neural/biological criteria tacitly assume consciousness has an irreducibly biological nature. The methodological impasse is really just the hard problem restated at meta-level. The key premise: what counts as 'the right kind of evidence' depends on whether consciousness is substrate-independent, and we can't answer that without already having a theory of consciousness.

Appraiser (blind)
That methodological disputes reflect first-order theoretical commitments is recognized; the computational/biological framing doesn't add enough.

U148. The search for tests to adjudicate between consciousness theories illegitimately assumes theories make different predictions, when actually they differ only in what they count as 'deep' versus 'shallow' explanations of the same phenomena.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The entire consciousness science research program that treats different theories as empirically competing hypotheses awaiting crucial experiments
Sketch

Consider HOT theory, GWT, IIT, and quantum theories—they appear to make different predictions about which systems are conscious. But on closer inspection, each can accommodate any pattern of conscious/non-conscious attributions by adjusting auxiliary hypotheses. What actually differs is their explanatory ambitions: HOT theorists are satisfied explaining consciousness via representational mechanisms; IIT theorists demand an explanation that derives phenomenal properties from information integration; quantum theorists insist only quantum processes provide 'real' causation. The key premise is that these are not empirical disagreements but disagreements about what stops the explanatory regress—about which level of description is metaphysically fundamental versus merely instrumental.

Appraiser (blind)
The 'explanatory level' diagnosis has been made before; distinguishing empirical from metaphysical disputes is familiar territory.

U149. Any test capable of confirming a consciousness theory must already presuppose the theory's criterion for consciousness, making empirical confirmation logically impossible.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Methodological naturalists like Dennett, Frankish, and Graziano who treat consciousness as a straightforward scientific object, plus neuroscientists who think experiments will settle theoretical disputes
Sketch

To test whether IIT is correct, we'd need to check whether systems with high phi are conscious and systems without it aren't. But determining which systems are actually conscious (the ground truth) requires already knowing what consciousness consists in—which is what IIT claims to tell us. We might use human cases as calibration, but extending beyond cases where we're already confident requires the very theory we're testing. This differs from normal scientific testing because consciousness has no theory-independent detector. The position 'consciousness theories can be empirically tested' is thus unstable: it requires but cannot provide theory-independent access to the phenomenon. This doesn't make consciousness unscientific, but it makes theory choice more like choosing mathematical axioms than testing physical hypotheses.

Appraiser (blind)
The calibration problem is widely recognized; comparing to axiom choice is suggestive but the argument's core is standard.

U150. The persistence of consciousness theory disagreement reveals not that the problem is hard but that theorists are answering different questions while using the same word 'consciousness'.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — The assumption shared across methodological positions that there is a single consciousness question that different theories try to answer, just from different angles
Sketch

When Dennett asks about consciousness, he's asking 'what enables self-reporting and higher-order cognitive control?'; Chalmers asks 'what grounds phenomenal properties?'; Tononi asks 'what determines subjective perspective?'; Block asks 'what determines phenomenal versus access features?'. These are not competing answers to one question but different questions that happen to use the same term. The key premise is that we can reverse-engineer each theorist's implicit question from what they'd accept as a satisfactory answer. This is not just saying theorists use 'consciousness' differently (trivial), but that they're engaged in distinct intellectual projects with different success conditions. The apparent intractability comes from treating these different projects as competing when they're actually non-competing.

Appraiser (blind)
Diagnosing equivocation is common; reverse-engineering questions from answers adds little to existing 'talking past each other' critiques.

U151. No purely third-person test can adjudicate between consciousness theories because any such test must already presuppose a theory of the consciousness-report relation, creating an underdetermination that's not merely epistemic but constitutive.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Methodologists who think neural/behavioral data can neutrally adjudicate between theories without presupposing one
Sketch

Proposals for testing consciousness theories (neural correlates, causal interventions, behavioral measures) all face a dilemma: either we rely on subjects' reports as ground truth, or we don't. If we do, we need a theory of how consciousness relates to reportability—but that's precisely what theories like HOT, GWT, and IIT disagree about. If we don't rely on reports, we need independent access to facts about consciousness—but ex hypothesi we lack that in third-person testing. The 'stable' position of the empirical-testing advocate thus collapses: either into theory-laden circularity (using GWT to test GWT because we trust reports GWT predicts), or into eliminativism (abandoning reports means abandoning the explanandum). The key premise is that the consciousness-report relation is not a neutral bridging assumption but itself a theoretical commitment that divides the theories.

Appraiser (blind)
The report-relation underdetermination is recognized; framing as 'constitutive' rather than epistemic doesn't clearly advance the debate.

U152. The disagreement about how to empirically test consciousness theories rests on a prior disagreement about whether consciousness is explanatorily basic or derived, which means the methodological dispute cannot be resolved before the metaphysical one.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared assumption across Chalmers, Dehaene, Tononi, and methodological reformers like Kanai that we can settle the empirical methodology question independently of metaphysical commitments
Sketch

Current debates about methodology (neural correlates vs. function vs. reportability) appear to be about experimental design, but they actually inherit their structure from incompatible starting assumptions about explanatory direction. Those who think phenomenal properties are basic (like IIT proponents) demand different evidence than those who think functional/computational properties are basic (like higher-order theorists), not because they disagree about experimental rigor, but because they disagree about what grounds what. The key premise: evidential standards are not theory-neutral but depend on which properties you take to be doing the explaining versus which are being explained. This means the methodological impasse is actually a metaphysical one in disguise, and appeals to 'just do better experiments' miss the point entirely.

Appraiser (blind)
That methodological disputes reflect metaphysical ones is familiar; the explanatory priority point doesn't add much novelty.

U153. The standard demand that consciousness theories make 'testable predictions' requires a background theory of the relationship between consciousness and behavior that is itself one of the contested theories of consciousness.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
instability — Methodological naturalists like Dennett and mainstream cognitive neuroscientists who treat 'makes testable predictions' as a neutral constraint on acceptable theories
Sketch

Philosophers and scientists demand that consciousness theories generate testable predictions, but testing requires behavioral or neural measures as proxies for consciousness. Which measures count as valid proxies depends on your theory of how consciousness relates to its effects—that is, on a background theory of consciousness itself. For instance, if you think consciousness requires reportability (a theoretical claim), you'll accept verbal report as a test; if you don't, you won't. The key premise: there is no theory-neutral translation manual from 'subject is conscious of X' to observable predictions. Therefore, the methodological standard of testability is unstable—it appears to be a neutral criterion but actually presupposes answers to the very questions it's meant to help resolve.

Appraiser (blind)
Theory-ladenness of testing is well-known; application to consciousness-behavior proxies is competent but expected.

U154. Theories of consciousness are empirically distinguishable only when they disagree about borderline cases (simple organisms, organoids, future AI), not about paradigm cases (humans, mammals).

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
reframing — The shared assumption that we're discovering facts about what consciousness fundamentally is, rather than making pragmatic decisions about conceptual boundaries
Sketch

The debate appears to be about what consciousness IS, but empirically it can only be about where to draw boundaries. IIT, Global Workspace Theory, Higher-Order Thought theories, etc. all agree that normal human adults are conscious and rocks aren't. The actual empirical content lies entirely in their verdicts about edge cases—whether bees are conscious, whether split-brain patients have two consciousnesses, whether feed-forward networks could be conscious. This reframes the dispute: we're not discovering what consciousness is in paradigm cases (we already agree), but rather negotiating how to extend a concept from clear cases to unclear ones. The key premise is that theories making identical predictions about all possible cases are empirically equivalent regardless of their underlying mechanisms.

Appraiser (blind)
That theories agree on paradigm cases and differ on borderlines is widely recognized; the reframing as boundary negotiation is familiar.

U155. The methodological commitment to physicalism in consciousness science makes all empirical consciousness research question-begging against dualist theories, revealing that 'empirical testability' already presupposes a metaphysical position.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — The widespread framing that treats consciousness science as straightforwardly empirical while treating dualism as unscientific
Sketch

All proposed empirical tests of consciousness theories look at physical systems (brains, computers, organoids) and ask whether they're conscious. But this methodology already assumes that facts about physical structure determine facts about consciousness—which is precisely what dualists deny. A Cartesian or property dualist could accept all the neural data and say 'yes, these physical conditions correlate with consciousness, but they don't constitute it or explain it.' The commitment to finding empirical tests commits us to the view that consciousness facts supervene on physical facts. This means the debate between physicalist consciousness theories can be empirical, but the debate between physicalism and dualism about consciousness cannot be—it's metaphysical all the way down. The key premise is that empirical testability requires that the phenomenon supervenes on observable properties, which begs the question against non-supervenience views.

Appraiser (blind)
The observation that physicalist methodology begs questions against dualism is already widely recognized in the literature and doesn't advance the discussion.

U156. We can only tell which consciousness theory is right by identifying which theory best explains consciousness's biological function, but both sides of the hard problem debate are committed to denying that consciousness has any such function.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — Both physicalists who treat consciousness as a spandrel and dualists/mysterians who deny physical efficacy
Sketch

The argument draws a distinction between causal role (what consciousness does) and biological function (what consciousness is for—what it was selected for). Physicalists typically say consciousness plays causal roles but avoid function-talk because it suggests consciousness is an adaptation rather than inevitable byproduct. Dualists deny consciousness has biological function because that would make it physically efficacious in ways that threaten their position. The key premise is that only functional analysis—asking what problem consciousness solves—can adjudicate between theories, because it connects first-person and third-person evidence. But this path is blocked by commitments both sides share for different reasons.

Appraiser (blind)
The claim that both sides avoid function-talk is questionable empirically, and functional analysis is already widely discussed as a methodology.

U157. Any empirical test that could adjudicate between consciousness theories would presuppose an already-settled answer to what counts as consciousness, making theory-neutral evidence logically impossible.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — The widespread assumption that consciousness science can proceed like other sciences through theory-neutral observation, held by most empirical consciousness researchers including IIT and GWT proponents.
Sketch

Every proposed test (measuring phi, detecting global workspace signatures, identifying higher-order representations) requires specifying which systems to measure. But selecting which systems are candidates for consciousness (humans yes, thermostats no, worms maybe) already assumes an answer to what consciousness is. This isn't merely an epistemic limitation but a logical one: the very act of designing an experiment requires drawing boundaries around the explanandum, but those boundaries are what's theoretically contested. Defenders might claim they're only assuming 'clear cases' everyone agrees on, but agreement on cases without agreement on what makes them cases is exactly the problematic circularity.

Appraiser (blind)
Restates the circularity problem from thesis 0 without adding substantive new insight about theory-neutral evidence.

U158. The demand that consciousness theories be empirically discriminable commits us to eliminativism, because any genuinely empirical test would have to work on systems we're certain lack consciousness, which requires that consciousness itself be eliminable.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
commitment — Philosophers who demand empirical testability while maintaining that consciousness has irreducible first-person features or could be present in unexpected substrates
Sketch

To empirically validate a consciousness theory, we need cases where the theory predicts presence/absence and we can check the prediction. But checking requires independent access to ground truth about consciousness. If we had such access, we wouldn't need theories. The only way to have ground truth is to test on systems where consciousness is guaranteed absent (simple thermostats, rocks). But this only works if consciousness can be guaranteed absent—which requires that facts about physical organization alone can settle consciousness facts. This commits you to strong functionalism or eliminativism: consciousness must be the kind of thing that can be settled by third-person physical facts. The key premise is that empirical testability requires calibration cases, and calibration requires the possibility of verified negatives, which requires consciousness be a purely structural property.

Appraiser (blind)
The argument conflates needing verified negatives with eliminativism; calibration doesn't require what's claimed and the conclusion is too strong.

U159. We can tell which theory is right by testing which one artificial systems designed according to each theory would themselves endorse after philosophical reflection on their own cognitive architecture.

Scores
promise 2/5 · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
Move · Target
distinction — The assumption that consciousness theories are third-person descriptions rather than first-person-accessible architectures
Sketch

Current approaches ask which theory correctly describes systems we already think are conscious. But this draws the wrong distinction—between theories-as-descriptions versus theories-as-designs. Instead, build systems satisfying each theory's criteria (high phi for IIT, global broadcast for GWT, metacognitive loops for HOT), then expose them to philosophy of mind and ask which theory they argue for based on introspection. The correct theory is the one whose implementation produces systems that, upon reflection, endorse that very theory. A GWT-based system that introspects and concludes 'I seem to have higher-order thoughts but no global workspace' falsifies GWT. The key premise is that consciousness theories should be self-intimating: a system genuinely conscious according to theory T should be able to recognize its own architecture as fitting T upon sufficient reflection.

Appraiser (blind)
Creative but the self-intimation criterion is questionable (why should correct theories be self-recognizing?) and the proposal may be circular.
